## A.2 Project / Programme Executive Summary (max 300 words)

Nearly 3 billion people worldwide use solid fuels such as firewood and charcoal, as the primary source of cooking. Burning such fuels releases large amounts of greenhouse gases (GHGs) into the atmosphere, which contributes to global climate change. The potential for greenhouse gas (GHG) emission reductions from the transition to improved cookstoves is significant: (a) it can be achieved at a low cost; (b) it can be scaled up quickly and easily; (c) it reduces the specific GHG emissions significantly; (d) it can be sold at large scale and (e) will be used over years at large extent once they are bought by households.

The project will be implemented as demonstrating an innovative ICS market transformation strategy, starting in two countries, Kenya and Senegal, and by doing so improving global knowledge about ICS sector contribution to Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs). It will significantly increase the number of ICS users amongst rural and most vulnerable populations. The project will benefit 112,350 men people and 1,816 mini mainly rural households, including 6,811 mini women-headed households and 5,971 mini.

The project will significantly limit consumption of non-renewable biomass in the cooking sector compared to the baseline situation. It is estimated that the project will reduce GHG emissions by approximately 204,300 tonnes CO2eq until 2030. It will enable Senegal and Kenya to reach their stated NDC targets for GHG emissions in energy consumption.

The project paradigm shift objective is to accelerate the growth of the ICS sector, in particular in more remote and rural locations with an irreversible market transformation. To do so, it will transform the sector from one which is dominated by small-scale producers and informal traders into a modern, commercialized and competitive sector with sufficient a technological basis and business management capacities, access to commercial capital and ability to deliver high-quality products at affordable prices. The expected main project outputs are: (a) the overall outcome of the project is to triple annual ICS production and sales volumes by the project end (after 5 years) and achieve a 6-fold increase in market share; (b) the project will have created an enabling environment for ICS producers to access ODA-independent growth. To reach this paradigm shift objective, the projects works on the ICS market development strategy, including: (a) increasing the number of ICS producers through providing them with access to market-based finance (supply side activities); and by (b) raising consumer awareness and creating an enabling market environment (demand side activities).

# FINANCING / COST INFORMATION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 3 OF 120

### B.1. Description of Financial Elements of the Project / Programme

#### 1. GCF contribution in the form of grant is being requested to address barriers preventing ICS market growth at the rate required to meet energy cooking sector GHG emission reduction targets in NDC. A grant is the most appropriate financial mechanism for this purpose as it will provide a stable and predictable source of funding that can be used by both financial markets in both Kenya and Senegal: The ICS sector is not mature enough to access non-grant finance on the scale required to meet the ambitious NDC targets. This proposal seeks EUR 18.5 million from GCF (see Table S-2 for detailed description of the ICS sector and financial markets in both countries).

#### 2. The project will deploy GCF grant resources in the form of technical assistance (TA) and in-kind investment support to improve the performance of ICS producers, increase quality, increased production volumes, and geographic coverage. It will also provide TA to boiler consumers' awareness and support a market environment conducive to the adoption of ICS.

#### 3. The project will address prevailing financial barriers to access non-grant finance with targeted technical support and coaching to selected producers that are near bankability, as well as active dialogue with financial institutions to convince them to extend credit to ICS producers.

#### 4. Starting late in the GCF contract period, performance-based, intensive financial coaching, will be offered to ICS producers that are on the cusp of being bankable and have expanded to a level of at least 1,000 ICS per month. This element, along with other project activities, is designed to help ICS producers to engage with financial institutions, including providers and to delay the lending costs and risk associated with further investments needed for producers to further expand their production capacity. The objective will be to improve access to finance, financing, the hand-on to alternative financiers, and the long-term sustainability of the ICS sector in the two countries.

#### 5. The project consists of 2 national-level outputs and 1 global output (excluding Project Management):

- **Output 1: Accelerated market development for climate-friendly cookstoves in Kenya** (GCF finance: EUR 19.81 million; co-financing: EUR 0.37 million)
- **Output 2: Accelerated market development for climate-friendly cookstoves in Senegal** (GCF finance: EUR 17.56 million; co-financing: EUR 0.44 million)
- **Output 3: Sharing and replication of knowledge on climate-friendly cooking solutions and their contribution to NDCs, including the role of GCF in supporting this transition** (GCF finance: EUR 18.5 million; co-financing: EUR 1.61 million)

#### 6. A detailed description of the project design is provided in Section C.3.

#### 7. The total project costs are estimated at EUR 57.17 million, including EUR 38.29 million GCF grant and EUR 18.81 in confirmed co-financing: EUR 12.78 million from BMZ and EUR 6.03 million from a range of project partners. The total cost of the project is shared between Kenya (EUR 45.1 million) and Senegal (EUR 12.07 million). The Ministry of Health – EUR 0.5 million; Senegal: Ministère du Pétrole et des Energies – EUR 0.19 million and Ministère de l'Environnement, du Développement Durable et de la Protection de la Nature (MEDDPN) – EUR 0.28 million from project executing entities (EEs) and other partners. The total ratio of confirmed co-financing to GCF is 0.53.

#### 8. The GCF funds will be applied alongside other sources of financing (Table 1). The project will leverage a considerable contribution – EUR 1.51 million - from the ICS sector. Further, the technical assistance in the form of material and equipment support provided by the GCF grant will be used to reduce the cost of production after the required share of co-financing (20%) is provided in cash by the beneficiaries, thus minimizing the risks for GCF. The ratio between the total project costs and the amount of co-financing from the ICS sector (outputs 1.3 and 2.3) will lead to additional leveraging from the financial sector in the form of loans to the ICS sector amounting EUR 50 million, which is 87% of the total project cost. The ratio between co-financing ratio (co-financing ratio for GCF including parallel sources of finance and confirmed co-financing) is 0.551.

<sup>1</sup> 1 EUR from GCF mobilizes another 0.55 EUR in co-financing and parallel financing, not including the amount of leveraging from the ICS consumers.

# C1. Strategic Context

GHG emissions from energy sector and its relevance for climate change mitigation.

## 13. Traditional biomass represents approximately 15% of total global energy use and 80% of current biomass use and is a major source of GHG emissions, particularly in developing countries. Burning of biomass releases CO2 directly into the atmosphere as well as other greenhouse gases (GHGs) such as black carbon or organic carbon aerosols and methane (CH4) estimated at 1-10 Gt CO2eq/y or 1%-3.2% of global GHG emissions.

## 14. The use of wood-based fuels (firewood and charcoal) is the largest single contributor to the energy sector in Ghana, representing approximately 85% of total biomass consumption. This fuel source provides a major share for domestic needs (over 86% of the rural population). The wood fuel supply is constantly decreasing while the population is growing by 2.7% per year and firewood fuel consumption is increasing at a rate of 3.1% per year.

## 5. Improved Cookstoves are a proven solution to mitigate GHG emissions in Kenya and Senegal.


| Table 1 | Total Biomass Consumption (TBC) in Ghana |
| --- | --- |
| Fuel Type | TBC (Mton/year) |
| Wood fuel | 40,000 |
| Other biomass fuels | 25,000 |
| Total | 65,000 |

## 15. There is evidence that the stove models defined as "ICCS" in Kenya and Senegal are reducing the specific fuel consumption and likewise reducing the specific GHG emissions.

### Definition of ICCS
In line with the approach used by the Energizing Development (ED) project, a stove model is defined as an "Improved Cookstove for Clean Cooking" (ICCS) if it meets the following criteria: 1) the stove has a fuel saving efficiency of at least 40% and above; compared to the fuel consumption of a traditional wood-burning stove; 2) the stove is based on a Controlled Cooking Test (CCT); in case other significant improvements (e.g. air quality, health, etc.) are achieved by the stove model, it can be defined as an ICCS provided that the users prefer cleaner stoves or if the users prepare smaller meals or drinks; 3) the overall efficiency of fuel use will be lower when preparing a typical full meal, yet if the users

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Green Climate Fund (Funding Proposal), Page 9 of 120

The ICS disseminated by the project are domestically produced, low-cost efficient biomass stoves that are well-adapted to local cooking habits and pots; and which are thus appropriate and affordable for the large majority of households in the targeted countries. Knowledge of the habits and cooking needs of the population has been incorporated into the design.

16. There is market evidence that the traditional biomass cooking sectors in the targeted countries are ready for transformation: the demand for more efficient solutions is on the rise driven by constantly increasing scarcity of rural wood fuel, as well as by rising incomes. The results from the baseline studies and subsequent impact studies also show that the ICS supported by the project are in high demand once they have been introduced. In particular, the results from the baseline study conducted for the Kenyan component of the project show that EnDev ICS activities, that after a year, 95% of households who had received an intervention stove (EnDev ICS) still used their stove and 80% of these households reported that they were satisfied with it. After two years, 76% of users still used their ICS. Three and a half years after the trial, only 49% of the households could still use their ICS, but all of them were satisfied with its performance. Similarly, in the baseline study conducted for the Malawian component of the project, 80% of households who had received an EnDev stove (after two years in EnDev Ico monitoring) and households that did not have access to a replacement stove. Similarly, in the baseline study conducted for the Ethiopian component of the project, 95% of households who used their stove were still using it six months after receiving it; only 10% of users had replaced them with other stoves.

Based on the evidence on functionality, market potential and high sustainability of use in households, the Governments of Kenya and Senegal included targets on ICS in their NDCs and other development planning (see below). The Kenyan NDC includes a target to reduce the share of biomass energy used for cooking by 20% with emission reduction targets for the energy sector. Because of the large share of emissions stemming from the use of wood fuel for cooking, this is one of the key areas for reducing emissions in Kenya. The Kenyan NDC sets a target between 2010 and 2030, and interventions for reducing the use of non-renewable wood as fuel for domestic cooking, such as (1) promotion of ICS; (2) promotion of alternative energy sources to replace biomass energy in charcoal production; (3) increased sustainable wood fuel production and (4) Diversification of household fuels.

Further, promotion of climate-friendly cooking solutions has been identified among the key development priorities of the Government of Senegal. The country’s NDC includes a target for reducing the share of biomass energy used for cooking by 20% with emission reduction targets for the energy sector. Because of the large share of emissions stemming from the use of wood fuel for cooking, this is one of the key areas for reducing emissions in Senegal. The Senegalese NDC sets a target between 2010 and 2030, and interventions for reducing the use of non-renewable wood as fuel for domestic cooking, such as (1) promotion of ICS; (2) promotion of alternative energy sources to replace biomass energy in charcoal production; (3) increased sustainable wood fuel production and (4) Diversification of household fuels.

Kenya. Over 80% of Kenyans rely on the traditional use of biomass as the primary source of energy for cooking and heating, with kerosene (12%) and electricity (5%) being used to a lesser extent. The main uses are: wood fuel (43%), kerosene (9.1%) and electricity (0.4%). The use of biomass is predominant in rural areas with about 95% of the households using it for cooking, while only 28% of urban households use biomass as a primary source of energy. Kenya’s forest resources are limited; according to UNFCCC, 92% of biomass consumption in the country is categorized as non-renewable.

In its NDC, the Government of Kenya has committed to the mitigation contribution of 30% reduction (42.8 Mt CO2e) of GHG emissions by 2030 relative to the BAU scenario of 143.9 Mt CO2e. The Kenyan NDC includes ICS as a priority area for contributing to the emission reduction targets under the category of energy demand sector. The specific NDC target for cooking energy is an annual abatement of 2.6 Mt CO2e in 2030 (see Section C.2 for more details).

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Improved cookstoves (ICS) value chain

### Actors in ICS production:
- **Individuals** producing only ceramic linings (potters)
- **Individual artisans** producing final ICS (smelters and/or potters)
- **Cooperative groups** (mostly of women) producing only liners, but sometimes (in Kenya) sub-contracting the metalworking to other cooperatives
- **Large workshops** producing both first ICS, which may produce ceramic linings and/or buy them from other producers, or sell them as raw material to industrial manufacturers

### Actors in ICS distribution:
- **Importers/exporters** (mostly produced ICS only) (Kenya)
- **Wholesalers at regional capitals**, that can manage larger stocks of ICS for redistribution to local dealers and limited direct sales (currently only relevant for the few business class ICS markets existing)
- **Stock holders** (e.g. in Nairobi), who may be retailers or wholesalers, acting as a demand aggregator for their target communities and process bulk ICS orders from producers or wholesaler
- **Local traders**, who sell ICS to end-users directly (in rural areas) or through small shops (of charcoal or wood)
- **Last-Mile Entrepreneurs** (LMEs), who retail and/or install ICS in their neighbourhood/village in rural areas
- **Women's groups, who commonly use their own 'consumer financing' instruments (Sangalel, tontine) to facilitate investment decisions of members by lowering the investment barriers. Sometimes they even market ICS beyond their own group**
- **Community Based Organisations** (CBOs) involved in awareness raising and marketing on the village level.

### The public sector or non-profit organisations support this sector in other value-chain functions which individual producers are unable to fulfil, such as testing, innovation (R&D), quality control and awareness raising that generate demand. These include:
- **National, regional and local government authorities**, which are responsible for necessary policy frameworks and regulations, including the provision of technical assistance and environment campaigns
- **NGOs** that invest in public-good information campaigns
- **Universities or institutes** that can work on testing, innovation, and quality-control

### All categories of producers may employ workers part-time or full-time, on a casual basis or more permanently. The working conditions are generally poor (low pay, no social security), ICS production and employment mainly takes place in the informal sector. (The producers of baseline stoves work in similar structures and conditions, and many ICS producers formerly produced baseline stoves.)

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (GCF) PROJECT FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 11 OF 120

### ICS Producers Categorization

#### 23.
For this proposal, the following categorization of ICS producers has been adopted for both countries depending on the size of the enterprise and its production level. These categories are further used in structuring the project assistance to these producer groups (see Section C.3):
- **Artisanal** (small scale) level production: up to <100 stoves per month.
- **Professional** (medium) level production (3–7 workers): up to <1,000 stoves per month; and
- **Business-oriented** (large scale) producers of 1,000 stoves or more per month.

This last category is currently rare in both markets.

### Sales Channels

#### 24.
The sales are either directly to the end client, or to a range of intermediaries such as wholesalers, traders on the weekly markets, LMEs, projects or NGOs or CBDIs, or women's groups in villages. Some of the intermediaries may also be involved in the production process and sell stoves to their own clients. The sales are made both wholesale as well as private individuals purchase the stoves or the liners at the production centre and transport them to the place of distribution. Wholesale sales can be made through direct sales, by attending exhibitions and roadshows, in small shops, by door-to-door sales, as well as upon request at householder-installation.

## Kenya Baseline ICS Market

#### 25.
Kenya baseline ICS market: Up-to-date sector-wide statistics on the levels of stove production and adoption in Kenya are currently not available. Two sector studies that should address these information deficits are underway and should be published early in 2019. The baseline description of the stove sector in this Proposal is based on current knowledge, including the results from the two sector studies. The first study was conducted by E3W for EnDev in Kenya since 2005, and has supported mainly locally produced wood-burning ICS as described in the Technical Evaluation Report (TER) of the project. The second study was conducted by E3W together with a few development organizations to support locally produced wood-burning ICS companies and some artisanal producers previously supported by EnDev may still produce, but sell stoves independently without reporting to EnDev.

#### 26.
The first study, initiated by the E3W programme of the World Bank in collaboration with SE4All, is a household survey on a nationwide level to monitor and evaluate energy access, including modern cooking energy services. The data will contribute to monitoring the impact of the project. The second sector study in the pipeline is the sector study on clean cooking in Kenya, supported by the members of the Clean Cooking Alliance (CCA) and implemented by E3W. This study aims at providing a more detailed picture of the stove market in Kenya. These two studies will allow for a more precise evaluation of baseline situations in regards to the current number of households using local or manufactured ICSs, and of the different categories of baseline stoves. (Please see discussion of the NDC targets in C.2.)


---
**Page 16**
---

26\. For commercial stoves in Kenya, most production is of the Kenyan Ceramic Jiko (KCJ). This is now counted as a baseline stove, but it has very good efficiency compared to other traditional charcoal stoves used across Africa. It is the product of the first-generation donor-supported ICS, which has become QCJ independent and is the standard charcoal stove in urban Kenya. The Kenyan Ceramic Jiko (KCJ) was developed by the Kenya Ministry of Energy and Natural Resources. There does not have statistics on how many producers are involved in KCJ production. The GCF project proposes to support production of 10,000 units per month. A new version is currently being tested; that should be affordable and robust, and meet the Kenyan national cookstove standard.

The market for industrially manufactured stoves is mainly limited to Nairobi and other urban centers. There is a variety of different types of industrial stoves available in Kenya, but most are imported from India or China. The largest volume sold in 2014 was the RBF Facility programme, managed by SNI for Energizing Development, was introduced in Kenya to pilot the sale of improved cookstoves (ICS) that meet the Kenyan national standard and are affordable to poor households but also offer higher safety and efficiency standards ("Higher Tier"). The stoves in the facility use a variety of fuels, including charcoal, wood, and biogas. The RBF Facility programme has sold over 100,000 units across Kenya since it was first introduced. The market penetration is estimated at no more than 5% in 2015. The sales of the RBF facility seem to be reducing as the Kenyan Ceramic Jiko (KCJ) and other locally produced stoves are growing but they have a very important role in reaching the most vulnerable households, who are not well adapted to local cooking practices, and are often small-scale entrepreneurs that can afford these more expensive stoves.

The classic "Endeavour" ICS intervention in Kenya has reached a market volume of about 240-350 thousand ICS sold annually. This is the largest volume of any ICS sold in Kenya, with approximately 130 producers and 4,000 small-time entrepreneurs (LMEs) supported by the baseline project. The project estimates that these sales satisfy nearly all of the demand for the sustainably produced stoves in Kenya. The "Endeavour" ICS is a high-quality, efficient stove with a large market share; it dominates the ICS market for domestically produced stoves: over 80% of the market share for ICS comes from artisanal producers who produce these stoves.

Senegal baseline ICS market. The ICS market in Senegal is relatively closed. On the one hand, there are hardly any ICS imported for distribution in Senegal. On the other hand, exports of ICS produced in Senegal to neighbouring countries have been increasing over time. In 2014, Senegal exported about 350,000 units of ICS, and in 2015, more than 200,000 ICS. Like in Kenya, the market is currently dominated by artisans; nearly 90% of the ICS producers in Senegal are artisanal producers. The artisanal sector has been growing over time as a result of the increasing number of producers have invested in mechanical machines and employ additional staff. These "professional producers" reach more customers than artisanal producers, but they also face higher costs due to their larger production capacity. In 2015, there were about 30 professional producers in Senegal. The largest producer has demonstrated the full potential of this sector. He sells more than 3,000 ICS per month; two others have reached nearly 1,000 ICS per month and will soon join the new "business class" of ICS producers (See Table 4).

## ICS market demand potential

The ICS market in these countries is developing and growing thanks largely to donor and governmental support, albeit very slowly due to several challenges associated with its recent stage. However, this growth is based on ODA investments and will be difficult to sustain without a strong private sector component that can contribute meaningfully into the growth of their business. Over 80% of the ICS small/micro-scale entrepreneurs, largely informal artisans, are not able to access finance from any source other than informal sources such as family or friends.

The current market is dominated by artisanal producers who are not yet in a position to produce at scale and enter into the larger business sector. This GCF project therefore puts a focus on transitioning artisanal and professional producers to reach a higher level of production and enter the business class sector. The ultimate goal of this project is the creation of a group of business class producers with bankable business plans that can be implemented based on private sector financing.

## ICS market demand potential

The size of the global cooking fuel markets suggests that the potential demand for clean and improved cooking solutions is large. In 2019 alone, consumers in the developing world spent USD 10 billion on all cooking fuels with charcoal being the most popular followed by firewood. The global cooking fuel market is estimated to be worth USD 35 billion per year. However, the world consumer spending on cooking appliances across all clean or improved technologies, which is estimated at less than USD 2 billion annually, represents only a small fraction of this total.

The majority of consumers in developing countries are not able to afford even the most basic and affordable solutions for at least a basic equipped cookstove at price around of USD 5. Although most donors (and partly the consumers as well) see the potential demand for improved cooking solutions, the affordability issue is a major barrier. Most clean solutions are not feasible for the large majority of consumers in developing countries due constraints of cost, technology and access to energy and fuels. In fact, global statistics show that even with rising incomes, biomass cooking does not disappear.

## Kenya

It is estimated that about 37%[1] (which is about 3.7 million households) in Kenya use ICS based on 2015 data. These are mostly urban consumers who have access to electricity and can afford the higher cost of gas stoves as well as firewood stoves, mainly used in rural areas where fuelwood is still available, and cooking power is low. The remaining 63%[1] (which is about 6.3 million households) use traditional biomass cookstoves. A significant proportion of the households (over 50%, i.e., 5.3 million households) still use traditional biomass cookstoves, including open fires[2]. With current demographic trends (2.7% annual increase), by 2030 Kenya’s population is projected to reach about 48 million people. The number of households will also increase at a rate of 5%, only about 50% of the potential consumers will be reached by 2030 leaving around 7 million households still relying on the use of traditional inefficient stoves.

**Page 18**

33\. In Kenya, wood and charcoal fuel will remain predominant well into the future, and is without alternative for most households, because there is neither sufficient supply of electricity or LPG nor the available infrastructure to work towards a market transformation that would include rural areas. According to UNDP/WFP's 2008\19 national survey, wood is used by 75% of Kenyan households and charcoal by 64%. The use of biomass for cooking in Kenya is also high, with resources to eliminate wood and charcoal for cooking in cities, although the Government of Kenya has begun subsidizing LPG. In rural areas, however, where most Kenyans live, there are no such subsidies. According to a survey conducted by the Ministry of Energy, approximately one quarter of all households do their "primary" cooking with charcoal, LPG and kerosene stoves. (Yet, due to the high cost of these fuels, many Kenyan households use wood for primary cooking.) The situation in Kenya is different to the rest of the country, with 44% and 47% of the population reporting that they cook primarily with LPG and charcoal respectively. This is because the majority of Kenyans are constrained to the outsize of Nairobi and it is more expensive than charcoal and kerosene even with subsidization.

34\. Senegal's ICS sector is now at the stage that allows 13% of the households to have access to two ICS (most households use only one ICS). The number of households using LPG has increased from 20,000 in 2008 to over 50,000 today. This means that at least 97% of the market is still served by traditional cookstoves. The total market potential is at the level of 1 million ICS per year. The number of households using LPG has increased from 32,000 in 2008 to over 65,000 today. This is going to increase in view of positive demographic dynamics up to nearly 221,000 people in 2050, which will require about additional one million ICS to meet their cooking needs. Six regions (north on the east and west) of Senegal have access to LPG. The market is still dominated by traditional cookstoves with a very low penetration rate. Marketing interventions are also more difficult to reach, as population density and purchasing power of clients are low in rural areas. In 2013, the number of households using LPG was only about 5% of total households. Senegal has a very high potential for ICS, but it is still at an early stage. The penetration rate of LPG to cooking at the beginning of this century. Still to date around 50% of the households have LPG equipment in their homes. This means that there are no more than 1 million households using LPG. Senegal has a very low level of access to LPG and is now a "breakfast fuel" in Senegal, and there is no way to change this unless a donor is prepared to invest in marketing interventions. The penetration rate for ICS is still very low (less than 10%). There are no other uses for electricity and the prospects for expanding the production base are currently limited.

35\. Yet, even in areas where the demand for ICS is already established, the producers are not able to respond to the increasing demand. The reason lies with the lack of investment in the sector. The Senegalese Ministry of Development works to work with existing producers of ICS stemming from previous store projects. Annual sales were at 40,000 ICS units and the production capacity was only about 15,000 ICS per year. This is a very low level in comparison with eight regions of the country. Most enterprises were just producing for their direct neighbourhood based on direct sales to households or small retailers. The Senegalese Ministry of Development has been supporting these producers. However, producers failed to respond to market demands. While investment would allow higher volumes of sales, production capacity is currently limited by the lack of investment in the sector and the very low level of income nature of their business, and the low turnover of artisanal production (on 1,000 EUR per month), they could not access formal sector financing. UNICEF decided to support to investors into larger production units to overcome this investment barrier and boost production. The average growth rate of the annual sales 2006-17 reached 17%.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 15 OF 120

A group of 25 professional producers emerged with average monthly sales of approximately 110, however, even with their average prices of EUR 1,500-2,000 they are not interesting as clients for the Financial sector.

### High and rising fuel prices is a major demand driver for ICS

According to the World Bank[3], between 2000 and 2010, LPG prices have risen 8% annually in normal terms for key Africa LPG markets; kerosene prices have grown by approximately 5% per year. In contrast, the price of coal has remained stable at around EUR 40-60 per tonne.

Because of increasing demand and growing biomass scarcity, charcoal prices have grown even faster – more than triple in some countries over this period[4]. This is reflected in the fact that many households are now regularly paying for cooking fuels can afford the upfront cost of a basic ICS.

### Existing studies and consultations held during project development show that most consumers are able to pay for the technology

The results from a recent survey conducted by GIZ and EnDev in Kenya, together with a consumer survey in a number of Sub-Saharan African countries, indicate that ability to pay is not a major issue for basic ICS supported by the project. Except for the very poor (less than 10% of households), most consumers are able to afford the upfront cost of an ICS. The average monthly level of less than USD 12-15 per day, are also at paying USD 3–7 for basic ICS, once they have access to repayment schemes.

The conclusions imply a potential market demand for ICS from around 50% of all households[5], most of which would be able to pay the upfront cost. However, this is not necessarily an indication that all these people will benefit of the ICS and are not familiar with the technology will not be willing to invest until they have been convinced that the additional costs are worthwhile: it’s both in terms of potential cost benefits and suitability of the products, as well as awareness.

### One aspect, which may negatively affect consumers’ ability to pay, is their liquidity constraints. Yet, evidence in the literature on both ICS and analogous energy saving devices[6], such as solar lanterns, suggests that a target of a technical assistance loan of 20% of the total cost of an ICS would be sufficient for most households to afford it even though the range of acceptable payment for most consumers would likely be between one and six months. Experience from Kenya shows this is also true.

### Financial analysis of the fuel-saving costs for a range of ICS products in both Kenya and Senegal clearly indicates that simple pay-back for consumers for such investments is within acceptable ranges of 2 to 5 months even with high interest rates (10-30% per year). For example, in Kenya, the average monthly cost of using an Sengalhouse stove on spends EUR 6 per month on fuel[7]. A typical stove for this category of consumers is the Jambiar Jabbar firewood. The pay-back period for this stove has been estimated at around three months and it’s proven that the Jambiar Jabbar firewood reduced the specific fuel consumption by 40%. For an average household, even if they are not able to pay upfront cost, they can afford to pay back within a few months (3-5) as the pay-back is recovered in 3 months. A frequent solution to the liquidity constraint is for women to organize group borrowing in the form of microfinance schemes. The proportion of households that have access to such schemes and are able to cost-efficient cooking solutions, is small. Complementary approaches are implemented by NGOs or other projects, which may reach the customers with no ability to pay.

### DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 16 OF 120

40. Overall, the continuation of current trends over the next decades promises to offer many opportunities for transformative advances in the adoption of ICS. On the demand side, key factors include escalating prices for cooking fuel and steadily growing household incomes. A large share of consumers already pay for their cooking fuels and can benefit from improved access to ICS. However, there are also significant barriers to market growth, including a lack of consumer ability to pay for basic ICS in both Kenya and Senegal.

41. Potential Market Demand Summary: In Kenya, there are 5.3 mln unserved households that would be able to pay for an ICS. This represents a latent demand potential based on the number of households with access to electricity. Of these, about 70-90%, or 4.7 mln – 6 mln households are unserved households that would be able to pay for an ICS. This represents a latent demand potential based on the number of households with access to electricity. The numbers of potential "customers" will increase in view of generally positive population dynamics. However, major obstacles remain in terms of affordability and willingness to pay. Limited awareness about benefits of ICS and low willingness to pay higher prices for new products is one of the greatest long-term obstacles to market growth on the demand side (please refer to Section C.2 for a detailed discussion about barriers to ICS market growth).

# C2. Project / Programme Objective against Baseline

The projected doubling of ICCS adoption rates at Kenya and Senegal would have a significant portion of Kenya's and Senegal's population without access to even minimally improved cooking solutions and would still rely on traditional biomass for their cooking needs. This is because the current market conditions suggest there will be no irreversible shift in the market to ODA-independent growth leading to the majority of cooking being done on ICCS in 2030. The lack of sufficient demand, limited access to finance, low purchasing power and insufficient incomes and infrastructure to access cleaner cooking solutions for the vast majority of consumers in these countries.

### Kenya

1. **Kenya's Total GHG Emissions**: In 2010 were 69.8 M tonnes according to the latest official national data from the GHI inventory.
2. **Forestry and Energy Sectors**: These are the largest contributors to national GHG emissions.
3. **Energy Demand Sector**: Wood fuels account for the largest share of consumption (~70% of wood, non-electricity, non-cooking). The energy demand sector is projected to grow by 15% in 2030 compared with 2010 levels. Emissions from energy demand will grow in the baseline scenario from 7 M tonnes CO2e in 2010 up to 10 M tonnes CO2e in 2030.
4. **NDC's Ambitious Target**: Kenya established an ambitious target for energy demand sector, i.e., a 6.9 M tonnes CO2e GHG emission reduction by 2030 compared with the baseline scenario (see Figure 1). This is based on the estimated technical potential for GHG emission reduction in the energy demand sector.
5. **Low-Carbon Solutions**: Among the eight low-carbon solutions, the most promising are: improved cookstoves and fuels (ICCS), which have a total potential of ~5.6 M tonnes CO2e a year in 2030. In line with the adopted approach to establishing the NDC target, at least 50% of that potential is required for Kenya's GHG emission reduction a year should be realised in the cooking sector to enable Kenya to meet its NDC targets.
6. **Baseline Scenario**: This assumes historic ICCS adoption growth rates (13%), annual GHG emission reductions will come from the energy demand sector, and the use of the GCF. Whereas, the GCF contribution is assumed at an annual GHG emission reduction of 3.85 M tonnes CO2e by 2030 (see Figure 2). Proposed target for GHG emission reduction in the energy demand sector is 6.9 M tonnes CO2e a year in 2030, which means that the GHG emission reduction from increased ICCS adoption can be counterbalanced by GHG emissions increase from traditional stove use (on absolute terms) due to continued and high population growth rate in Kenya.

*This growth assumption for the baseline is used in the impact model. However, the actual baseline growth for most local ICCS communities is likely to be lower than this.*

### Senegal

50. In Senegal, GHG emissions from the energy sector amounted to 11 Mt CO2eq in 2005* or about 40% of the national GHG emissions. Wood-based fuels (firewood and charcoal, including charcoal production) are the largest single contributor to the GHG emission in the energy sector, accounting for 66% of sectoral emission or 7 Mt CO2eq. Of these, firewood accounts for about 50% of total wood-based fuel consumption. The use of wood-based fuels in the sub-sector of domestic cooking has been emitting almost one-fifth (17%) of all annual GHG emissions of the country. Furthermore, the use of wood-based fuels is projected to increase further up to 20 Mt CO2eq in 2030 largely as a result of anticipated population growth and continued reliance on wood fuel (Table 6).

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Supply-side barriers

### Barrier 1: Weak skills and capacity to improve production processes and product design to suit customer needs.

There is huge potential for efficiency gains and improving productivity in existing ICS manufacturing processes, but the companies lack basic assets, technological knowledge and access to knowledge or technology. The artisanal sector has a strong tradition of innovation and adaptation, but it has not been adapted to the cooking needs and household size of the clients in Senegal. However, the designer at the time focused on the design of the stove for the urban market, which is different from the requirements of efficient production concepts. Artisanal and professional players, in particular, contend with low margins, rising costs of materials and components, and a lack of access to finance. The artisanal sector also faces challenges related to quality control issues, which include the high cost and logistical challenges of accessing stove-testing services, and the limited capacity for testing and certification.

### Barrier 2: Importing manufactured stoves is not an adequate answer to this barrier.

International companies developed stove designs with industrial production concepts and products that promise better performance based on lab tests. However, these stoves are often too large or heavy for rural households in terms of size and penetration. National ICS markets are challenging as customer needs differ a lot from common other markets. For example, the Jambiar stove is a very small stove with a capacity of 10 kg of rice per day. The imported stoves tend to be over-dimensioned. International producers, on the other hand, optimize off the shelf-sizes for lower power cooking needs and thus are not suitable for the high-power cooking needs in rural areas. The most effective way to achieve a fast uptake of ICS use countrywide is the scaling of the existing, well-known and good performing stoves that are adapted to local cooking needs and household sizes. This can be achieved by using parts and stove of the Jambiar stove which may leverage additional efficiency gains. The alternative - the introduction of completely new (higher) stove designs would be rather challenging; (a) it would require very high pilot phase to test a product that has never been used before, (b) it would require very high price point to reach a market segment with low purchasing power (current ICS are at a range of EUR 10-15), and (c) it convinces a population that is fond of the Jambiar stove and is not willing to change. The cost per household for the introduction of new stoves in the framework of the GCF project and the climate effect would be much lower than the proposed approach even if savings per stove outweighs the costs.

### Barrier 1.2: Under-developed ICS supply chains

Even when consumers are aware of an appropriate cooking solution, it is often unavailable or difficult to access due to under-developed ICS supply chain. Although access to basic services and infrastructure has improved in rural areas over recent years, there remains a significant gap in the urban areas in Senegal. More than 90% of the ICS sold countrywide in 2016 were charcoal stoves that merely serve urban households. The majority of the rural population still uses traditional cooking methods such as firewood and kerosene. Small-scale, often family-based and not formally registered artisanal enterprises are the typical case in a tin-swinging on a sheet metal under a tree. These small stove producers have limited capacity to scale up production and are often reluctant or slow with developing with the supply chain. Their levels of sales and revenues are just sufficient to cover basic running costs, but not enough for them to invest in quality control systems, marketing strategies, or the development of ICS to end users. Most of these small producers sell their ICS only at their workshop or have a very limited distribution outreach (hardline distance).

### 58. Barriers to Limited access to finance (informality, high interest rates, collateral requirements, etc.)

In terms of access to finance, the private sector in Senegal and Kenya is dominated by microfinance institutions (MFIs). This is not due to a lack of potential financial offering, but rather the current available financial products are too expensive for Kenyans and are not adapted to the needs of informal, artisanal/enterprise sectors such as those of the ICT sector.

#### Senegal: Senegal's finance sector is segmented into a range of different institutions that are not yet fully integrated. The commercial banking sector in Senegal is dominated by foreign banks, which have been key for the development of the private sector. The informal character of a significant part of the economy and the absence of an independent regulatory framework have led to a situation where it is difficult to lend to private enterprises.

The Senegalese market has a range of financing options for different sized businesses. Traditional commercial banks work with registered companies and established entities and offer average loans of 50% (100% of assets) at interest rates between 20-30%. Microfinance institutions (MFIs) are active in the sector, offering credit to individuals and small enterprises at a fixed rate of 100% (loan of assets). Commercial banks tend not to service the informal sectors of the economy. One exception is the microfinance bank BOKO which has been established by the government and supervised by the national FONGIP programme in terms of micro-finance. Senegal has a dynamic micro-finance sector.

The larger commercial banks have traditionally been reluctant to lend to small and medium-sized enterprises, and tend to focus on corporate banking, although the annual interest rates range from approximately 20% to 22%.

Despite the dynamics of the sector, MFIs turn down approximately 80% of loan applications from small and medium-sized enterprises. The commercial banks are seen as strict for non-commercial banks, although the annual interest rates range from approximately 20% to 22%.

#### Kenya: Kenya's financial sector is the most developed in East Africa. The banking sector is diverse with 28 domestic banks and 13 foreign banks operating in the country. There are also a number of savings and credit cooperative associations. In practice, the commercial banking sector is concentrated and dominated by four major banks. These banks have been able to dominate the market because they have access to large-scale sub-Saharan countries. Recent interest rate caps introduced controls by the Central Bank in 2016 to cap lending rates have shown some success in reducing interest rates from approximately 35% per annum to around 24% per annum and to provide increased access to credit, but the effect has been for commercial banks to freeze or sharply increase loan terms. The Central Bank of Kenya conducted a survey in February/March 2016/2017 by conducting the Central Bank of Kenya notes that respondents from medium and small firms find it difficult or very difficult to obtain bank loans, especially after the introduction of interest rate caps. This is due to limited bank liquidity, which has been exacerbated by the recent increase in interest rates and the shortening of loan repayment periods. Overall, the effect is to limit credit growth in the private sector, particularly to SMEs and individuals. The Central Bank of Kenya notes that respondents from medium and small firms find it difficult or very difficult to obtain bank loans, especially after the introduction of interest rate caps.

Interest rates and stringent collateral conditions are a major barrier for accessing finance for commercial banks is currently relatively regulated. Both interest rates caps and stringent collateral conditions have been introduced by the Central Bank of Kenya (Mombasa) and SABC, collateral conditions may be less stringent but interest rates remain over 20%.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Green Climate Fund (GCF) Page 21 of 120

### Demand-side barriers

#### Barrier 1: Low willingness to adopt new stoves
The main barrier on the demand side is traditionally the low willingness of consumers to adopt a new stove, stemming from a number of factors such as the appropriateness of stove designs and fuels for local cooking practices. This is compounded by lack of awareness about the benefits of the technology, as well as consumer ability to access the stove in the base line projects.

#### Barrier 2: Lack of confidence in new products/vendors
Even though consumers are increasingly aware of the numerous benefits of ICS, the level of such awareness and trust in new products varies widely. This is particularly true for rural areas where education levels and access to mass media are lower than urban areas. In these areas, the project needs local partners and local marketing campaigns to build consumer confidence.

#### Barrier 3: Low awareness of risks associated with traditional cooking practices
Low awareness of the risks associated with traditional cooking practices and the multiple benefits of ICS is another barrier on the demand side. This lack of awareness and ignorance about ICS is particularly pronounced in rural areas, where education levels and access to mass media are lower than urban areas.

#### Barrier 4: Lack of confidence in stove suppliers
In addition to low awareness of risks associated with traditional cooking practices, there is a significant level of consumer distrust towards stove suppliers. This is due to the lack of trust in stove sellers, who may not be able to provide reliable and affordable ICS promoted by the project through word-of-mouth or targeted marketing.

#### Barrier 5: Lack of confidence in stove vendors
The combination of geographic isolation, consumers have been convinced of the benefits of an ICS before they will invest.


---
**Page 28**
---

The most important reasons for poor consumers to invest in ICS are often not the obvious reasons that concern fuel and firewood cost-saving, but reasons of convenience, safety, time saving, as well as reduced smoke exposure. Consumers must be introduced to all the stove benefits before they will invest in the first ICS and revisit in a replenishment cycle.

64. Barrier 2.3: Non-favourable market environments. Apart from demand-side barriers, there are several policy, institutional and co-ordinational challenges that need to be addressed to create a favourable market environment for ICS. These include lack of enabling policies and regulations, weak institutions, poor coordination between the electrification and non-rural providing sustainable energy access to modern cooking. For the creation of a conducive market environment, it is necessary to engage all relevant stakeholders in the sector and increase the enabling conditions for growth, which are for example increased support for the formulation of national ICS policies and regulations, improved institutional capacity building for small businesses. Strengthened capacities in the public sector to revise relevant policies promoting ICS use and to mentor sector development are also necessary.

### C3 | Project / Programme Description

**C3.1: The project seeks to address the emissions from the unsustainable consumption of wood-based fuels in the household cooking sector, which is the largest single contributor to the GHG emissions in the energy sector in both Kenya and Senegal.**

The project will seek to address this problem by shifting the paradigm shift objective is to accelerate the growth of the ICS sector with an irreversible structural transformation that will significantly increase the level of ICS production and sales, in particular to remote more rural and local zones, thereby enabling the two project countries, Kenya and Senegal, to significantly reduce their GHG emissions.

To enable such paradigm shift, two main barriers will have to be addressed. On the supply side, the ICS sector is not capable of securing the investment required to scale up ICS sales, whereas the demand for ICS remains concentrated in urban areas where it is more expensive and less accessible than firewood. To address this barrier, as illustrated in the Theory of Change (TOC) diagram (Figure 5), the project will adopt a two-pronged approach.

### 68. First, on ICS supply side, it will support a transformation of the ICS sector from one which is characterized by ODA-dependent artisanal, under-capitalized and informal SMEs into a much stronger formal economic sector with a higher level of investment in production infrastructure and technology. This will enable the sector to secure investment needed to accelerate growth and sustain high levels of ICS sales. As a result, the transformed ICS sector will be able to provide more reliable and affordable energy services for households living in remote (rural) areas and provide services to vulnerable population groups. Second, on market demand side, the project will support the development of markets for ICS products by increasing awareness among consumers, particularly in rural locations not yet reached by the project. (Please see the discussion of talent-demand and ability to pay in C1-ICS Market Demand Potential)

### 69. Over the last decade in both Kenya and Senegal GIZ supported the ICS sector to move from Pre-Commercial Innovation (PCI) stage to commercialization assistance (training and advisory services) to the small group of domestic ICS producers that exist in both countries and are described in the 2017 baseline situation. Most producers are still at the PCI stage, but some have reached the stage where they are ready to grow, albeit at very low rate. For example, GIZ’s prior activities in Kenya resulted in the establishment of a small group of ICS producers that now produce and sell approximately 350-400,000 ICSs/year; GIZ has successfully tested some of proposed new approaches, e.g. the use of confidential business information (CBI) as an incentive for producers for scaling-up, or established a pool of credible ICS producers with good performance track record.

### 70. The project will enable current artisanal stove producers to reach the professional level of operations and professionalization of their production status in order to be able to scale up their production capacity. When the majority of companies in the ICS sector moves from artisanal to professional and business category and reach the level of sales, which they can sustain on their own, it means that the market enters the expansion stage.

### Figure 28 of 120

According to EBRD's Market Development Scorecard (Figure 6) for modern energy products and services, it is only at the stage of development that participants start generating revenues on the level that is sufficient for them to raise capital and sustain the high production volumes and growth trajectory without the need to rely on grant-based funding. The ICS project will support Kenya in moving through this critical phase by enabling Kenya from pioneering through expansion phases so that both reach their maturity phase by the GCF project end, thereby enabling markets to continue grow and expand on a self-sustaining basis to reach NDC-related NDC goals by 2030.

| Development Phase | Description | Promising Phase I | Expansion Phase | Maturity Phase II | Maturity Phase III | Decline Phase |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Development Phase** | In the context of a new product or service, the market is not yet established and has not served the customer base. The product is not yet reaching its full potential. At only small scale of production, the product does not yet have a significant impact on the country's economy (high marketing costs, low profit). | **Promising Phase I** | As the product gains acceptance in the market, it starts to reach a larger customer base and has started serving the customer base. The product is now reaching its full potential. At only small scale of production, the product does not yet have a significant impact on the country's economy (high marketing costs, low profit). | **Expansion Phase** | As the product gains acceptance in the market, it starts to reach a larger customer base and has started serving the customer base. The product is now reaching its full potential. However, increased competition for market share may lead to price drops and reduced profitability. | **Maturity Phase II** | This is usually the longest phase of the life cycle as the product gains acceptance in the market and product prices drop due to increased competition for market share. As a result, profitability decreases significantly. The number of suppliers increases and existing ones increase their production capacity. |
| **Decline Phase** | The product has lost its appeal to customers and is no longer growing at a significant rate. The product's service life is coming to an end. Eventually the product will be replaced by a new product. | **Maturity Phase III** | The product has lost its appeal to customers and is no longer growing at a significant rate. The product's service life is coming to an end. Eventually the product will be replaced by a new product. |

#### Generic strategy for both Country Outputs 1 and 2:

72. Each of the two country outputs will follow a similar strategy to achieve market transformation of the cooking sector with climate-friendly cook stoves in Kenya, based on the situation and needs of ICS sector in Kenya and Senegal respectively. The strategy focuses on three core elements in order to address the identified barriers to market transformation.

- **The first core element (Sub-component 1):** is to strengthen the ICS supply and delivery chain to accelerate uptake of climate-friendly cook stoves by households, businesses, and institutions in both countries on a large-scale basis. The project will do so by providing tailored TA and in-kind investment support to improve professionalization of ICS production, distribution and retail chains, as well as their expansion to unsevered areas.

- **The second core element (Sub-component 2):** will provide TA to strengthen demand and enable market orientation of the ICS sector. This will include building awareness among consumers about climate-friendly cook stoves. It will also finalize and streamline the institutional and policy frameworks governing clean cooking sector, including development of national action plans and setting up of performance indicators and targets. In addition, the project will assist countries to develop and strengthen quality assurance frameworks for ICS production, distribution and retail chains as well as their adoption in the context of climate-friendly cookstove/feast performance in local conditions.

- **The third core element (Sub-component 3):** will undertake regular ICS market monitoring and project impact evaluation in order to validate and adjust, as necessary: the market transformation strategy and the scope and modality of GCF support.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Sub-component I: Strengthening ICS supply and delivery chain

### Improvement of the professionalization level of straw producers

#### The project will implement a performance-based support scheme to enable artisanal and professional producers to upgrade their production processes, including:

- **Artisanal producers**: Will be provided with a tailored "professionalisation kit" (e.g., roller, jerry, cutting machine), electric machines (e.g., welding machine, compressor for spray painting), suitable transport equipment (e.g., bicycle, motorbike, tricycle/motorbike transporters). This standardized kit is designed to enable the growth of an artisanal ICS producer from a small-scale producer to a business class producer.
- **Professional producers**: Will be provided with support kits for professional ICS producers that should grow to the business class level. The support kit will include items such as — among others — hand tools, manual machines, electric machines (e.g., drilling machines, electric jerry-cutting machine), suitable transport equipment (e.g., bicycle, motorbike, tricycle/motorbike transporters). This is designed to enable the growth of a professional ICS producer to a business level from an average sale volume of 10 tons per year to at least 25 tons per year. The support kit will be provided for each producer with preparation of a business plan for the expansion. Tailor-made "kits" will be based on the details of the business plan.

#### The kits will be provided in phases with an agreed-upon business plan and result-based criteria and milestones:

- **Artisanal ICS producers to enter the scheme**: Will be established based on past performance, i.e., minimum ICS sales records during the last two consecutive years: at least 25 ICS/ton for artisanal producers and at least 10 tons per year for professional producers.
- **Professional ICS producers**: Will be established based on their description of each component by country below (for more details).
- **Producers to enter the scheme**: Will be established based on their description of each component by country below (for more details). In case of business-class producers, linked to business plans (See Figures 10 and 11 for more details).

#### The kits will be provided in tranches with a total value of EUR 25,000.00 per producer in Kenya (offer a kit with 3 tranches and a total value of around EUR 23,000.00). In Senegal, tailor-made kits (also offered in tranches) will range from EUR 18,000 to EUR 25,000 per producer. The exact criteria for the configuration of the kit(s) will be elaborated by the business consultants during the project implementation.

#### The grant recipient will be responsible for the purchase and delivery of the kits to the producers. Producers who have serious business development motivation can benefit from the scheme. The exact criteria for the determination of the share of own contribution will be elaborated in an operational manual (see below). The ICS producer will be required to provide a business plan for expansion by 12/07/23; they will not sell the assets of the kit without prior consent of the GCF project (or the Chamber of Crafts).

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Example: Provision of performance-based incentives to producers in Senegal

### A Short Summary Explanation - Performance-based incentives, taking Senegal as an example

Artisanal (family business) producers in Senegal all start from a similar baseline of assets. They have one or two sets of hand tools and no mechanical or electric machines for production. They operate at their homes rather than in workshops. The products are sold to the end client at the workshop, either on credit or cash demand (and payment in advance) and sell at their product only to the end client at their workshop.

The investments needs to lift them to professional level as well rather uniform (e.g. a workshop structure, better hand tools, electric machines, etc.). The ICS producers are provided with a kit of equipment (called "professionalization kit") has been designed based on the experiences of EnDev Senegal since 2013. This is called "kit" because it contains all necessary equipment for production.

Once the professionalization kit has resulted in the expected increase of the average monthly sales from 25 to 60 baskets per month, the producer will be eligible to receive a performance-based incentive (PBI) payment. The proposal for material investment needed for producing up to 120 ICS per month, with a maximum of EUR 8,000.

The current 25 professional producers, on the other hand, have more diverse baseline assets. Some of them now own electric machines and some have rented workshops. The key challenge is that the current model of Providing a standard "professionalization kit" would lead to inefficiencies. Hence, the producers will be assisted by the local ICS team in Senegal to design their own professionalization kits.

The kits are ranging between EUR 17,000 and EUR 24,000 depending on the kind and number of manual and mechanical equipment included in each kit.

## Operational Manual

An Operational Manual will be developed for each country at project commencement to guide the implementation of performance-based support scheme. It will cover in detail the following aspects of the scheme:

- **Eligibility criteria**
  - Key performance indicators for each category of ICS producers, i.e., a minimum percent increase in sales volumes and/or minimum level of ICS sales (in absolute numbers)
  - Investment requirements, flow of funds and contractual/legislative arrangements between beneficiaries and responsible parties
  - Financial contributions, disbursement procedures

- **Criteria for the definition of the share of own contribution of the ICS producers**
  - Step-by-step approach and process flow-diagram explaining the key steps in this process: a) application of ICS; b) assessment of baseline assets; c) design of professionalization kit; d) purchase of professionalization kits and milestones; e) estimation of the GCP-covered costs of the kit; f) signing legal contract with beneficiary; g) provision of professionalization kit to producer; h) verification of the use of the kit; i) evaluation of impact (impact)
  - Eligibility guidelines and requirements for supported producers

## Professionalization Kit

The professionalization kits will be complemented with the provision of specific trainings carried out with national partners, covering such topics as:

- Construction of workshop equipment
- Workshop safety
- Improved quality of production
- Social standards of work and environmental management
- Business and legal advisory services to professionals who potentially formalize the businesses. These trainings will be provided in order to make sure that assets of the kit are to their full potential.

## Financial Institutions

Financial institutions prefer business partners with a proper business address and with facilities that can act as a collateral for a loan. However, most professional producers still work on rented land in provisional facilities like shacks or garages. The ICS project will provide support to the producers to formalize their businesses by making improvements required for increasing the acceptability of the producers as loan-clients of the finance sector. ICS producers will procure land that will be used to develop the workshop building.

## Expansion of the ICS distribution and retail chain

80. To address the ICS supply chain barrier, the approach supports the expansion and professionalisation of distribution and retail chains. As the project expands into new markets/regions, it will undertake various activities to utilise existing LMEs (last mile entrepreneurs) in the distribution and retail chains. The support is provided through wholesalers, market traders, last mile entrepreneurs (LMEs), women groups, community-based organisations, cooperatives, etc., who are already familiar with the local market conditions and demand for ICS demanding the product once the awareness of the advantages has been established. The support is provided through procurement of distribution equipment and assets for producers, who sell directly to increase their flexibility, outreach and sales.

- Procurement of distribution kits (including flyers, advertisement materials, shelf, large umbrella, mobile phone) for LMEs to increase their outreach and advertisement.
- Procurement of distribution equipment and assets for existing LMEs
- Institutionnalisation of trainings at formal training institutions (youth polytechnics and Vocational Training Centres, etc.).

# Example 2: Expanding the Market-for ICS - An Integrated approach that works

In Senegal, the supply of ICS to urban markets in eight regions is well established. During the GCF project, the urban markets in six new regions and the rural markets in all fourteen regions will need to be expanded, or even newly created.

The urban markets in the six new regions will be integrated into the distribution network of the ICS producers based on the existing market structure. The rural markets are located in areas where there is no established market region. They are provided with an inventory stock of ICS based either on sales or on commission, resulting in a gradual expansion of the market. In both cases, the main objective will be to ensure that the same quality standards as those observed in urban markets are maintained.

The main aim of the project funding in the expansion of urban markets is to support the establishment and development of new ICS shops and/or kiosks in these regions. This will be achieved through demonstrations and theatre performances on markets to attract the attention of the potential clients. Radio and TV spots will also be used to promote the introduction of a new product and its advantages (and reach clients across each region beyond the urban areas).

The market expansion to rural population is significantly more difficult to achieve. Research shows that RWH (see above) are not yet widely available in rural areas, even though there has been some progress in reaching the ICS owners (though not exclusively). However, it is the challenge to bridge the gap between the depths in the capital cities and the villages. The GCF project aims at reaching 150,000-200,000 villagers of Senegal.

The following supply-sub-chains are envisaged and supported by the GCF project:

* NGOs organize the demand in "host villages" and facilitate the purchase at the depot or directly at the producer. The NGOs will also provide information on ICS and its advantages to the villagers.
* Traders on the weekly markets in rural areas will sell ICS to villagers who are visiting the market in search of other goods. This is a very important channel for reaching the villages, as it allows the traders to reach 50 persons in villages that are capable and willing to retail ICS in their community and neighboring communities.
* Local NGOs will be involved in the weekly markets in rural areas by organizing demonstrations, cooking demonstration and other awareness activities.
* Local NGOs will also organize a series of training sessions for the weekly market traders. The GCF project will support these through an initial kit of tools and marketing materials and financial support to cover the costs (village level).

### 82. Capacity building activities will address the following barriers:

- **Limited business nature:** The business class producers will receive particular support to enhance their ability to secure financing from financial institutions conditional upon their formal registration and their understanding of the requirements for accessing such finance.
- **Lack of market knowledge, skills and training:** The business class producers will benefit from training and coaching by business consultant (business skills, monitoring of stocks and finances, marketing of products, etc.).
- **Lacking risk-guarantee and own financial or property participation:** Participation in the professionalisation programme will enable the business class producers to access financing through collateral requirements through the technology provision in the professionalization kits ("push" grant mechanism). The business class producers will also be provided with training on how to use these tools to educate financiers about ICS sector specifics, including the nature, type and volume of production assets, and will support them in preparing their own financial statements.
- **Unfamiliarity with Loan Applications/Business Plans:** Through TA support, this process will create potential new business opportunities for the business class producers. The technical assistance will also be provided to other entities with more viable and feasible projects.

### 83. It is envisaged that around 20 companies in Kenya and 25th companies in Senegal will "graduate" with business status after receiving professionalization kits and will be ready to access market finance by the end of project. The expected total cost for this activity is EUR 1,974,860.

Generic approach in Sub-component 2: Enhancing consumer demand and ICS market environment

85. Under the second sub-component in both countries, demand-side interventions will address consumer demand barriers (i.e. Barriers 2.1 to 2.3, see Section C.2) and market environment barriers (i.e. Barrier 2.4). Awareness is key for behavioral change in this very traditional sector. Demand for ICS is likely to be influenced by better understanding of the benefits of ICS compared with other cooking technologies, such as improved health, reduced time spent on fuel collection, increased food security and improved household income through savings on biomass, convenience etc. The biggest challenge in this section of the value chain has been the lack of investment in large-scale awareness campaigns to promote ICS adoption at a national level. In many cases, these campaigns are largely public and not private. In this case, any company that invests in raising consumer awareness will be competing with other companies who may have already invested in similar campaigns and can be assured that consumers will buy from them rather than from a competitor. Furthermore, the marketing approach for rural markets is often to sell ICS on a "door-to-door" basis, which means that salespeople are often paid by commission “for fear” with family labour, and often do not realise the other benefits a modern ICS brings, such as improved health, reduced time spent on fuel collection, increased food security and improved household income through savings on biomass. PR for the national market: Awareness campaigns with nation-wide outreach as well as local and regional events that will be held in collaboration with key stakeholders (e.g. government, NGOs, private sector) to mainstream and increase efficiency and outcomes. Following a customer segmentation approach, interventions will target specific groups of consumers (e.g. women, young people etc.) through awareness/behaviour change campaigns, as well as tailored marketing of cleaner and efficient stoves for specific target groups in urban areas.

86. This intervention would also help to finalise and streamline the institutional and policy frameworks governing clean cooking sector, improve sector coordination and develop roadmaps and monitoring systems for sectoral engagement. The project will support the development of a market monitoring and verification system (MRV) including capacities for cookstove/fuel testing which will provide transparent data on cookstove/fuel performance in local markets. This will be done through a combination of national and international standards, which include national standards, testing capacity, long term training capacity, a functioning sector association, sector data and information management systems, and the development of a market monitoring system (MRV). Various institutions are involved in building this enabling environment from the national down to the county level.

Generic approach in Sub-component 3: ICS market monitoring and impact evaluation

87. The project will undertake regular ICS market monitoring during and after the completion of the GCF project to collect data on sales, market share, consumer awareness and other relevant indicators. This will initially apply through previous EnDev work through a developing monitoring and verification (MRV) system in its current form. The MRV system is designed to provide reliable sales data and track progress on project level, including via regular reporting of sales data from retailers and consumers. Retailers will be required to report monthly sales with which, sales that show that consumers are taking up the product. Retailers will provide data about sold or installed stoves to a central unit in the MRV system. The MRV system will also collect consumer awareness data on a regular (monthly) basis. Data will be managed in a central management unit that will perform random spot checks of retailers and consumers to ensure accuracy of reported sales and awareness levels. This data will then be applied for the purpose of monitoring and reporting on the impact of GCF project (See Section H.2 for a detailed description). The MRV system will also collect data from other sources, such as market research that use the EnDev Market Development Scorecard to systematically collect, analyse, and compare ICS market performance in different countries. This will be used to monitor the progress towards the end of project development and the progress towards its inflection point, i.e. when the market has reached the end of expansion phase (Figure 6) and is entering a period of natural growth (Figure 6). This point is an irreversible shift in the market that implies that it will develop further without ODA.

* In Kenya specifically, the project will work with the MoE, Ministry of Energy, and relevant public and private stakeholders to design and implement awareness materials that can be modified for different target groups in the counties served by the GIZ and the WRI project.
* In case the products sold in lots to distributors, the system may record only the bulk sale.

### Output 1: Accelerated market development for climate-friendly cookstoves in Kenya

#### Sub-component 1: Strengthening ICS supply and delivery chain

88. In Kenya, the project will strengthen supply chains for improved biomass cookstoves aimed at upgrading 52 local cookstove entrepreneurs from the artisanal level to the professional level and 13 from the professional level to the business class level (see Figure B.8). As of the original baseline survey in 2014, 79% of the artisanal and 60% of the professional class ICS producers form the backbone of the sector, and account for about half of the annual market sales of about 350,000 units. The business class ICS producers contribute to the rest of the market volume. Twenty new production centres in new areas for artisanal and professional level producers will be established by the end of the project period. It is expected that a total number of approximately 20 currently supported very small artisanal producers will drop out due to limitation in growth potential, lack of access to finance, or other reasons. The business class ICS producers are mostly traders and vendors, who are based in the localities and therefore reaching the last mile (i.e., LEM). By strengthening larger ICS production capacities in existing and new regions on the other side, the GCF-supported ICS market segment will continuously grow to meet demand for improved biomass cookstoves. The project will support 52 artisanal producers with about 100% of their capacity and 13 professional level producers with about 70% of their capacity until 2030. In line with overall project approach under Sub-component 1, such assistance will be provided based on performance-based principle and in packages covering improvements in mechanisation and production techniques, access to finance, business development services, renovation and expansion of sales/distribution channels as well as facilitation of access to market-based financing.
