| Artisanal (≤ 50 ICS/Months) | 102 | Upgrade artisanal producers to professional level |
| New (Estimated up to ≤ 1,000 ICS/Months) | | Establish drop-in artisan producers |

#### Activity 1.1.1: Professionalisation of ICS production

89. The activity improves the production volume, quantity and quality of ICS and of the ceramic liners. Producers in the artisanal level and professional category will be supported to facilitate their business expansion and thus transition to the professional and business class respectively. This activity will increase the number of professional and business class producers from 13 to 26 and from 2 to 52, respectively. The project in Kenya will upgrade 52 local cookstove entrepreneurs from the artisanal level to the professional level; and will support the transition of 18 entrepreneurs from the professional level to the business class level.

To do so, the project provides "professionalisation kits" investment supports for tools, equipment, machines, storage, safety equipment and distribution assets) via a performance-based grant mechanism to producers and facilitates stove quality assurance by stove testing, national standards and branding. ### Artisan Producers

Artisan producers who showed good performance during 2018 and 2019 (more than on average 40 stoves per month) would qualify for a package that helps them to reach professional production level (100+ to > 1,000 ICS per month). The first component is illustrated in Figure B. As illustrated in the figure, "the Start-up incentive" will be provided to artisans who have sold more than 40 ICS/month over a period of six months and who are willing to invest in professionalisation tools and training.

The second component is provided in two stages: If average sales over year increase above 100 ICS/month, the first half of the second incentive package will be provided. If average sales over one year exceed 250 ICS/month, the second half of the second incentive package (the third tranche for artisans transition to the professionalisation approach) will be provided.

The exact mechanism will be further elaborated in the operational manual and part of the project including criteria for eligible types of investments (e.g., production machines, containers...).

### Eligible Artisan Producers

- The first tranche of the professionalisation kit is expected to be around USD 4500. Eligible artisan producers are offered the professionalisation kit as a loan at an interest rate of 2% per annum for up to 20% of the value of the first tranche (1 of USD). This kit is step one in the professionalisation approach.
- During the GCF project, the monthly production and sales of the producers are closely monitored. Once the monthly average production exceeds 40 stoves over a period of six months, the artisan producer will qualify for step two of the professionalisation kit. And respectively with 200 stoves old per month over the past six months, the artisan producer will be eligible to receive the second tranche of the professionalisation kit to provide up to 20% own contribution. The exact mechanism will be further elaborated in the operational manual and part of the project including criteria for eligible types of investments (e.g., production machines, containers...).

The innovation project will also support the development of new production technologies and/or new production processes, which cannot use conventional machineries in order to enhance stove production capacity and quality.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Sub-component 2: Enhancing consumer demand and ICS market environment

### Activity 1.2.1 Awareness raising of consumers

103. In Kenya one of the key hurdles to adoption of ICS is social and cultural behavioural norms. Ordinary marketing and awareness activities, focusing only on the features of the cooking technologies/units, are not robust enough to change these deeply ingrained habits. The project will therefore focus on a combination of ordinary marketing and awareness activities with those that focus on elements of behaviour change to cater for the underlying cultural and social behaviours. The project will be implemented in collaboration with the Ministry of Energy, through its Clean Cooking Action (CCA) programme and the REDD+ programme– support the government agencies, particularly the Ministry of Energy, Ministry of Environment and Forestry, Kenya Meteorological Department (KMD), National Environment Management Authority (NEMA), Kenya Power and Lighting Company Limited (KPLC) and other relevant stakeholders. The project will focus on developing and implementing the behavioural change campaigns which will focus on health and climate impacts related to low quality cooking fuels and technologies. In addition, a series of awareness raising activities and promotional packages will be developed and applied in all counties by the GIZ/GIZ+WB KOSAP projects. The existing national media campaign will also be supported through this project.

### Activity 1.2.2 Creation of enabling market environment

104. The GCF project implementers will work with the Ministry of Energy to improve the policy and regulatory framework for ICS in Kenya. This will include working closely with the Ministry of Energy, the National Climate Change Directorate, will be supported in strengthening the monitoring of NDC targets for the climate friendly cooking technologies. The project will also support the Ministry of Health (MoH) in domesticating the indoor air quality guidelines with specific input from a clean cooking expert and other stakeholders. This will be done through the implementation of the Kenya National Climate Action Plan IV of 2018 - 2022. Since 2016 MoH has implemented the 'Domestication' of the WHO Household Air Pollution Guidelines in Kenya. The project will also support the Ministry of Energy to implement the Clean Cooking Act of 2017, which is projected to come into force by end of 2018. The project will also support the implementation of the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) and the National Development Plan IV (NDP4), which is planned until 2022. MoH established and institutionalized a coordination mechanism to address household energy and air pollution and climate change issues within the cross-sectoral Technical Working Group (TWG) [16].

### Project Implementation

105. The project will support the existing efforts of jointly supporting the sector in collaboration with government agencies, particularly the Ministry of Energy, through its Clean Cooking Action (CCA) to actively foster collaboration and coordination among sector players. This will be done by providing technical assistance for the development of a national ICS policy and an instrument rule for sector planning, lobbying and advocacy. CCA will be supported by establishing and maintaining a knowledge management system for sector data and information including providing regular updates on progress.

106. The project will finalise and facilitate enforcement of cooking sector regulations and standards by working closely with the Energy Regulatory Commission (ERC) and Kenya Bureau of Standards (KEBS), including the development of ICS specific standards and guidelines. This will be done through a series of workshops. Technical assistance will be provided to strengthen stove testing capacities in line with established standards in existing laboratories. The project will also support the establishment of an ICS testing laboratory and training centre as a reference centre and laboratory in Kenya and the region. In addition, the project will support

[16] Indoor air pollution is associated with diseases of lower and upper respiratory tract and the most vulnerable groups include women and children. According to WHO, 40% of all deaths from pneumonia among children under five years old are due to indoor air pollution. The problem is more acute in the rural areas where over 85% of the population lives. Indoor air pollution also causes a range of other health problems such as eye irritation, headaches and coughing. It is estimated that 30-40 million people die each year from exposure to indoor air pollution. This figure is higher than the number of deaths caused by malaria in all countries combined. Kenya is present in all the counties, sub-counties and district level with over 8M population and prevalence of indoor air pollution is high. The problem is more acute in rural areas where over 70% of the population lives.

[17] Both WB KOSAP and GIZ/GIZ+WB projects will support and closely coordinate their work on the strengthening of enabling environments for ICS market growth, including the development of relevant standards and other regulatory provisions.

### Establishment of new stove testing centers preferably in academic and research institutions to complement RIVD

#### Sub-component: 3. ICS market monitoring and impact evaluation

The project will conduct regular monitoring of ICS market development and evaluation of project impacts as further detailed in Section H.2.

This monitoring is aimed at delivering reliable results. On a monthly basis the producers will be visited and the production and sales data will be assessed and compared with the sales of the distributors. Additional sales with increased quality and quantity are generated due to and as consequence of the GCF project's investment in professionalization, capacity building, and market development activities. These additional sales can be attributed to the modernized and standardized production processes and can be counted as additional to the baseline or business-as-usual scenario.

A joint monitoring and reporting platform as well as the verification approach will be developed and applied based on existing EnDev monitoring system and experience. The platform, in collaboration with MEFP (especially in the context of the GCF project's contribution towards achieving the climate action agenda targets achievement) will include all Achieving Entities and Implementing Partners who are responsible for the implementation of the project activities and/or alternative measures as defined in the Gender Action Plan and include EMDs monitoring as defined in the ESMP.

#### Output 2: Accelerated market development for climate-friendly cookstoves in Senegal

### Sub-component: 1. Strengthening ICS supply and delivery chain

In line with the overall strategy to accelerate ICS market growth in Senegal, the project will increase the professionalisation of the ICS sector and transition of ICS producers to higher production levels as illustrated in Figure 2.3. The project aims at reaching a total of 100 business case producers by 2025 (at least half or more of the projected annual volume of 58,600 ICS annually). This 'business class' of ICS producers will form the backbone of Senegal's ICS market and will be responsible for a significant portion of the country's contribution to the sector (for more details and definition of producer categories - please refer to Section C.2.). The commitment of the project to support the professionalisation of the ICS sector in Senegal, through the 14 regions, 650 retailers (last-mile entrepreneurs), distributed across 3 regions of Senegal, will improve the access of consumers to high-quality and affordable ICSs.

By investing by expanding the outreach in new regions and the rural areas on the other side, the ICS market will continue to grow. The project aims at reaching a total of 25 business case producers by 2030 (at least one a much steeper growth path). The project will ensure that Senegal can substantially reach its unconditional objective of the NDC on ICS sales in 2030.

**Note:** In Senegal, about 160 large-scale producers are expected at the end of the 5-year reporting period, but another nine (9) producers are expected to reach business class sales volumes shortly after the project exit, totalling the total on the market to 25 business case producers.

### Artisan Producers who showed good performance during 2018 and 2019 (more than on average 25 stoves per month) qualify for a package that helps them to reach professional production level (100 to > 1,000 ICS per month). The kit will be provided in two tranches. The first tranche is co-financed by the GCF project and it will be a standardized one (same level of support for all producers). The production system of artisanal producers is often characterized by small deliveries, low capital investment, lack of access to credit facilities, etc. EDCo piloted the use of these professionalization kits to increase artisanal production in the baseline project. Based on this experience, the GCF project will provide a second tranche co-financed (value €71,800) for reaching professional production levels (100-1,000 ICS per month): hand tools for 4-7 staff, manual rollers (norer, jenny, cutting machine), a container for storage of raw materials, a container for storage of finished products, cutting gears, a Container for storage of assets and inventory, a tricycle for transportation of smaller deliveries etc. The actual composition of the standard kit will be further refined at the beginning of the GCF project through consultations with artisanal producers.

The GCF project procures the assets for the professionalization kits in bulk. The value of the first tranche of the kit is expected to be around EUR 7,000. Eligible artisanal producers are offered the professionalization kit but only if they have made their required payments according to agreements with the producer association. This contribution is put into account of the producer association which the producer is affiliated, which is part of the GCF project. The producer association will be responsible for the maintenance and distribution by the account of the association and will ensure that payments have been done according to agreements. They also need to provide a guarantee for the payment of the kit. The producer association will be able to further develop the ICS sector of the region (e.g., by awareness campaigns, trade fairs). Once the producer has made his required payments, the Chamber of Crafts informs the GCF project to hand over the

### Professional Producers

Professional producers who produced in average more than 100 stoves per month in 2019 qualify for a professionalization incentive that helps them to reach basic class production level (> 1,000 ICS per month). It is estimated that there are approximately 35 such producers in the project area (small scenario) and 48 in the large scenario.

The production systems of these producers are diverse. Some have workshop buildings, some use already electric machines, others have a good infrastructure for storage and distribution. The professionalization kit is designed to fit this group, as it is either defined too narrow or it is too comprehensive as some producers already possess some of the assets (leading to inefficient resource allocation). Instead of providing a standard professionalization kit, the project will provide each producer with an action plan and budget for each of the estimated 22 eligible producers. This tailor-made support ensures effective allocation of resources and avoids unnecessary costs. The total cost is estimated at EUR 180,000 (small scenario) and EUR 215,000 (large scenario). Producers will contribute up to 20% of the value of the kit on their own contribution in cash or kind. The project will provide a detailed action plan for each producer that defines the share of own contribution.

| Step | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Step 1: Initial set-up incentive | <ul><li>Producers receive a professionalization kit (€ 4,500)</li><li>Producers receive an initial production plan (€ 6,000)</li></ul> |
| Step 2: Performance-based incentive in two stages | <ul><li>Stage I: Producers receive a second professionalization kit (€ 3,000)</li><li>Stage II: Producers receive an additional production plan (€ 6,000)</li></ul> |

### Figure 11. Professional producers incentives in Senegal (small scenario)

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Eligible Producers | 35 | 48 | 35 | 48 |
| Total number of stoves produced | 7,600 | 10,900 | 12,000 | 15,000 |

### DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND (GCF) PAGE 1 OF 120

**116.** Many of the 22 professional producers are still working under rather informal conditions (at the roadside or on a market place with no address and no fixed building). Eligible professional producers will also be offered an investment package under which they are assisted to obtain a workshop building on a plot they provide (as their property) and the government provides the construction materials.

The project – through the BMZ co-funding – will support these investments with up to 90% of the total value of the investment, up to a maximum amount of €15.000 per producer. The exact criteria for eligibility and conditions will be further elaborated in the operational manual.

**117.** The recipients of all professionalization kits (artisanal and professional level) will receive very intensive technical and business training.

**118.** In addition, under activity 2.1.1, the project will support innovation and upgrade of ICS products, including revising and updating design for faster production; developing and testing technical solutions to reduce exposure risks in commonly found cooking systems of Senegal, as well as conducting lab- and field-testing of new national and imported ICS products.

**Activity 2.1.2 Explanation of distribution and retail chains**

**119.** The Senegalese ICS market is unevenly developed. Under this activity, the project will address deficiencies in distribution and retail networks with a particular focus on six under-served regions with lowest sales level (Figure 14). This will be done by identifying potential retailers and distributors of ICS products, establishing direct linkages between potential retailers in the regions and the producers and distributors of ICS. Various distribution models will be tested to ensure that the most appropriate model is selected for each region.

Institutional sales will be supported. The project will select young people in rural settlements to start retailing ICS in their communities as LMEs. They will receive training, marketing materials and to start their business. The up-take of products by these LMEs will be monitored and incentives provided for achieving specific missions. The exact mechanism will still be defined, but similar to the following example:

**Table 10: Financial Incentives for first ICS sales**

| Number of units sold | Total of ICS € paid |
|---------------------|--------------------|
| First 2 ICS are sold | 5                   |
| First 3 ICS are sold | 10                  |
| First 4 ICS are sold | 15                  |
| First 5 ICS are sold | 20                  |

These LMEs are bridging the gap between the existing suppliers of ICS in the weekly markets and the rural communities that surround them. The most efficient vehicle to connect rural households with ICS markets will be these young women. They are found within every village and are a key element of the social support system that allows women (including female heads of households) to overcome investment barriers through the building of their own creditworthiness, as well as providing an opportunity for them to develop their leadership skills to sensitize and convince women groups becoming retailers of ICS both for their own members as well as to other women in their communities. This will be particularly important in order to reach out to the poorer populations in villages below par. In parallel, an output-based performance incentive will be implemented for participating distributors if they achieve defined performance benchmarks.

**C**

#### Sub-component 1: Creation of enabling market environment

The project will work closely with relevant national authorities to ensure regular exchange of information about ICS market development, including the establishment and maintenance of a joint monitoring system for the sector. The project will also provide assistance in the development of an investment plan for the domestic energy sector, including estimation of the investment required to achieve NDC targets.

#### Sub-component 2: ICS market monitoring and impact evaluation

The project will conduct regular monitoring of ICS market development and evaluation of project impacts as outlined below. The project will also support the implementation of a gender mainstreaming approach with relevant national and local institutions.

#### Output 3: Improved knowledge on climate-friendly cooking solutions and their contribution to NDCs

The project will use the results, knowledge, and experience generated through Outputs 1 and 2, as well as the Executing Entity’s own research, to support the development of an ICS sector transformation strategy and the contribution such strategies can make to NDCs achievement.

#### Activity 3.1 Collection and codification of knowledge on ICS market development from Kenya and Senegal

This activity includes the compilation of data generated at national level as results of Outputs 1 and 2, including project results and impacts, with particular attention to gathering knowledge and evidence of the climate change benefits of ICS in both countries. The project will also conduct further research on the potential for scaling up impacts. The project will conduct regular ICS market studies, surveys and other analyses to assess the effectiveness of the proposed ICS market transformation strategy and identify lessons learnt and best practices including gender aspects.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 49 OF 120

### Energising Development (EnDev)

The baseline project activities in Kenya and Senegal are implemented by GIZ Kenya and GIZ Senegal as part of the global energy access programme, Energising Development (EnDev) energy access partnership. EnDev is currently financed by six donor countries: the Netherlands, Germany, Norway, the United Kingdom, Switzerland and Sweden. EnDev aims to provide affordable, reliable, sustainable and modern energy services for all people in developing countries.

The programme currently operates in 25 partner countries in Africa, Latin America and Asia, including 19 countries with active GIZ country offices (Figure 1). EnDev is a unique public-private partnership that has implemented global projects which operate on a small scale but have the potential to facilitate access to modern energy to millions of people by 3.22 billion (out of 6.2 billion) at the end of 2017. Through its performance-based approach and an unique business model, EnDev is able to work with local partners in developing countries.

The assessment of the governance and management of EnDev (See Annex 14: Evaluation Report of Baseline Project) showed that the programme has a lean and appropriate management setup. With the recent changes in the set-up, the management has been well adapted to the changed organisational requirements. EnDev management is now led by a Programme Director who reports directly to the GIZ Country Director. The Governing Board (meetings twice a year) bring their views of point subject to their country’s policy lines.

### Component 1: Kenya

The project works with one government EE and two International EEs in Kenya, which complement each other. It follows the most efficient and effective enabling entity structure for the envisaged activities. Following GIZ standards, all activities will be implemented transparently and the project will be open to expand partnerships and collaborate with other stakeholders. The project is led by a Project Director who has extensive experience in EE history and respective experiences in the sector globally and in Kenya and brings in transformative innovative approaches.

The capacities and competences of partner partners can be expanded and increased by this project.

### Detailed Project / Programme Description

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND (GCF) PROJECT PROPOSAL | PAGE OF 120

**139. GIZ and GIZ Kenya are implementing the Kenyan cookstove project since 2005 under the global energy access partnership EnDev. The activities are implemented in three major clusters of Kenya: Lake Victoria, Western cluster and Central cluster, addressing ICS demand in 22 focal counties and starting to expand to seven growth counties. The target population is estimated at over 13 million people.**

**The project has been successful in scaling up the market for improved cookstoves and the demand for ICS is still significant. GIZ Kenya is contributing to scaling up the cookstove market in Kenya, combining strengths of both GIZ and GIZ Kenya. The project is implemented by a consortium of partners working under an enabling environment. The key assets of GIZ are the long-lasting working relationship of trust with different partners, including government institutions, private sector companies, civil society organizations, international national institutions for standards and testing, research institutions, national and international NGOs, and others.**

**140. GIZ Kenya has the following tasks in the implementation of all activities that are at overall sector level:**

#### Sector coordination
- Coordination of the Project Coordination Committee on regular basis
- Consultation of the Project Advisory Board on regular basis
- Compilation and submission of project progress reports/documents
- Contribution to the implementation of the stove regulation and the national stove standards
- Contribution to the implementation of the awareness campaign
- Institutionnalisation of stove training and awareness creation

#### Professionalization of ICS producers:
- (Co-)Investment in production assets
- (Co-)Investment into distribution assets
- Linkage and capacity development to enable access to finance
- Technical assistance for quality control and improvement
- Advise on work norms (social, environmental and health standards)
- Contribution to the implementation of testing and training
- Extensive business training and coaching

**141. Ministry of Energy (MoE): The MoE hosts the State Department of Renewable Energy, which is responsible for policy, regulation and implementation of renewable energy projects in collaboration with relevant Partners. The Ministry has been a key partner to GIZ Kenya since 2005 in the implementation of the project. In implementing the project, MoE has been involved in the following areas: development of improved cookstoves; development, eftorhan for clean cooking and development of standards and regulations for improved cookstoves. Besides being a key partner to GIZ Kenya since 2005, MoE has been the lead political partner of EnDev and is chairing the EnDev Project Steering Committee (PSC).**

**142. MoE has the following experience in the ICS sector:**
- Provision of training of local artisans via the 16 energy centres
- Development of standards for improved cookstoves via the Kenyan Bureau of Standards
- Development of Regulations for improved cookstoves
- MoE is the chair of the Interministerial Committee on clean cookstoves

**143. The total project cost in the project period totals EUR 2.4 m.**

**144. The MoE has the following tasks in project implementation:**
- Chairing the Project Advisory Board of the project
- Finalisation and implementation of policy and regulation via the 47 county governments
- Development and dissemination (dissemination) of awareness and behaviour change messages and strategy and coordination of the implementation via the counties governments and via the local associations
- Implementation of awareness creation measures and stove demonstration/vihibition through the 16 energy centres
- Contribution to training and capacity development of stove producers and distributors
- Provision of the platform for coordination, collaboration and knowledge sharing between the two GCF operations of GIZ and MoE

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal | Page 51 of 120

### Component C: Senegal

#### 146. On the one hand, the project in Senegal is operating at a national level with sector-wide interventions. This entails all activities aimed at the professionalisation of the ICS production and large-scale distribution as well as the measures to improve the coordination of the sector. All these interventions will be implemented by GIZ Senegal as the lead implementing partner (IP) for the project. GIZ Senegal has a long-standing experience in the sector, with extensive experience in the ICS sector in the country. No other organisation in Senegal has comparable experience in the ICS sector and no other organisation is better placed to implement this project. The project will support the professionalisation support and coordinate the necessary activities to transform the market. The GCF project includes capacity building and performance-based incentives, access to finance, awareness raising and last-mile marketing.

#### 147. On the other hand, the project will be reaching out not only to the 15,000 villages in the 14 regions of Senegal for awareness raising and last-mile marketing support. This requires a) intense supervision of implementing partners (see below), b) the development of an effective network of local cooperatives and/or associations (LCA) and c) combination of talents of various organisations. For this purpose, regions have been grouped into five clusters of 2-3 regions each with a cluster manager (CM). The CMs are supported by the National Coordination Office (NCO) with EE functions (Cluster Management Unit, CMU), who are already working in these areas and their network of smallholder farmers (see Annex A). The smaller number of NCOs ensures reaching parity among all regions and rural households by their proximity to the villages, which is crucial for their success. These EE have been selected together with the Ministry of Petroleum and Energy via a competitive Expression of Interest process including an assessment of technical capacities and a thorough due diligence process of management and administrative capacities.

#### 148. GIZ Senegal profile and roles: GIZ Senegal has been working since 2005 to develop the ICS sector in the country, starting with the development of a market for local charcoal and later moving on to the production side over time to transform the ICS sector. GIZ has been working since the beginning with all ICS producers closely and is supporting them through capacity building and performance-based incentives. This close cooperation results in an increase of annual stove sales, which will continue much faster under the GCF project. GIZ is close linked with the Ministry of Petroleum and Energy, and has supported the ministry in the development of the sector through studies, coordination meetings, etc.

### Sector Coordination

- **Information of stakeholders (including civil society)**
- **Mobilization and engagement of stakeholders of the ICS sector (including annual 'town hall' camps')**

- **Networking with media (TV, radio)**

### Professionalization of ICS Producers

- **Co-investment into production and distribution assets**
- **Technical training on stover production, workshop organization**
- **Improvement in quality of stover production through inputs, materials and toolsing**
- **Advice on work norms (social, environmental and health standards)**
- **Extension services for producers**

### Three Cluster Management Units Profiles and roles:

- **ENDA ENERGIE has existed for about 30 years in Senegal. Their work focuses on access to energy, climate change adaptation and sustainable development.**
- **ENDA ECOPOP has 25 years of experience in good governance in rural areas of Senegal. They work mainly with women's groups and youth.**
- **CONCEPT has been working in Senegal for more than 20 years. They are active in 6 regions for the development of renewable energy, particularly solar energy.**

### The three regional management units will implement, procure and supervise services for the following tasks:

- **Assistance to the ICS sector in the identification of priority areas according to the capacity needs identified in the due diligence processes to enable the unlocking of their full potential and to equip them for long-term involvement in the sector after the project end.**
- **Local distribution (Last mile entrepreneur, weekly markets, women's group)**
- **GIZ Senegal will provide technical assistance to the three regional management units according to the capacity needs identified in the due diligence processes to enable the unlocking of their full potential and to equip them for long-term involvement in the sector after the project end.**

## C.7 Institutional / Implementation Arrangements

### During the project implementation, GIZ will act in the double function as Accredited Entity (AE) and as Lead Implementer (LI). The AE role is assumed by GIZ's headquarters corporate structure while the LI role is assumed between the two different roles. The AE and the EE roles of GIZ will be assumed by different units within GIZ's headquarters corporate structure in Kenya and Senegal. The AE role is assumed by GIZ's headquarters corporate structure, the EE role is assumed by GIZ's operation EE structure in Kenya and Senegal. Both structures are strictly separated and are accountable to different management structures within GIZ.

### As an Accredited Entity (AE), GIZ will take overall responsibility for the overall project as defined in the Accreditation Master Agreement between GCF and GIZ. As AE, GIZ will administer the funds on behalf of GCF and will provide oversight guidance and quality assurance through its relevant head quarter units for the Executive Entities.

#### Responsibilities include:
- **overall responsibility and oversight for the project**, including project preparation and implementation coordination
- **receiving the GCF proceeds as well as disbursing, administering and processing the funds (financial management)**
- **ensuring the proper use of the GCF proceeds** 
- **supervising the EEs**
- **assessing the integrity and capacity of the EEs**
- **setting up a subsidiary agreement with the EEs**
- **monitoring the performance of the EEs, including the performance of EEIs**
- **securing EE’s procurement according to AE’s rules and policies**
- **keeping records and submitting reports towards the GCF**
- **establishing internal control routines**
- **ensuring proper financial reporting**
- **providing financial reports to the GCF**
- **evaluating the project**

### 155. Overall quality assurance is guaranteed in cooperation with the specific departments at GIZ’s Head Office.

#### Departments:
- Finance department – responsible for strategic and operational financial control of the company, maintaining standard accounting practices, ensuring proper record keeping, managing accounts receivable and payables, etc.
- Procurement department – responsible for procurement, contracting, setting up the financing agreements with the executing entities; the execution and monitoring of tender processes through the procurement plan, etc.
- Compliance and Integrity
- Legal Unit and Insurance
- Auditing
- Environment
- Risk Management Unit
- Compliance with GIZ Safeguards and Gender Management System

## Overview of flow of funds and contractual arrangements

### 156. For the financing of the Project, GCF will enter into a Funded Activity Agreement with GIZ as AE, which will administer the funds on behalf of GCF and provide oversight guidance and quality assurance through its relevant head quarter units for the Executing Entities in accordance with the AMA and the FAA. The FAA will include a provision to the effect that the Project is financed by the German Federal Ministry for Economic Development and cooperation (BMZ) with project and GCF have approved a financial contribution to such project.

### 157. GIZ will enter into a co-finance arrangement (commission) with Germany’s Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ), and in-kind co-financing arrangements with the Governments of Kenya and Senegal, as well as the Ministry of Energy in both countries as well as the Ministry of the Environment and Sustainable Development in Senegal.

### 158. GIZ will also enter into Subsidiary Agreements with the Executing Entities in respect of the elements of the project to be implemented by such entities. The Subsidiary Agreements will be legally binding and outline the detailed financial arrangements, including the terms and conditions for disbursement of funds as well as other relevant provisions for the compliance with the requirements from the AMA and FAA.

### Project Governing Board:
The project uses the existing EnDev governing board structure for the overall governance of all activities. Members of the board are representatives of the Directorate-General for International Cooperation of the Dutch Ministry of Foreign Affairs (MFA NL), the German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ), the European Union Delegation to Kenya, the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), the Department for International Development (DFID), the Swiss Agency for Development and Cooperation (SDC), and the Kenyan Government. The project management unit of GIZ Kenya/Senegal is also a member of this board. All members are invited to these meetings for regular reporting on project impacts, outcomes, outputs, and implementation progress.

### Implementation and governance arrangements in Component 1 and 2:
The project uses the existing EnDev Project Advisory Board (PAB) in Kenya for overall guidance and advice only prior to the establishment of a new PAB that is newly formed for the GCF project. The baseline project already coordinates with these same entities in its work.

### Key Entities:
- Ministry of Energy
- Ministry of Environment and Forestry
- Ministry of Health
- Climate Change Directorate (CCD)
- County governments in all the counties in which the project is active

### PABs meet twice a year and have the following mandate/tasks:
- Provide overall guidance for the project, particularly regarding aspects of targeting the most vulnerable, gender balance, and sustainability.
- Provide regular feedback on annual work plans, annual reports, and audits.
- Ensure project synergy and coherence with the evolution of the international and national context, including country strategies.
- Be informed of project adherence with E&S safeguards and Gender Action Plan objectives.
- Support coordination of project activities across different line ministries and between private sector, public sector, and civil society.

### Lead Executing Entity (GIZ Kenya/Senegal):
The Lead Executing Entity provides the respective reporting and input to these meetings.

### The Project Coordination Committees (PCCs)

The PCCs meet monthly with the following tasks:

- Enhance common understanding among EE of the theory of change and how the ICS sector in both countries shall be transformed.
- Discuss strategies to promote best possible synchronisation of implementation between the EEs (to ensure that supply and demand for ICS grow simultaneously).
- Define project level work plans.
- Ensure that budgets and work plans are on track and monitor project progress.
- Identify issues requiring attention at the country level and bring them forward to the project level.
- Monitor adherence to environmental, social and fiduciary safeguards; monitor implementation of the Project Environmental Management Plan (PEMP) and Gender Action Plan (GAP, provided as a separate document), and steer review of these plans if needed.
- Identify issues required to be brought to the attention of the PCC and/or political decision makers.
- Provide guidance on the development of project sub-components.
- Agree on terms of reference, recruitment of experts.
- Discuss progress towards achieving outputs and results.
- Discuss reporting to the PAC and the overall project management.

## Notes

In Senegal, the Department of Forests and Water of the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development may also take part in the PCC. In Côte d'Ivoire, the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock, the Ministry of Energy and Mines, and the Ministry of Petroleum and Energy may also take part in the PCC due to their role in coordinating and supporting the enabling environment.

# Implementation partners

## Research
- Quality assurance, testing of innovations, national stove camp events.

## Training Institutes
- Training on ICS production methods, business training and coaching, social and health approaches, environmental management, social management, and gender mainstreaming.

## (Micro)Finance Institutions
- Access to credit, training on application

## Suppliers of tools and machines
- Local production of tools and machines for ICS production, supply structures.
- Use of existing extension structure for food security, environment, energy, forest etc. to reach out to households. Ecowe has already identified NGOs in both countries which are willing to add ICS promotion through cooking demonstrations etc on their normal activities.

- Producers and local retailers will be linked to the identified demand (eg women groups).

- All data gaps will be identified in the course of project implementation.

### D.1 Value Added for GCF Involvement

The GCF contribution in the form of a grant is critical to comprehensively address a complex set of barriers jeopardizing ICS market growth and investment in the sector. The GCF grant will help remove these barriers on the demand side, including: (i) lack of awareness about ICS products; (ii) low transaction volumes and geographic coverage, creating consumers' awareness and putting in place an enabling policy and regulatory environment; (iii) limited access to finance for ICS producers; and (iv) weak market conditions. In the target countries, the ICS sector has no opportunities to access other forms of finance rather than government grants.

Without the GCF intervention, it is unlikely that either the ICS sector in Senegal or Kenya would substantially improve independently to achieve the level of ICS production and sales compatible with NDC targets and the market conditions required for a sustainable long-term growth trajectory. The current market conditions are not conducive to achieving the NDC targets in the near future. Although the Emerging Development Partnership has been working in the ICS sector in both countries for many years, it is unlikely that the EDP alone will be able to catalyze the needed market transformation to reach the NDC targets by 2030.

The GCF partnership with the EDP and other stakeholders, including the private sector, will leverage lessons learned in the last few years, successfully support a gradual market expansion at about 1.5% or 5% growth as is the case of Kenya. The GCF grant will be used to finance the implementation of the first phase of the proposed GCF project to transform the market and reach an ODA-independent trajectory that will lead to the achievement of the NDC targets by 2030.

The financial sustainability of ICS producers in both countries includes engagement with the traditional banking sector (in both countries, the financial returns from initial capital investments are insufficient to contract market-based finance) and, importantly, the professional techniques and technologies are not widely known or available to ICS producers. The professionalization of the ICS sector is a key element for its long-term sustainability. Although the quality has only improved but, the shift of the sector away from current practices to a sector that produces more efficient shows that the sector is moving in the right direction. However, the lack of access to finance remains one of the major financial sector (or finance provider) in each country, thus providing it with long-term sustainability.

High collateral requirements and high interest rates are the obvious barriers. However, even existing concessional financing mechanisms such as microfinance institutions (MFIs) do not provide sufficient support for ICS sectors as it currently stands. This is because approximately 90% of ICS production comes from micro-scale, individual, part-time producers who have no access to formal credit and financial services. The lack of collateral, incomplete regulations, balance sheets, credit history, documented sales records, and formal corporate structure makes such informal sector producers unable to access finance through the traditional banking sector. In addition, many ICS producers have to rely on either self-financing or on informal sources (e.g., family and friends). GCF grant support is essential for the ICS sector in both countries as it will help to address these barriers by providing technical improvements, identify and set-up appropriate distribution channels, etc. and bring artisanal and professional producers to the business model that can be scaled up so that they are able to access finance from formal financial institutions.

To this end, a grant rate to the GCF project will provide additional performance-based supplemental technical support and training packages will be offered to ICS producers of the business class that meet certain performance criteria and other conditions. The GCF grant will also help to address the barriers on the supply side by engaging the ICS sector with finance providers and by deferring the lending costs and risk for the FIs in these countries that are at risk adverse and have not yet engaged well with domestic ICS producers. The targeted additional support should be provided through a combination of technical assistance, capacity building, and financial intermediation, ensuring the use of GCF financing, the hand-off to alternative financiers, and the long-term sustainability of the sector.

This project offers a high-value-added proposition for the GCF due to its well-designed approach to significantly lower domestic GHG emissions in these countries permanently with an irreversible market transformation.

# RATIONALE FOR GCF INVOLVEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 51 OF 120

The project will result in a functioning ODA-independent value chain and market for climate friendly cookstoves that will increase steadily after project end until market saturation to allow the majority of households worldwide in low-income countries to have access to affordable, efficient, and clean cooking solutions. This will meet their needs and improve their health. Furthermore, the lessons-learned from this approach will be introduced and could be scaled up by other development and climate finance actors to reach an additional 1 billion people and the nearly 3bn persons worldwide cooking primarily with wood and charcoal (solid fuels). The global collaboration with ENDev will ensure that the project is sustainable in the long-term. All relevant lessons, best practices, and institutional knowledge gained from the project will be shared with other development and climate finance actors.

## D.2 Exit Strategy

The overall objective of this project is to achieve an irreversible shift in the market for ICS within 5 years so that the markets in both countries revert from ODA-driven to ODA independent growth, and thus continue to grow in many other developing countries as well. The project will be designed with a clear exit strategy which design concepts are meant to ensure sustainability of its results and impacts in the long-term specifically:

- **The project will focus on the private sector** as the driver and enabler of large-scale cooking sector transformation using a performance-based incentive approach that fosters ownership and sustainability of the business model. The project will work with ICS producers, distributors, and retailers to ensure that they are equipped with sufficient technological base, managerial capacity and working capital/access to ICS market growth opportunities. This will be achieved by providing technical assistance, training, and access to finance for ICS producers moving from artisanal to professional and business categories, with sales rising commensurately. The project will also provide a performance-based incentive package that provides high returns to ensure high production volume and a sustainable growth trajectory without the need to rely on additional subsidies.

- **The project will strengthen all elements of the ICS supply chain**, production, distribution and after-sales services. The latter is essential for the project’s sustainability as it will a) ensure customers’ easy access to repairs and maintenance; b) provide an opportunity for ICS producers to expand their market share by means of operations in new service areas. Furthermore, the project will strengthen partners needed to continue the development of the ICS sector beyond its life cycle. This includes strengthening the role of local NGOs and other organisations as well as local government – who must continue work on further design improvement, testing, labelling and awareness raising of the health and economic benefits of using ICS for households.

- **ICS will be sold at a price that is competitive with existing cooking solutions** to consumers. Therefore, as a minimum, ICS sales are not decided at the end of the project (unless the economic situation deteriorates significantly) as it was often the case in the end of the publicly sponsored ICS distribution programmes.

- **The project will promote localised, affordable technological solutions**, which have been identified and field-tested through ENDev’s earlier work in the countries, and/or which will be further developed during the project. The reason for this is that there are significant differences between regions and even within regions at this stage that is inherent when piloting consumer acceptance of new stoves. Also all ICS will be produced and assembled locally to ensure a strong home and materials throughout the entire supply chain, which is an important factor for long-term sustainability.

- **Performance-based access to financial technical support package** will be specifically designed to encourage professionalised ICS producers to actively engage with the financial sector during the project in

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 80 OF 120

parallel technical assistance activities are designed not only to support ICS producers in the development of persuasive business plans and financial proposals, but also to introduce selected financial institutions to the GCF window. The objective is to encourage the establishment of Savings and Credit Cooperatives (SACCOs) as intermediaries between ICS producers and distributors for investment capital and working capital, such as the establishment of SACCOs20, promotion of their membership, and provision of technical assistance in terms of business planning, marketing, and linking of (MFI) and business class ICS producers. Therefore, there is a definite exit strategy for GCF support after 3 years from the start date (2024) and a take-over by local financing institutions to support the sustainable development of the sector.

176. In summary, the approach will lead to the commercialization of the sector. The provided support is designed to encourage professionalized ICS activities first to ramp-up production and then sales avenues in the distribution network. Technical assistance will be provided to ICS producers for the development of persuasive business plans and financial proposals as well as technical assistance on marketing, branding, and other similar stakeholders to help develop appropriate products and explore opportunities to bring in non-grant instruments to finance the sector (e.g., microfinance institutions, SACCOs, etc.). The exit strategy is to mobilize local banks and MFIs to provide credit for ICS producers.

177. The project strategy rests on the assumption that with over 1000 ICS sales/monthly, the business class ICS producers will be creditworthy. Therefore, its exit strategy is to: a) maximize number of producers at business class status by providing technical assistance and marketing support; b) mobilize local banks and MFIs and other stakeholders to develop and market dedicated business loans to ICS producers; and at the same time d) mobilize local banks and MFIs to provide credit for ICS producers.

20 Savings and Credit Cooperatives (SACCOs) are community based financial institutions. A SACCO is owned, managed and run by its members who have a common bond of membership is open to all community members regardless of race, tribe, gender, political affiliation, religion or job status. A member of the SACCO is a person admitted to membership after registration in accordance with the SACCO’s bylaws.

### Adaptation impact potential

#### 180. In addition, due to the market-based approach, the ICS sector will continue to grow beyond the implementation period (albeit at slower growth rate but higher than in the baseline scenario) leading to estimated cumulative sales of 11.68 Mts ICS and indirect GHG emission reductions of 24.77 Mt CO2 eq until 2030.

#### Adaptation impact potential

**181. The project will also result in additional climate change adaptation benefits, as follows:**

- **Reductions in fuel use are directly contributing to the saving of resources (time or labour) for households and communities.**
- **Improved access to energy services through the availability of biomass fuels. Reductions in specific fuel use allows households to adapt to shortages in the supply of fossil fuels.**
- **Improved health outcomes for families adopting the ICS technology, which will lead to an increase level of household income.**

**182. The project will also benefit vulnerable groups. The project has been designed to maximize and ensure long-term sustainability of its climate impacts (in the form of GHG emission reduction) by a national strategy that ensures sustainable adaptation co-benefits for the most vulnerable groups of consumers. For example, in Senegal, the regions in Senegal that are currently underused and will be targeted by the project have also been identified as areas where there is high vulnerability to climate change due to their heavy dependence of the local population on depleting forest resources (Section 3.5.2 climate risk map). Therefore, the project will contribute to reducing the negative impacts of climate change through changes in consumers' behavior that will lead to use of ICS and the adaptation benefits listed above, while also contributing to sustainable development goals.**

#### Further measures

**183. Further measures to improve the supply of biomass fuel and improve forestry management outside of the proposed project in both countries are also necessary to adequately address the problems resulting from climate change. The project will engage with relevant stakeholders and partners within the country, outside of the proposed ICS project. The project will encourage the development of a national strategy that ensures sustainable adaptation co-benefits for other stakeholders and partners within the country, outside of the proposed ICS project. This will be feasible to make a meaningful and efficient contribution to this "other side" of the risk problem in the same GCF project.**

184. Efficiency gains on fuel use of cookstoves are accompanied by reduced exposure of the cook and often children or elderly who are also in the cooking area to harmful emissions such as particulate matter, carbon monoxide, and other toxic gases. The reduction in these emissions from the stove can contribute to reducing the health risks of diseases among the most vulnerable populations in both Senegal and Kenya.

185. For example, an independent scientific study of a random sample of households in Senegal[3] showed that there is a significant reduction in eye infections and respiratory illnesses among those who cook with an ICS of the type promoted by EnDev. Some of this benefit resulted from the reduction of smoke from the stove, but other factors were also involved such as the fact that the stove is easier to clean than wood stoves; the good-shielding of the stoves, which makes it possible to cook outside even when it is windy; and the fact that the stove allows for better moisture monitoring and allows the cook to work on other tasks away from the stove during the cooking period.

186. Health benefits are a major impact area or co-benefit of ICS use, and Emerging Development is engaged in several projects with partners in Senegal and Kenya to assess these impacts. As part of the project will apply the Cooking Energy System (CES), currently being developed by Emerging Development, to assess the health impacts of ICSs on households using them. The CES measures health impacts from cooking, such as using improved ventilation, cooking more outdoors, and regularly using the ICS. The CES is a tool that can be used in several countries and contexts. It has been tested and will continue to collaborate with universities and other partners involved in the Clean Cooking Alliance that conducts research on the health impacts of ICSs. A study conducted by RWI-Leibniz-Institut for Wirtschaftsforschung in a health impact study on cookstoves including ICS promoted by EnDev, which will be published during the project lifetime.

### EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

#### E GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 84 OF 120

**E.7** The ICS system, which will be cost-effective and addresses best practices for reducing HAP (Household Air Pollution), is an evaluation approach for ICS interventions developed by EnDev that analyzes the quality of cooking energy services based on three criteria – Access, Health and Convenience. Under Health, a holistic assessment of the health benefits of improved cooking energy services is conducted using proxy indicators. More factors are evaluated in order to determine the health effects of cooking based on proxy indicators.

**188.** The health benefits from ICS-interventions can be described along the following impact chain: Reduced烹饪排放物 (HAP) exposure, which leads to improved respiratory health and reduced mortality. This is achieved by design elements, which improve the combustion in the stove resulting in less smoke and a hotter fire that could lead to better cooking results. The design of the ICS stove itself will be evaluated based on the design of cooking location (kitchen with/without window, hangar, low wall high walls). The remaining emissions are mainly due to incomplete combustion of biomass fuel. This is influenced by factors during the cooking process (and on the prevalence of other sources of smoke), thus the exposure in one room can be lower or higher than the average HAP, depending on behavior and location of each person.

**189.** EnDev will apply the CES approach in the ICS project and assess empirically the effect of the ICS technology on (a) air pollution reduction and health benefits and (b) user behavior will be developed and integrated into large-scale awareness campaigns conducted with partners. The results from the field surveys will be used to develop a revised CES methodology, and the revised CES methodology will be applied again in both countries during the project period.

In applying the CES, the project measure the following with field surveys and then address them in awareness campaigns:

- How to minimize HAP when using the ICS
- Treatment of smoke
- Treatment of stove cleaning, maintenance, repair
- Ventilation and kitchen design

**190.** The proposed project will not devote project funds for monitoring air pollution by gas and PM, as health effects are not a focus of the project. In particular, the project will not attempt to measure reduction of black carbon, as this is not an established practice, but rather a scientifically difficult, costly and time-consuming process. The project will also not attempt to measure the impact of ICS on CO2 emissions reductions, for which we have evidence. This project does also not promote the replacement of existing cooking technologies with ICS stoves. The project is funded by Energinet Development's interventions for improved energy access with solar lamps.

| $E R_{\mathrm{y}}=B_{\text {average }} \int f_{\mathrm{my}} N C V_{\text {annual }} \cdot E F_{\text {required fueloil }}$ |
Where:
- $E R_{\mathrm{y}}$: Emission reductions during the year y in tCO2e
- $B_{\text {average }}$: Quantity of woody biomass that is saved in tonnes
- $f_{\mathrm{my}}$: Fraction of woody biomass that can be extracted from the total amount of woody biomass

Expected total number of direct and indirect beneficiaries:

193. Only the number of direct beneficiaries disaggregated by gender and other vulnerable groups have been estimated to stay on the conservative side, as follows:
- An adjustment factor of 1:2 (i.e., every second household) determines the number of people benefiting from the ICS use (of ICS = average household size). However, in Senegal, households always use 2 stoves and in Kenya, field observation shows that some households use two stoves. Therefore, a factor of half of the household size is used to estimate the number of direct beneficiaries.
- In parallel, an adjustment factor of 1:9 (i.e., every ninth household) has been adopted to estimate indirect beneficiaries:
    - Number of women beneficiaries – based on national statistical data (share of women in total population)
    - Number of women-headed households – based on national statistical data (share of women-headed households in the total number of households)
    - Number of children – based on national statistical data (share of children per household)

#### 199.
The replication potential of the project within the targeted countries is significant. The unversed market size in Kenya and Senegal is estimated at 4 million households. If all the planned replicated project aims at significantly tapping this potential, ICS sales will grow during project lifetime by 24% and 30% per year respectively.

The replication potential in other countries is also significant. Globally there are over 8 billion people living on less than $1 a day. The project aims at reaching 5 million households in the ICS sector growth beyond its timeframe thus ensuring continued replication impact of the GCF investment.

### E.2.2 Potential for knowledge and learning

#### 201.
The creation of knowledge among the many ICS value chain stakeholders: producers, distributors, individual consumers, public authorities, as well as public authorities is a key element of the ICS market transformation strategy adopted by the project. Learning objectives and activities have therefore been integrated into all sub-components.

#### 202.
At the national level, under Sub-component 1 "Strengthening ICS value chain" in both Kenya and Senegal, the project will provide extensive training and tailored mentorship to some 100 ICS producers to help improve their business practices. The training will be provided by experienced trainers from the project team including working conditions, health, safety, and environment. It will also support ICS producers in learning about market access strategies, sales techniques, and marketing skills through a series of workshops. In addition, the project will provide training to 2,000 additional ILMs on effective marketing and sales approaches for ICS products.

#### 203.
Further, under Sub-component 2 "Enhancing Consumer Demand and ICS Market Environment" the project will raise awareness and knowledge about ICS and their benefits (economic, health, and environmental) among consumers. The project will also provide training to community leaders, youth, and education campaigns, as well as local authorities and community organisations, including women's groups in Senegal on how to promote ICS products. It will also provide training on monitoring energy applications at youth psychics as well as vocational training centres.

#### 204.
The project will implement a Gender Action Plan (GAP) with project partners, monitor the results, and share lessons learned. The project will ensure that all activities under the GCF have the expectation to continue gender-sensitive support to the ICS value chain.

### EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROGRAMME | PAGE 1 OF 120

The GCF puts a strong emphasis on dedicated training for women, including gender-specific business and entrepreneurship training, as well as other training (including literacy, if needed) on demand. Although women already play an important role in the ICS value chain, particularly in the production of clay inserts, and it is estimated that 70% of the ICS producers are women, they still face challenges in accessing ICS production companies. Female entrepreneurs in particular feel hampered by a lack of business skills, which can be addressed by training.

The project will support collaboration with universities and public offices involved in testing and labelling ICS, will support national and local government in allocating resources for necessary public sector expenses related to the promotion of ICS, and will provide technical assistance to key private sector stakeholders on designing effective promotion campaigns for ICS. In Kenya, the project collaborates for example with the Ministry of Energy and Petroleum, which has a mandate to promote ICS together with a wide range of stakeholders on marketing of the ICS; in Senegal, the project will collaborate closely with involved ministries, and provide a professional communication expert to support the Ministry of Petroleum and Energy. The project also collaborates with the National Institute for Agricultural Research and Rural Development (INAR) in Senegal, which is responsible for the promotion of renewable energy technologies at national level; INAR will be involved in the design and implementation of marketing activities on county and village level.

206. Sub-component 3 in Kenya and Senegal, regular ICS market monitoring activities will directly contribute to the project’s impact (e.g., by providing information about the market for ICS products (production and sales, consumer awareness, preferences, etc.). Impact evaluation studies will assess co-benefits of ICS (health benefits, environmental benefits, etc.) and provide evidence on how to improve the business case for continued political support to the sector among public authorities and international organisations.

207. Lastly, Component 3 of the project has been designed to improve knowledge on climate-friendly cooking solutions in Kenya and Senegal. The project will document all relevant experiences made and lessons learnt in the project are documented. These are made available as knowledge products for other parties, national governments, development organisations etc., and applied in interventions as well as replicated in future project proposals.

### E.2.3 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

#### 208. The project will contribute to the creation of an enabling environment for accelerated ICS market growth in Kenya and Senegal.
- A strong ICS production sector capable of delivering sufficient quantity and quality of ICS products to meet demand (SDG 7)
- Professionalisation of ICS production* will ensure that in both Kenya and Senegal such a sector exists, is sustainable and can be scaled up
- An effective network of distributors and retailers reaching out to even the most remote parts of the country will ensure that ICS are available for all households
- Expansion of distribution and retail chains* will support this measure.
- A greater number of consumers will be informed, educated, and motivated in the support of the ICS sector: by collaborating with larger NGOs on EEs and service providers in the implementation of awareness campaigns (SDG 4), and by engaging with government officials to keep them with the relevant ministries
- Broader coordination and support from the relevant sectors of the sector players, like the Clean Cooking Association in Kenya (CCAK). The project support CCAK in the implementation of its core activities.
- Interministerial cooperation, including the ministries responsible for health, environment, energy etc., on a platform for coordinated clean cooking awareness creation campaign preparation. The project will also work with other relevant stakeholders such as the Ministry of Energy and Natural Resources (MENR), PAB and PCC
- A greater number of MFIs and banks capable and willing to provide financial services in support of investments in production and distribution infrastructure of larger ICS producers. See Activities 1.1.3 and 2.2.
- A broad awareness on the benefits and viability of ICS usage amongst all relevant population groups (SDG 4)
- Effective support from public and civil society stakeholders at the national and local level (See Activities 1.2, and 2.2)

#### E.2.4 Contribution to the creation of enabling policies and work

209. In Kenya, the project will support the Energy Regulatory Commission (ERC) and Kenya Bureau of Standards (KEBS) in finalisation, adoption, and enforcement of ICS regulations and standards, as well as national energy policy.

#### 210. In Senegal, the project will support the development of an investment plan for the domestic energy strategy, including estimation of the investment needs and potential public, private, and international sources to finance these investments (SDG 7). The project will also support the establishment of a joint monitoring committee with relevant national stakeholders in both countries. In addition, close collaboration with national ministries and relevant departments will ensure that sufficient knowledge and capacity for further support on ICS market development solutions through market-based approaches is available for further necessary support after project end.

### E.3 Sustainable Development Potential

#### 211. The project will deliver numerous sustainable development benefits and will make a tangible contribution to the achievement of several SDGs, including but not limited to: reducing poverty (SDG 1), lessening exposure to health risks (SDG 3), reducing childbirth for women in collecting wood fuel (SDG 5), improving access to modern energy (SDG 7), reducing GHG emissions and strengthening communities resilience against climate change (SDG 13). The project will also contribute towards the achievement of SDGs 2, 6, 8, 9, 10, 11

The IPCC clearly recognises the high potential of ICS to deliver multiple sustainable development benefits, which go beyond climate change mitigation. First, the use of inefficient cooking devices leads to indoor air pollution and is a major cause of respiratory diseases, as well as other health problems. The use of the improved cooking systems promoted by this project will contribute to the reduction of the health risk caused by smoke and soot because of decreased indoor air pollution in the households where ICS are used.

213. Scaling-up ICS adoption also reduces the pressure on forests and biodiversity[6], reduces drudgery for women in collecting wood fuel, and saves money if fuel needs to be purchased. Benefits from wide-scale adoption of ICS include reduced deforestation, improved air quality, and increased health benefits. Social and environmental benefits are accounted for. Furthermore, the IPCC 5th Assessment Report clearly identifies energy poverty as a major driver of deforestation in developing countries. The project will reduce pressure on forests, thus increasing resilience of the forest ecosystem to climate change[7]. The project will limit its intervention area to areas where ICS have not been widely adopted yet and where other public and donor programmes in both countries are preparing effective programs to improve forestry. Please see Annex 1 for a map of the adoption areas.

214. Other sustainable development benefits of the project include reduction of energy poverty, creation of new long-term jobs, additional opportunities for education and income-generating activities, in particular for women engaged in ICS value chain.


[6] Please see also the discussion on co-benefits to be discussed in p. 53. This project will not address the value chains for the supply of biomass, as these can be addressed effectively on other projects focused on the forestry sector.

[7] In addition to the IPCC 5th Assessment Report, please refer to: J. Hauke, J. D. Moomaw C. McVie, N.N. Reimannstein, W.G. Rice, S. Ribicoff, A.A. Romanovskaya, F. Segawa, and T. Tubaki; 2014. Agriculture, Forestry and Other Land Use (AFOLU) in the IPCC Fifth Assessment Report of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), Cambridge University Press, Cambridge, United Kingdom and New York, NY, USA: Chapter 11, p. 86

### E.4. Needs of the Recipient

#### E.4.1. Vulnerability of country and beneficiary groups (Adaptation only)

**n/a**

#### E.4.2. Financial, economic, social and institutional needs

- **202 countries are low income to lower-middle-income countries**, which have demonstrated commitment in implementing NDCs but lack sufficient implementation and financial capacities to carry out comprehensive market transformation programmes without additional international support.
- **Kenya**: Kenya's population is estimated at 48 million (about 9.6 million households) (2016) and is growing at an average rate of about 3%. Of the total, 75% of the population are in the rural sector and 25% in the urban area. 32% of urban dwellers and 51% in the rural areas (45% of the population) live under the poverty line on less than USD 1.90 per day. The majority of Kenyan households (87%) are small-scale farmers, with most being labour markets that include household-based enterprises, subsistence agriculture and a small wage economy. Most urban dwellers work in the informal sector or are unemployed. In rural areas, about 65% to 77.7 percent have no work. Most people in rural areas (44%) work on family agricultural holdings with low or no pay. This has a bearing on the affordability of the cooking technologies available in the market.

This project will be implemented in at least **12 counties** (22 districts) of the Enclaved region but also venture into at least 15 additional new counties in the North Eastern, Central Region, Coastal region and Northern Kenya where the market for ICS is still underdeveloped.


---
**Page 77**
---

2023 Kenya has been one of the fastest growing economies in sub-Saharan Africa for the past five years ranging from 5-9% annual GDP growth per year. The country has strong international trade and transport links, a growing middle class, increased public infrastructure investments and established tourism, financial services and manufacturing sectors. However, the economy is still vulnerable to external and economic shocks from rising costs, security concerns and climatic conditions. The World Bank, IMF and other agencies forecast growth in the same range (3.5% for 2016; 6.1% for 2017) and adds the impact from slow credit growth since 2016.

2024 The Gokt is committed to reducing its fiscal deficit to less than 6% of GDP from over 8% currently through current reforms, including a new tax law, which will be implemented in April. Kenya has also signed with the IMF to maintain access to the precautionary Stand-By Agreement of USD 1.5 billion to withstand monetary shocks.

2025 Both the IMF and the Department of Treasury have recently warned about the level of Kenya's public debt. Official data shows total public debt at KSh 4.37 trillion (approximately EUR 38 billion) at the end of December 2017 as the build-up of massive borrowing that began five years ago continued unabated (including USD 5.6 billion in 2017). The mountain of debt is comprised of 51% of foreign loans and 48.1% of domestic loans. The overall increase is attributed to a combination of factors, including the country's reliance on external borrowing compared to external loans during the period, according to the Treasury. Concern over Kenya's debt load is mainly due to the fact that it has been used to finance consumption rather than growth, indicating the widening gap and putting pressure on the government's capacity to repay loans.

2026 Senegal is one of the world’s least developed countries; it is ranked 182 out of 188 in the human development index. The country faces a number of challenges including climate change. On the external front, debt was estimated at 62% of GDP in 2017 and the current account deficit worsened from 4 to 7.9 billion in 2017 due to rises in imports of oil and capital goods, and exports expanded less rapidly.

2027 The economy relies heavily on cash crops and fishing, both of which are vulnerable to climate change. However, the country’s population is growing at a rate of about 3% per year, with over half of its people living in the capital, Dakar, are poor, compared with two-thirds of those living in the country's rural areas. The World Bank estimates that 20-40% of Senegal’s population (or at least 17%) of people are food-insecure and in some – mostly rural - parts of the country, the prevalence of global acute malnutrition is critical. Rapid population growth (3.2% a year) will further exacerbate competition for and pressure on natural resources.

2028 Almost one of the 1.6 million households of Senegal use one, and for the most part two stoves for firewood or charcoal to prepare their daily food. EnDev estimates that about 87% of households still rely on inefficient solid fuels such as wood, charcoal and crop residues. These are not only expensive but also uncleanly served, as far as access is to clean water. According to the National Agency for Civil Aviation and Meteorology (ANACIM), Senegal has a very high level of air pollution in its cities. The project will focus on both the centre of the country and the south to increase the ICS market; to increase the affordability of these stoves, the project will also provide subsidies to households using these fuels. It will help the population to manage their individual adaptation to a changing climate environment.
