E.S. Country Ownership

### 5.1 Existence of a national climate strategy and coherence with existing plans and policies, including NAPAs, NAPs and NAPs

#### Kenya: Kenya's NDC has also identified clean energy technologies (including ICS) as a high potential intervention to reduce CO2 emissions from the sector. The country has already made progress in this area in a number of specific plans and policies. Kenya has developed a National Climate Change Response Strategy (NCCRS), which is aligned with the Kenya Vision 2030, the Kenya National Development Plan (KNDP) 2016-2030, which provides the vision for low carbon and climate resilient development, and the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP). The NDC also identifies several specific actions to address different areas such as afforestation and reforestation, geothermal and other clean energy development, energy efficiency, renewable energy, agriculture, and drought management. However, the draft NDC for 2018-2022 is still under development, and therefore, the strategic alignment of the following measures aimed at expedited ICS market development in Kenya:

* Promotion of the use of quality wood stoves and promotion of quality standards and servicing of clean cookstoves, e.g., tax-relief incentives for manufacturers, training (both management and technical) to improve production and distribution networks.
* Supporting local businesses to stock improved cookstoves, with an emphasis on women-led businesses.
* Establishment of timber plantations for sustainable biomass supply through various programmes such as the Community Forest Programme.

#### Senegal: Because of the large share of CO2 emissions stemming from the use of firewood and charcoal for cooking, there are explicit interventions for reducing the use of non-renewable wood as fuel for domestic consumption. The NDC has set a target to reduce the use of firewood and charcoal in domestic cooking with wood fuels have been defined in the mitigation objectives in the NDC, including specifically the diffusion of 8.4 m³ ICS for firewood and charcoal. As far as Senegal's key development priorities are concerned, the government is currently implementing a project to promote the use of ICS, as formulated in the Plan Sénégal Émergent (PSE) – to foster economic growth and reduce poverty. To do so, "the PSE aims at promoting the diffusion of improved cookstoves and other clean cooking technologies" [17] that 'further progress is needed on implementing measures to facilitate SME access to capital and, more broadly, to support the development of a market for ICS in Senegal'. The promotion and commercialization of the largely informal ICS sector in the country, the project will make a direct contribution to this national policy priority.


[17] http://www.bridges-database.org/DocumentAnalysisReport-2018-2022.pdf page 40.

E.5.2 Capacity of accredited entities and executing entities to deliver

### ESDv2

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is committed to supporting sustainable development and technical assistance on climate change worldwide. More than 204 million people around the world have gained access to sustainable and climate-friendly energy services through the Energising Development Programme initiated by Germany and the World Bank.

#### 234. The multi-donor Energising Development Programme (EDev) is an energy partnership programme funded mainly by $6 donor countries and co-managed by RVO and GIZ. It promotes sustainable access to modern, affordable, reliable, environmentally sound and efficient energy services in developing countries with low economic, social or/and environmental impacts. Target groups of EDev are poor households, social institutions and small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs). The programme supports the development of off-grid technologies and products, including improved cookstoves and solar lanterns. By mid-2017, EDev covered 35 countries with 49 projects in operation. In addition to providing financial support for projects, EDev also provides technical assistance and capacity building. As at end 2017, projects concern the support to improving cooking systems. Further details on all EDev experience and results in Africa are available in the GCF Annual Report.

#### 235. There is a good gender balance in staffing within the GIZ project teams and several staff members are trained in gender mainstreaming. The project teams have a very good track record of engaging with both gender equality focal points (GEFPs) at headquarters level as well as with national governments, where this is qualified staff available for support at the headquarters level. The National Gender and Equality Commission (NGEC) has been engaged by GIZ in Senegal to provide advice on gender-sensitive budgeting, and these recommendations have been included in the proposal.

#### 236. Several of the project partners (EAs and implementing partners) have significant capacity in implementing gender projects at grassroots level. These can complement GIZ by bringing in significant experience and knowledge from their work with local communities.

##### EAS management capacity:

The EAS management capacity of the EDev project teams is good. The teams are not yet trained for EAS management, but have been shown to adequately follow up on relevant activities and provide feedback to the headquarters team. A training session will be provided in the near future, and additional training on EAS will be provided during the project. Project partners are subject to due diligence, which includes an assessment of their EAS management capacity.

#### 235. Senegal: The NDA of Senegal has been closely involved in the discussion and preparation for the project and provide strategic guidance into the direction and direction of the project, in particular its focus on ICS sector development. Throughout project development process a number of meetings have been held with the NDA and other key stakeholders including the Ministry of Energy and Mines (MEM). Such meetings have been closely supporting the project with human resources. Feedback from the NDA and other departments was provided to the GCF Secretariat in the form of comments on the draft project design, which were submitted by 12 October 2018. The Ministry of Energy and Power has been the closest implementing partner for EDev projects in Senegal. It is also a key stakeholder in the proposed project. As part of the GCF LSP Project A series of stakeholder consultations was held during the project design phase, as listed in the Table 1 below. In addition to these meetings, other national stakeholders will remain a key element of the activities on awareness raising and the enabling environment.

## Stakeholder engagement plan

### 242. The regular stakeholder engagement and consultation practice of the 'baseline' projects will also be set forth in the project documents. This will ensure that the GCF Fund is able to effectively engage with the engagement of the national institutional stakeholders. The Project Coordination Committees (PCCs), which are responsible for the implementation of the baseline projects, will continue their role as focal points for knowledge exchange on a global level, which are the focus of Component 3. This will allow for engagement with international organizations, including financial institutions, with producers and with other LMEs. This is embedded into the project activities in the form of regular consultations with stakeholders.

### 243. Formal gender-sensitive consultations will be held to ensure that gender needs are featured in all the project components, including awareness raising, marketing, product design, and production methods.

### Project. The project includes a double grievance mechanism: a private sector complaints mechanism where private sector complaints are regularly collected by the EAs during their consultations of beneficiaries; or an environmental and social grievances mechanism for supported business scale producers (see ESMP).

## E.6. Efficiency and Effectiveness

### Economic and/or financial soundness of the project/programme

#### 245. The proposed GCF project has been designed to ensure minimum concessionality of GCF such that GCF grant funding is only devoted to support viable climate initiatives that cannot currently be financed through non-grant instruments.

### Macro-economic context

#### Overall level of indebtedness: On a macro-economic scale, Senegal is at least developed country (LDC) and Kenya is low middle-income country. Both countries have reached or surpassed the level of external indebtedness considered prudent for developing countries (see Table 4.2). In the case of Senegal and public finance management, the government has been implementing a debt reduction programme that was approved by the respective Finance Ministries and figure as part of the annual public budget.

#### Private Equity: Private equity and investment are currently popular in some Sub-Saharan countries, particularly in Kenya. Socially responsible investment funds have also targeted activities in Sub-Saharan Africa.

**Page 83**
---

# EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

## GREEN FUNDING FUND PROPOSAL | PAGE 75 OF 120

However, given the total scale of the required investments[1] in both countries (on average less than EUR 3 mln per country) and the small, informal nature of most ICS producers, the ICS sector is unlikely to garner the attention of these entities at least until they have reached the professional stage at the end of CCF phase.

### Subcomponent 1

249. The current financing gap in the ICS sector in Senegal and Kenya is described below:

250. Modest Financial Returns at Project Level: GIZ has conducted extensive studies in the ICS sector in both Senegal and Kenya. During project development, financial analyses were conducted with different classes of ICS producers based on required capital investments. The average IRR indicators for 4 different producer categories are shown below. These indicate that the returns from ICS production are sufficient to finance debt or other market instruments because IRR is inferior to the prevailing market rate.

| Country | Category | Professional | Artisan |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Baseline | FIRR | 5% | 2% |

251. Debt market conditions in target countries: The regulated (commercial) banking sector currently does not extend debt financing to ICS sectors due to the lack of collateral requirements, and perceived risk. Even if the ICS producers possessed the required documentation and registered assets, the debt market for similar sized capital investments starts at least 13% (Kenya) and 17-24% (Senegal). The debt market in both countries is also characterized by significantly higher interest rates of up to 22% depending on risk (see Section C.2 for a detailed overview of financial markets in both countries).

252. Increase (via up-front subsidy) the cash flow of the proposed capital investment projects such that the IRR either equals or surpasses the rate of the debt market. This approach works to increase the attractiveness of these investments and, given other factors affecting the banking sector's view of the ICS sector, is unlikely to generate much interest.

253. The strategy to be pursued is one of increased market and competition to distort the market for the shortest time possible to a break-even point; i.e., the point at which ICS sales are sufficient to grow profits which can sustain itself in the medium-term. NDC targets for ICS are not likely to be met by the supported enterprises by external sources to reach the production goals of the project. The conclusion is echoed in, for example, the 2018 report on Accelerating Energy Access in Africa: “The lack of access to finance remains a major barrier to scaling up clean energy solutions for companies trying to raise capital; less than USD 2 mln in total equity was invested in clean cookstoves companies in 2017.” (https://documents.un.org/UNRESOLUTIONS/Accelerating Access/Roles of Patent Capital Report.pdf)

## EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

### Sub-component 2

#### 254. At the end of the GCF project, it is expected that a subset of the ICS producers that have professionalized will successfully access the finance sector independently of donor support.

#### 255. TA sub-component 2 has also been designed to maximize efficiency of GCF grant for awareness raising and knowledge sharing purposes. It is important to emphasize the differences in the nature of costs associated with creating markets for small and micro-scale low-carbon technologies, as opposed to large-scale low-carbon technologies.

#### 256. Whilst for large-scale technologies the largest category of costs is capital investment, for the small and especially micro-scale technologies such as ICS, the largest category of costs “area from the information processing and dissemination” (IPD) costs. These are estimated at EUR 17.8 million in Kenya and EUR 20.5 million in Sweden. On-treatment costs for low-carbon technologies in developed countries[34]) For example, the efficiency of heat pumps has been found to be significantly higher than that of other heating systems (up to 30%) for Swedish families seeking to improve the energy efficiency of their heating systems back in early 2015. These experiences have been learnt by other developed countries when promoting markets for small-scale EE and I solutions.

#### 257. Kenyan and Senegalese consumers face significantly higher cost of obtaining information about an ICS and associated technologies compared to the costs faced by their counterparts in developed countries. This is because there will be a significant number who will be involved in outreach, as well as the targeted number of households, 1.91 million households under sub-component 2.0.

264. Without GCF Support The financial viability of the four ICS producer classes in the absence of GCF support is poor. The cash flow obtained from sales does not cover equipment investment. What support the producers do receive tends to be directed towards increasing marketing and distribution actions as opposed to production, which is a major concern for the future sustainability of the project. This is clearly evident in Kenya. It is essentially a stagnant situation with an increase of sales of 5% per year (Kenya) and 1.5% per year (Senegal) and limited capital investment.

265. For Kenya, the average FIRR is between 5 and 2%, which indicates very modest returns. In Senegal, capital investments in the artisan category is positive in the BAU case. For the professional class, there is a destruction of value since the BAU anticipates general under-investment and postponing of significant market entry. The GCF support to the professional class in Senegal is expected to be very beneficial as continued (at lower amounts). Investments in safety equipment, work wear and tools are also entirely eliminated from the BAU scenario.

scenarios for this category is -12%. Outside support for investment is clearly needed in order to reach the next sustainable trajectory.

### With GCF Support:

The financial viability of the four ICS producer classes improves with the introduction of GCF financing. The results range from 9% to 15% FIRR. The designated capital expenditures are implemented through a combination of direct and indirect investments into the production network. It is important to note that, though these FIRR results are positive, they are modest and do not allow the ICS producers to achieve their full potential. This analysis considers only cost-effective measures from a purely financial point of view. This sets the stage for the technical assistance and professionalization activities that will improve the ICS producers, increase productivity, and lead to a subset of producers that are able to formulate and implement an export-oriented production strategy in order to access the respective markets.

### Sensitivity analysis:

A sensitivity analysis with GCF upfront scenarios was conducted on the key investment assumptions at the ICS producer level in Kenya and Benin. The main risks concern the robustness of the up-front investments on the national scale in each country. The proposed capital investments will vary based on individual ICS producers' arrangements and geographic coverage. Sensitivity tests were therefore conducted to determine the sensitivity and robustness for the three scenarios, to examine the impact of these variations. In addition, the impact of cost of production and cost of sales variations were also assessed.

The following observations can be made concerning the sensitivity analysis:
- Generally speaking, the FIRR estimates are more volatile to changes in different inputs. This is particularly true for changes in sales and cost of production (positive or negative) as well as capital expenditures (Capex, Sales revenue, cost of production, and cost of sales) while taxes are minor and defined by law.
- FIRR calculations are most sensitive to changes in sales and costs of production. Variations in the capital expenditure and sales revenue have a significant impact on the FIRR estimates. The most significant sensitivity impact is the combination of a decrease in sales and increase in capital expenditures (negative outlook).
- EGAPs are also more volatile to changes in different inputs. Partly this is due to two factors: (the addition of economic value of GHG emissions reductions) to the four inputs used in the FIRR calculation, and the fact that the economic value of GHG emission reductions are very large and either equal or outweigh most of the other factors in value.

### E.6.4. Application of best practices

#### Lesson learnt: Emphasis on private sector and commercial market development as opposed to subsidizing the provision of ICS to consumers.

Working towards commercial markets is a constitutional part of EnDev's approach in the ICS sector. It does so through (i) interventions to strengthen the ICS products and supply chain, (ii) supporting the development of standards, and (iii) consumer subsidies for ICS. The latter have proven less effective in growing ICS markets than supply chain strengthening and market development.

In 2006 with a small intervention focusing on a rural target group. Soon it became clear that this approach was not intended to be sustainable as the project was not able to generate sufficient demand to sustain the achieved level of production and continue growth. In recent years, manufacturing has become more commercialized in terms of product development, marketing, branding, distribution, and public awareness. Quality control is addressed using production tools (e.g., moulds/dies) which reduce the risk of producing substandard products. The supply chain is managed by a number of large-scale cookstove manufacturers (or producer groups) selling their products to commercial markets.

#### Lesson learnt: Substantial technical assistance is required to jump start ICS market and accelerate its growth. Due to the nascent stage of the ICS supply chain, producers and other entities involved in manufacturing and marketing are still learning about the technology. This involves business development aspects starting from basic planning and financial advice to technical support with design, production, quality control, branding, distribution, and public awareness and preferences. This also requires market intelligence and understanding of consumers' preferences and needs.

For example, the first phase of the project in Malawi required EUR 16 million to establish manufacturing systems based on 'local production with local materials for local markets and initial annual production of less than 500 ICS. The second phase required EUR 8.2 million to increase production capacity through professional marketing, formal sector retailing and specific pro-poor approaches and annual sales of 85,000.

The total cost of the programme was EUR 13.5 million. This translates into EUR 16 per unit sold for the 5-year cumulative market volume. However, this example also demonstrates how the need for TA decreases as the project moves from the initial phase to the second and third phases. The first phase in Malawi required EUR 8.2 min to reach the annual production volume of 13,000 ICS, while the second phase required EUR 5 million to increase this output to 47,000 units per year. This shows that as the supply chain matures and becomes more commercialized, the need for technical assistance decreases.

#### Lesson learnt: Supply-chain targeting grants using result-based approaches are proving to be effective instruments to leverage private sector investments, laying the foundation for graduation to full-cost financing, and reducing barriers and risks for those enterprises on the cusp of investing in the ICS market. This can be an effective way to grow the various stages of the supply-market chain for ICS.

### EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 85 OF 120

Experience with result-based approaches shows that it has been effective as an incentive for suppliers to increase their inventory, incorporate more ambition in their marketing outreach, to invest in new distribution infrastructure, and to reach out to new geographic regions or target groups. The advantages of this approach are the ability to design a tailored support package for each market segment and the fact that a result-based support package can be designed to specifically target certain 'under-performing' market segments (e.g. remote areas). For example, in Tanzania, a result-based scheme was implemented to address bottlenecks at the end of the rural distribution chain. The scheme aimed to increase the number of retail outlets and improve their performance by strengthening distribution from the suppliers to the end retailers in the comparatively underserved rural areas of the far north of the country. As a result, 15 companies and 23 sales-hub employees were trained on improved customer supply services within one year with an engagement rate of 90%. The resulting ratio across its portfolio is in the range of 1:3.6; while in certain circumstances it can go higher up to 1:6.

#### 273.
A transparent system of auditing and verification is an essential element of supply-chain interventions. EnDev has developed a robust MRV system for the ICS market (see Section H.2), as has been described above, which enables the achievement of ambitious monitoring and reporting targets while meeting monitoring and impact evaluation ambitions. Their outcome monitoring system is very elaborate and enables the generation of detailed data on the number of beneficiaries per project, the number of direct and total beneficiaries for each country and technology and it is thereby able to calculate cost per beneficiary for each EnDev sub-intervention.

## Specific Structure

The Financial Model concentrates primarily on physical or capital investments undertaken with the assistance of GCF funding. It is assumed that substantial technical assistance will also occur during the GCF project including training to producers to use the equipment and to prepare bankable business plans. The project includes a number of activities aimed at increasing market access for ICS products, in particular to reach "bankability" and distributors, in particular LMEs, which is outside the financial model's scope. In parallel, training in marketing and sales will be provided to meet the needs and dynamics of the ICS markets in each country. The value of these investments is not modeled.

### Scenarios

#### w/ Without GCF Support

The largely inertia (ICS sector functions on a cash basis and most small producers still finance most investments through capital expenditures or working capital). Therefore, barring any external support, the 'business as usual' scenario consists primarily of under investment and limited growth of sales existing models. Given the precarious and informal nature of the sector, it could even contract without support.

Average Capital Investment: Approximately 10-25% of needed investments

Growth of Sales: 1.5 - 5%

#### w/ With GCF Support

With GCF support, substantial up-front subsidies are used to partially finance the capital investments supported by EnDev. The investments take place over 10 years period between 2020 and 2030, including with GCF support. These investments (and associated technical assistance) lead to significant increases in sales of ICS stoves. However, earlier financial analysis indicate that their impact on the sector is limited by the fact that they are not able to address the capital side of the issue (importantly, the capital investments and (associated technical assistance) in this scenario initiate the paradigm shift in the sector). The investments will be made at the next level of production, increases productivity, and are able to address the financial sector independently.

Average Capital Investment (10 yrs): EUR 15,000 – 85,000 depending on category

Growth of Sales: Significant increase

### F.2 - Technical Evaluation

ICS will be promoted by the project in both countries, including key technical and economic characteristics is provided in the tables below. More information and technical details can be found in the annexed Feasibility Study.

#### Kenya

The heavy reliance on solid biomass in Kenya implies that most of the cooking technologies in use are biomass stoves. These include traditional biomass cookstoves (i.e., three-stone fire and traditional metallic charcoal stoves) and improved biomass cookstoves (different varieties for both charcoal and fuel). In the Kenyan context, there is a wide variety of imported and locally manufactured biomass cookstoves, which range from artisanal and semi-industrial to industrial and imported products and include charcoal stoves as well as firewood stoves. Their thermal efficiency ranges from 20 to 45%, and their price ranges EUR 13 to 90.

### Project Description

284. In the past EnDev has focused on rural areas, which show a high modern energy access deficit. Therefore, EnDev supported the value chain of firewood ICS, like the Jiko Kissa and the Rocket Stove. These stoves have in a first step been designed for charcoal production (Jiko Kissa) or for cooking (Rocket stove). The latter are more expensive (stone fire or simple mud ring stoves), which they replace. These stoves are produced locally in artisanal or semi-industrial manner and therefore have an affordable price ranging from EUR 5 to 30. These models have proven to be appreciated by the rural population, especially women.

285. The projects intend to continue working with these stove models. However, as there is increasing demand for charcoal stoves and currently no affordable charcoal ICS on the market, the project will also support locally designed and manufactured charcoal ICS for charcoal production at an affordable price of EUR 10 or less. Locally designed and produced ICS for charcoal, at an affordable price of EUR 5 to 10. Other products like the industrial stoves (Jiko Kissa) are not suitable for charcoal production.

286. The project will support the development of locally manufactured charcoal ICS in rural areas. This will promote an improved charcoal stove, which fills the gap between JKI and the industrial product, with an affordable price. These stoves can be produced by local artisans or small industries. They are suitable for universities and official testing centres and thereafter will be made available to the market. # APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 92 OF 120

The project has major positive environmental and social impacts, as described in the relevant sections of the proposal. This section addresses potential unintended negative impacts of the project, as well as opportunities for mitigation.

### Environmental Management

#### The E&S management capacity of the local EndEv team is good.
The teams are not yet trained for E&S management, but have been shown to adequately follow up on environmental and social issues. Project partners are subject matter experts in their respective areas and have a good understanding of E&S issues. Local teams also have a very good track record of ongoing stakeholder engagement in the "baseline projects". The GCF project design builds on extended consultation with stakeholders and provides for participation of all groups (see Section 6.5).

#### Residual environmental and social risks are low, and will be well as opportunities, will be managed through an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) and/or other measures in the areas.
The following sections provide the rationale for the definition of measures included in the ESMP.

### Environmental Impacts and Nuisances from Stove Production

#### The production of ICS is a relatively clean process. Environmental impacts and risks have to be managed in the production of ICS, and only when this production reaches a certain scale.
Individual producers have no E&S impact on their own. However, there are potential environmental and social risks associated with the use of mechanised workshops/factories (of which only two currently exist) may cause limited nuisances and pollution due to the use of mechanical equipment.

#### Improved cookstoves hardly generate any waste, since the ceramic liners are made only of clay and sand and can be reused. The metal from discarded stoves is recycled into new line production.
Metal waste in production is minimal and is recycled; metal from discarded stoves is also recycled. Paint is expensive and is currently always used to the last drop. However, there may be some paint spills during painting operations. There are potential environmental and mechanical equipment, as well as paint residue or cleaning solvents from the use of spray painters.
To mitigate potential risks, the project will ensure that all producers have access to ESS training and will provide scale production, among others to correctly handle hazardous products.

#### Quantities of primary materials such as clay and sand used for stove production are negligible compared to any conceivable environmental impact. The limited quantities of metal waste generated also have a limited impact locally on rivers.
The project will promote the use of materials from legally approved sources, and in case of local sourcing (in Kenya only) may require producers to obtain permits.

### Labour and working conditions, including occupational health and safety

#### For workers directly engaged by the project partners, GIZ and SNF are fully compliant with the GCF's standard ESS 2015. However, there is a risk of potential non-compliance in areas not identified potential non-compliances requiring mitigation, expect potential safety issues in Kenya (see below).
The project includes measures to guarantee compliance with the other paragraphs of ESS health and safety, complete training for all workers on health and safety.

#### The supported private ICS production centre will employ a limited number of employees. Full compliance with paragraph 24 of ES2 is not possible in the informal economic sectors in Kenya and Senegal, which still concerns the majority of the workers in these countries.
However, in the baseline projects, the constant monitoring of producers stimulates them to improve their practices. The results are that the health and safety conditions for workers are better than in sectors not supported by the project. The GCF project will set forward this practice and reinforce it by means of training and awareness raising activities.

### Potential land requirements

297. Thereby the project will create significant opportunities for enhancing labour conditions and operational health and safety of smallholder producers in Kenya and Senegal, and will contribute to shift larger producers towards formalisation and professionalisation.

298. Any land acquisition required for the expansion of production sites, or the creation of new production sites for smallholders, when the land acquisition is financed by the producers themselves, will have to be managed in compliance with ESS1 on land acquisition and resettlement. GIZ/Dev staff will be trained on identifying potential conflicts of interest and managing them.

### Indigenous peoples

299. Indigenous peoples are present both in Kenya and in Senegal. The project is expected to have an overall positive impact for indigenous communities, since it will contribute to the preservation of natural resources, which are often vital to indigenous peoples, and reduce conflict potentials over these resources. Marketing and sales activities are not expected to negatively affect indigenous peoples directly as they do not involve direct contact with producers or processors. This is not expected to affect indigenous peoples negatively, or affect their culture, knowledge, or practices in any significant way.

### Financial intermediation

300. The GCF requires financial intermediaries to be screened in conformity with their E&S policy. Potential financial intermediaries will be required to undergo a screening process at the project level (E&S1), and at the lowest level of risk: 1) when an intermediary’s existing or proposed portfolio includes financial exposure to activities not projected by the project, but which are considered high-risk; 2) when an intermediary is providing financing only for E&S management capacity (standard ESS1) and labour conditions (standard ESS3). A mechanism is included in the project to screen all FIs who may get involved at a later stage, and review their E&S management capacity annually.

### GIZ Human Rights safeguards

301. The project was screened concerning GIZ’s human rights safeguards. The screening was based on the same document as for the assessment of the project concept (E&S2 [labour], ESS5 [involuntary resettlement], ESS7 [indigenous peoples] and prevention of discrimination and right to participation (both included under ESS1). This information will be updated annually during the consideration of the project activities.

### GIZ Conflict and Context Sensitivity safeguards

302. GIZ’s safeguard system requires an analysis of conflict potential and context sensitivity for Kenya. Kenya is a highly polarised country with a history of ethnic violence, political instability, and armed conflict. Kenya has been building up a solid legal framework since 2010, risks may be fuelled by unequal access to resources, impunity within the security sector, lack of justice, corruption, and weak institutions. The project will not contribute directly to affluence or asset for political purposes, abuse of political finance, land conflicts, non-recognition of indigenous land rights, and other forms of discrimination. However, there is a risk that the project could exacerbate poverty, youth unemployment, radicalisation (and transnational) extremist groups, proliferation of weapons, presence of armed militia in rural areas, and increased competition for resources, including water, which may be leading to violence due to exasperation of the pressure on pastures and availability of weapons.

303. In this context, GIZ will manage employee security through their corporate system and require partner organisations to have a conflict sensitivity plan in Kenya, which are particularly at risk. The project will use experience of local EEs in risk-prone areas.

304. Since violent attacks have occurred in the sand extraction sector, producers in Kenya will be required to shift as much as possible to using sand from legal sources. In Kenya, the project will further avoid cases where it could

**Project risk categorisation**

305. The screening did not identify other potential negative impacts for the project. In GIZ’s Safeguarda and Gender management systems, the project is automatically categorised as all under ‘the Conflict and Context Sensitivity’ safeguarda for Kenya. From an environmental and social point of view and according to the GCF’s categorisation, the project is considered a Category 1 activity. The project has been assessed against the GCF’s safeguards, which covers two entire countries, the human rights context of the countries, as well as the number of partners involved, makes it a Category 1 activity under the GCF’s environmental and social safeguarda, and the project is therefore categorised if as a precaution, with implementation of a full ESM plan management.

## Environmental and social management plan (ESMP)

307. The ESMP includes all the environmental enhancement measures defined above are included in the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) for the project. The ESMP has been budgeted in the overall project budget.

308. Assessment of the baseline projects has shown that the project teams currently exert a very close monitoring of beneficiaries and of supported ICS producers, and more generally of project ESM impacts. Up to now, this practice has proven to be effective. For example, in one of the pilot projects in Kenya, a producer complained about noise from a producer from a neighbour when the producer adapted working hours after consultation with the community. In another pilot project in Kenya, a producer complained that his land had been solved by technical adaptation of the kin. The project teams also have a good track record of managing land acquisition issues by engaging with the relevant stakeholders.

309. The ESMP for the project builds forward upon this successful practice of close monitoring and consultation, by further strengthening it in several ways:

- appointing and training dedicated national and regional EAS staff for the project coordinating E&S management
- regularly monitoring E&S aspects;
- negotiating agreements with communities and producers for affected people and for workers implementing a project grievance redress mechanism; and
- having all supported producers adhere to environmental and social management guidelines requiring that all supported business-scale producers develop and implement a producer ESMP.

## Gender impacts and gender mainstreaming

310. The gender assessment carried out for the project shows that the project has major positive impacts on gender equality, including providing housing for women and providing opportunities for women to engage in paid work. The project has no potential negative gender impacts.

311. Surveys of gender impacts of the baseline projects were available for each country and extensive consultations have been carried out with women’s organisations in both countries to inform the design of the programme. The most

significant outcomes of these consultations are that women still feel less confident than men to engage in entrepreneurship and business activities, and that they lack formal education, skills for income generation, and keeping records. This lack of skills and confidence hampers their access to finance, to available assistance programmes, and to procurement opportunities.

312. A Gender Action Plan has been developed for the project, where specific training of women is an important component. The gender focal person will be appointed in the project and should verify that women benefit from equal work opportunities, and equal incomes for equal work.

313. The gender mainstreaming capability of the proposed project organisation is good, with several implementing partners having a strong commitment to gender issues at the grassroots level and at all regions of Senegal and counties of Kenya. A GIZ gender focal person will be established for each country and each regional administration, ministry or NGO. This will ensure that women are included in all aspects of the project and contribute to improving the institutional framework for gender mainstreaming in Kenya and Senegal by disseminating best practices.

314. Regarding other special interest groups, the project generates significant employment opportunities for youth. The ease of use offered by GIS is particularly important for persons with disabilities or with HIV. The GAP will also force a change in attitudes towards women.

315. Domestic violence is a significant issue in both Senegal and Kenya. The baseline projects have shown that the project has rather a peace-building effect in the households. Organisations such as JOYWO and SNV have significant experience in this field, and their involvement will be essential for project implementation. It is also advised to investigate the conflict potential at the household level for new project regulations. Involvement of women in decision-making in projects activities, and gender-differentiated information on the impact of the project are important.

# F.4. Financial Management and Procurement

## 316. Procurement of financial management will be implemented as follows (in line with the general arrangements as described in Section C.7 and Annex 18):
- The Executing Entities – Ministry of Energy and SNV NL, as well as the five regional NGOs in Senegal under a consultancy agreement with GIZ, based on GIZ standard operating procedures for contracts for financing.
- Contracts for financial management will be awarded to suppliers or service providers having available to the Executing Entities for specific purposes to help them carry out certain measures.
- The respective Executing Entities are responsible for implementing and administering the measures in accordance with GIZ standard operating procedures.

## 317. In case of procurement by GIZ, GIZ will follow its own procurement guidelines. GIZ is required to comply with the relevant contracting rules as established in the German Act against Restraints of Competition (GWB), the German Regulation on the Award of Public Contracts (VgVG) and, if applicable, the Contracting Rules for the Award of Public Contracts (VgVG-Verordnung). GIZ will ensure that all procurement processes are transparent. When awarding contracts for supplies and services (including consultancy services) to be financed in full or in part from the contribution provided by the GCF, GIZ will follow its own procurement guidelines as described above. An overview of these minimum standards is available at <https://www.giz.de/en/procurement.html>. GIZ assesses adherence of submitted procurement documents to GIZ procurement regulations at defined stages in this process.

## 318. The financial management of the project will follow GIZ's internal rules and regulations. GIZ has bank accounts with both Deutsche Bank and Commerzbank. GIZ will not open a specific bank account for GCF proceeds and other GCF funds but will ensure that all funds provided are clearly identifiable from GIZ’s other funds by setting up separate sub-accounts in the respective bank accounts. All receipts of GCF funds, including interest income received and expenditures incurred will be booked to the respective cost unit according to generally accepted accounting principles (GAAP). The Executing Entities will apply the same principle. GIZ discloses funds to the recipients in accordance with the progress of the project. The Executing Entities have to prove the proper use of funds and the defined progress as a prerequisite for any further disbursement.

## 319. Independent external auditors will perform annual financial audits of the project in line with International Auditing Standards.

# G.1 Risk Assessment Summary

320. Informed by EnDev previous work on ICS market promotion in targeted countries and worldwide, the project does not expect that there will be any significant barriers to achieving the achievement of the project objective. Key market and financial risks are related to the likelihood of securing cash co-financing from ICS producers. The inability to secure this co-financing would have a high impact on the project prospects to accelerate ICS market growth in the countries. The co-financing will be provided by the enterprises themselves, and the project will not provide any direct financial assistance as it would be delivered in stages subject to ICS meeting certain obligations, including co-financing. At this stage of a cost recovery payment plan has been developed for the enterprises that cannot fund projects in order not to jeopardise their ability and ensure sufficient working capital for the enterprise.

321. The other category of risk is related to project implementation: first, potential delays at the start-up phase and second, the need to ensure coordination, as well as timely and effective delivery of inputs by project's many implementing partners.

322. Based on conducted EAS assessment, only minor environmental and social risks have been identified. However, due to the large geographical scope of the project and the conflict sensitivity context in Kenya, the project has been developed with a risk management plan has been developed to mitigate the risk, as summarised in Section F.3 of the proposal.

323. Lastly, there is a potential risk of market distortion by customer sub-programmes or other unexpected changes in market behaviour which may have to be monitored so "driving forces" will not all evolve exactly.

# G.2 Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures

## Selected Risk Factor 1: Co-financing from ICS Producers

| Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Financial | High (>20% of project value) | Medium |

The project will require ICS contributors to provide the costs of professionalisation kits with at least 25%, and the enterprises will be required to pay their contributions in full up-front due to limitations on their cash flow.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The approach to risk mitigation in this case is two-fold. First, the project's assistance package: the professionalisation kits will be provided by the project. In addition, the project will provide results by the recipient of support. Second, in order to consider the cash flow of the enterprises, the project may consider allowing entrepreneurs to pay their own contributions over a period of time (e.g., 6 months). This would allow the entrepreneur to continue operating and freeze the access of the supported enterprise to support until payments are made. This way, the project will lower the probability of risk occurring as this project would not be able to operate if cash flow was not material.

## Selected Risk Factor 2: Inertia of new rural and more remote ICS markets

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The project is planning to focus on new and more remote markets in Kenya, Tanzania, and Benin. In Kenya, the project may venture into more urban markets than it has historically worked with (e.g., Nairobi). In Senegal has not supported rural markets beyond the project's current scope. If the project were to be drastically expanding the geographic scope although they already work with rural markets | Other | High (>20% of project value) | Medium |

There is the risk that even with new approaches, the ICS model for rural households will not pick up at scale and the rate expected.

| Mitigation Measure(s) | Description |
| --- | --- |
| The project will scale up its distribution concept in Senegal, which will involve young people and women from rural villages in the commercial distribution of the ICS. This activity will close the gap between the clients and the ICS-trading partners. In addition to this, as part of Component 2, the project will also sensitize the population on a broad scale to the importance of ICS using new methods such as radio, TV, posters, etc., and through community meetings. The project will monitor and review if the new approaches are really helping to influence the rural population. An international research consultant will be hired in Senegal to conduct an impact assessment study. This consultant will provide their recommendations on how to bridge between rural market traders and households in the villages. Their recommendations will guide the programme on how to improve and maximise the impact of this project on the rural ICS market development. |

## Selected Risk Factor 3: Slow start-up

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Experience has shown that some projects take significant time due to the start-up phase because of lengthy procurement processes and the potential impact of delaying the project, which could hamper delivery of results within the specified period. | Technical and operational | Medium (5-10% of project value) | Low/Low |

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The GCF project is embedded into on-going ICS projects in Kenya and Senegal where the core project implementation structure is already in place, including the selection and assessment of executing entities, extensive stakeholder engagement, and the execution of key project partners. Therefore, the preparation and inception process is expected to be minimal, meaning that the project will become operational much sooner than if a completely new structure would need to set up.

## Selected Risk Factor 4: Executing Entities (EE) risks

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The project has a complex implementation structure. To ensure intended impact on the ground, strong and reliable implementing organisations are essential. The project will work via a number pre-selected executing entities (EE) in both Kenya and Senegal with varying implementation financial, technical, and operational capacity. Working through new implementation partners creates disruption of the project success on their performance. If one or even several of them do not perform as expected within the planned timeframe, the project results will be affected. | Technical and operational | High (>20% of project value) | Medium |

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The selection of the EE has been based on a competitive call for Expression of Interest. A range of categories has evaluated applying NGOs, including their historic track record, their physical presence in the country, their network of collaborators, and their experience with similar projects. The EE are all organisations that have already collaborated with EnDev or are known from their work in the country. For early detection of mal-

## Mitigation Measure(s)

The minor risks will be managed through the mitigation ESMP by regularly monitoring E&S aspects, training producers and implementing appropriate environmental and social management measures for producers receiving financial support.

By implementing this E&S management plan, the project will create significant opportunities for enhancing labour conditions in ICS production centres. The project will ensure that all ICS production centres are operating entirely compliant with national regulations. See Section F.3 for detailed description of E&S risks identified and proposed mitigation measures.

## Other Potential Risks in the Horizon

As part of the project, the producers of ICS will be selling their products at full market price to their clients. Direct end-consumer sales through retail outlets or supermarkets would be implemented significantly earlier if a large-scale programme would distribute ICS at a highly-subsidised level. This currently seems unlikely either in Kenya (where there is no significant retail sector) or Senegal (where the retail sector is still in its infancy stages).

In both Kenya and Senegal as well as within its global component, the project will support coordination efforts to ensure that the ICS market can be developed in a sustainable manner. The project will also do so by strengthening the capacities of national agencies in charge of the ICS market to coordinate work of the various stakeholders involved (producers, traders, retailers, consumers and other actors). This approach, which can deliver high market growth rates without consumer subsidies and other market distortion mechanisms.

Another mitigation mechanism is the work through the EE and the partnering NGOs. By using a network of organisations on the ground in large parts of the country, the evolution of such a subsidised competition will potentially be detected at a very early stage, hence allowing for discussions and mitigation before larger damage has occurred.

#### 1.1.1 Professionalization of ICS production
**Activity:** Professionalization processes and quality of ICS and ICS production centres, including artisanal and professional producer cooperatives, to ensure expansion and thus moving up on the value chain.
* Needs assessment of all production centres
* Provision of professionalization Kits and equipment (e.g. mechanization, equipping, and training) for 100 production centres
* Initial start-up investments
* Provision of performance based incentives and investment packages (e.g. further follow up performance payments)
* Further follow up performance payments
* Technical trainings on operation of mechanization & equipping equipment
* Establishment of store production centres on technology, materials, production space, work flow, quality control, and dedicated trainings for women
* Entrepreneurship/business training with consideration of specific training needs
* Regular stove testing to monitor progress in artisanal production centres and households (supervised by ARKOS)
* Quality assurance via technical training, testing and quality control

# H.2 Arrangements for Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation

## The EnDev programme has a 10-year track record in monitoring energy access interventions, including impacts (such as CO₂, gender, employment). EnDev's monitoring system provides donors and partners with realistic and practical information on the performance of projects and programmes. It is based on the premise that households and household members are the foundation for adaptive programme and project management. EnDev's monitoring system is designed to provide a clear picture of the impact of its interventions by tracking a variety of projects into a few meaningful figures to show its relevance with regard to the SDGs, in particular SGD 7. The monitoring is rather simple and relies on a combination of counting results to ensure credibility vis-à-vis the donor and reporting to them after appropriate validation and transparency system and a good biannual reporting quick and to the point financial monitoring, and prompt reporting on special requests.

### At the country level, the country project manager will be responsible for ensuring that monitoring is aligned with the GCF requirements. The EnDev monitoring system is based on a combination of quantitative and qualitative methods used are method and data will be stored in a secure and retrievable way. The basic building blocks of EnDev's monitoring system are: (a) monthly sales sheets, (b) quarterly market research surveys, (c) regular store visits to monitor cycles in every semester (c) roles and responsibilities describing who does what (d) knowledge management and reporting.

### Under this proposal, EnDev will monitor stove sales along the distribution chain on a monthly/quarterly basis. Sales will be registered on a sales sheet or using digital tools, and stove vendors will be trained in their use. Monthly sales sheets will be submitted to EnDev regional offices for validation. The sales sheets include names and addresses of each individual customer (it is time-consuming and inconvenient for the customers). EnDev will be able to provide an estimate of the number of stoves sold per month by region, based on the data collected at the regional distribution of stove owners). Stove usage will be assessed through impact studies in selected samples and in discussion with stove users. The EnDev monitoring system is designed to provide a clear picture of the impact of using stoves can express their observations and wishes towards stove performance and features. From EnDev experience, it has been found that stove users are more likely to recommend stoves to others if they have used repeat customers. Other instruments like set-up of a database driven by mobile-phone based customer self-registration will be tested to further advance knowledge about the customer base.

### Monthly sales sheets will then be communicated to EnDev regional offices. Monitoring officers will revise the sales data and cross-check it with other sources such as production records, stock checks and random spot checks. After accepting the data, they will be compiled and transmitted to the EnDev country office, which performs another set of verification before submitting them to the GCF for validation. The EnDev monitoring system is designed to provide an estimate of the number of stoves sold per month by region, based on the data collected at the regional distribution of stove owners). Stove usage will be assessed through impact studies in selected samples and in discussion with stove users. The EnDev monitoring system is designed to provide a clear picture of the impact of using stoves can express their observations and wishes towards stove performance and features. From EnDev experience, it has been found that stove users are more likely to recommend stoves to others if they have used repeat customers. Other instruments like set-up of a database driven by mobile-phone based customer self-registration will be tested to further advance knowledge about the customer base.

### The MRV system is designed to deliver results of additional sales above the baseline. On a monthly basis the producers will be visited and the production and sales will be assessed and compared with the sales of the past. In the case where the sales are higher than expected, the EnDev country office will inform the GCF of project's investment in professionalization kits, investment packages and T&A for accelerated production and sales. These investments can be counted as additional to the baseline or business as usual scenario.

### The number of ICS is used determines the number of people benefiting from the ICS (e.g., if 1ICS average household size is two, then one ICS per two households on average) are always using it at the same time parallel when preparing a meal, hence the effect of a single ICS is only applicable for half of the household size. The ICS promoted by EnDev have a limited life span

# RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FINANCING PROPOSAL | PAGE 118 OF 120


Only projects which are still within their useful five year, will render services to the households and affect the climate. Hence the number of ICS to be considered is only referring to ICS which are still within their life span. For example in Senegal only the stove sold in the last 2 years will be counted for the calculation of the outcome. Using these and other relevant figures allows EiEco to prevent over-reporting.


## Impact assessment

The project will assess the quality of the access provided applying the EnDev Cooking Energy System (CES) methodology. The CES methodology is EnDev's proxy-indicator approach for assessing the quality of access to improved cooking energy services. It takes into account the technical character as well as the complexity of improving access to cooking energy services. Improvements can be achieved by replacing traditional fuel or technology with modern fuels or technologies, improving cooking devices and cooking equipment, adjusting user behaviour and cooking practices as well as improving ventilation and modifying the kitchen. While some internationally discussed measurement approaches are mainly based on household surveys, the CES methodology is a hybrid approach combining elements of both methods: it is based on field-based approximations and household surveys.

### Further impact studies will be conducted to assess ICS adoption and daily usage, and the subsequent actual reduction in woodfuel consumption and emissions using the Kitchen Performance Test (KPT™), which is a field test used to evaluate ICS performance in real-world settings. It is designed to assess actual impacts on households' cooking practices, including the amount of time spent cooking, the number of meals prepared per day, populations cooking normally, and give the best indication of real world performance. Based on the KPT-based fuel savings data, a greenhouse gas emission reduction estimate will be made using the IPCC's approach in the ICS Impact Model™.

The EnDev Market Development Scorecard will be used to establish the stage of ICS market development as has been described above.


### Further socio-economic co-benefits (including gender, income, job creation, or health aspects), as well as potential adaptation impacts will be assessed by household surveys.


## Independent evaluation

An independent evaluation/term-time review: an independent mid-term review will be conducted in year three of the implementation. It will focus on an overall review of EnDev's programmatic approach, project progress, performance and impact (environmental, social, gender), and of finances. The review will also focus on the institutional arrangements and governance structure of the project. The review will be based on desk reviews and interviews with key staff and partners of all projects, and on field visits to two sites.

The mid-term review will include recommendations for (1) any corrective actions if necessary (2) updates to the project plan, and (3) adjustments in the project's budget. These recommendations will be accompanied by specific recommendations and recommendations for the EnDev GCF project as a whole. The recommendations will be addressed to the relevant stakeholders of both countries.

Final evaluation: An independent final evaluation will be conducted within six months of the end of the project. It will focus on an overall review of the project's performance, progress and impact and on implementation and the results of the recommendations given by the mid-term review. The results of the final evaluation will be presented to the stakeholders of the sector in each of the two countries.

# Secretariat's assessment of FP103

## Proposal name: Promotion of Climate-Friendly Cooking: Kenya and Senegal

### Accredited entity:
Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit GmbH (GIZ)

### Country/ies):
Kenya and Senegal

### Project/programme size:
Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project will transform the market dynamic of the improved cooking stove (ICS) sector in Kenya and Senegal, building in scale the sector's ability to meet the needs of the market and local people. Until 2030, the number of users can be cumulatively sold, resulting in a significant revenue stream for stakeholders (EUR 135.56). The project will significantly increase the number of users among rural and from urban households across both countries, directly benefiting 11.23 million people who are currently not able to access ICS. | This is a large project with many executing entities (GIZ acting as an AE and EE; 2 additional EEs for Kenya and 3 additional EEs for Senegal) and the Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit GmbH (GIZ) will be involved in this, but there is concern that there may be issues related to execution. |
| Greenhouse gas emissions reductions of 6.47 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent are projected during the project period | The project will provide assistance to the producers of cooking stoves on scaling production and marketing; increased production to actual sales needs to be monitored. |
| Sustainable market growth for the ICS sector is forecasted, with grant financing based on a tranche system. This in turn is based on sales of stoves to end users | The GCF funding is to be divided into two tranches: tranche 1 – EUR 16.51 million |

# Summary of the Secretariat’s review

## Project background

- Nearly 3 billion people worldwide use solid fuels, such as firewood and charcoal, as the primary fuel for cooking. Burning solid fuels releases emissions of carbon dioxide, methane, and black carbon, which are some of the most significant contributors to global climate change.
- The global population using biomass energy in rural areas is estimated at 2.5 billion people. The use of improved cookstoves (ICS) is estimated at 0.6–2.4 gigatons (Gt) of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2eq per year).
- The proposed global project is aimed at demonstrating an innovative ICS market transformation strategy, starting in two countries (Kenya and Senegal). By doing so, it aims to improve the lives of 10 million people by reducing their exposure to indoor air pollution.
- The project will significantly limit the consumption of non-renewable biomass in the cooking sector compared to the baseline situation, leading to GHG emissions reductions of 6.47 metric tons (Mt) CO2eq per year.

## Project aims and objectives

- The project aims to accelerate the growth of the ICS sector, particularly in more remote and rural areas.
- It will increase the number of small, artisanal, under-capitalized, and informal ICS producers into a much larger economic sector with a sufficient supply chain and business model capacity to ensure sustainability and ability to deliver better quality products to a greater number of consumers, particularly those in remote rural areas. The anticipated impact is that 10 million people will be served by the end of the five-year project and achieve a six-fold increase by 2030.
- The total project costs are estimated at EUR 57.17 million (USD 65 million), including a EUR 38.94 million contribution from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) (tranche 1: EUR 22.05 million; tranche 2: EUR 22.85 million); EUR 12.8 million (USD 14.53 million) from the German


---
**Page 132**
---

# Component-by-component analysis

The project consists of two national-level outputs and one global output (excluding project management):

- **Output 1:** Accelerated market development for climate-friendly cookstoves in Kenya (GCF finance: EUR 19.91 million; co-financing: EUR 9.75 million) for both tranches;
- **Output 2:** Accelerated market development for climate-friendly cookstoves in Senegal (GCF finance: EUR 30.84 million; co-financing: EUR 16.22 million);
- **Output 3:** Sharing and replication of knowledge on evidence-based climate-friendly solutions and their contribution to NDCs of relevant countries (GCF finance: EUR 0.95 million; co-financing: EUR 0.78 million).

### Component 1: Strengthening the ICS supply and delivery chain in Kenya and Senegal

This subcomponent will address the key barriers to professionalizing and commercializing the ICS sector (see annexes 1.1 to 1.3 in element C.2 of the funding proposal). To do so, the project will provide a comprehensive capacity-building package to qualified ICS supply chain stakeholders: to (a) improve their professionalization level; (b) expand their access to finance and credit; and (c) increase their access to markets and other forms of capital for larger business-class producers. The scheme will entail the provision of tailored training, mentoring, and coaching services.

The project will implement a performance-based support scheme to enable artisanal and professional producers to significantly increase their production sales and levels and move into class 1 or 2 market segments (see annexes 1.4 to 1.6 in element C.2).

The tool will be provided in tranches in line with the agreed business plan, results-based criteria and milestones. The provision of tranches will be linked to and based on actual achieved sales revenue and/or production levels as per the agreed business plans.

### Component 2: Enhancing consumer demand and the ICS market environment

In both countries, under the second component, demand-side interventions will address barriers to consumer adoption of climate-friendly cookstoves (see barrier 1.4) in terms of social and market environment barriers (see barrier 2.4). Awareness is key for behavioural change in this very traditional sector. Demand for ICS is likely to be influenced by a better understanding of the risks associated with using conventional stoves, such as health impacts on women and others. Technical assistance will support awareness-raising and public relations for the entire market chain, including media campaigns, exhibitions, and other promotional events. Regional events shall sensitize the population and disseminate messages on behavioural change.

This subcomponent will also help finalize and streamline the institutional and policy framework for ICS in both countries (see barrier 2.5). It will support the development of roadmaps and monitoring systems for sectoral mitigation targets. In addition, it will assist countries in developing and strengthening quality assurance frameworks, including capacities


---
**Page 133**
---

## Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### Impact potential Scale: High

The proposed project aims to create a self-sustainable market for ICS, leading to substantial climate mitigation impacts (i.e., direct project lifetime GHG emissions reductions of 24.7 MtCO₂e and cumulative GHG emissions reduction of 31.24 MtCO₂e during the project influence period until 2030), or a cumulative 31.24 MtCO₂e over the project's lifespan.

- Cumulative GHG emissions reduction impact: The project will result in large and highly cost-effective direct GHG emissions reductions because of the accelerated adoption of efficient ICS stoves, leading to an estimated additional sales of 5 million ICS units per year (compared with baseline scenario), leading to estimated additional sales of 13.08 million ICS and indirect GHG emissions reductions of 24.7 MtCO₂e until 2030.

- Impact on vulnerable populations: 96,000 poor urban people and 1.91 million vulnerable rural households, including 65.6 million women-headed households and 5.57 million children.

### Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

The paradigm shift resulting from the project will facilitate a substantial contribution to the national climate goals to reduce GHG emissions in the cooking sector through the adoption of ICS by an additional 30 per cent of Senegalese households and 36 per cent of Kenyan households. This will be achieved as a result of the market transformation of the ICS sector through:

- Establishment of a new class of ICS producers that are independent of official development assistance, investment ready, with sufficient technological base.


---
**Page 134**
---

# Project Overview

The project will result in a fivefold increase in annual sales of ICS (across both countries) compared to projected baseline levels by 2030. By doing so, it will effectively enable countries to achieve their stated NDC targets.

## Key Indicators

### (a) Household penetration
- In Kenya: from 15% in the beginning of 2020 up to 48%, leading to an increase in annual ICS sales from a projected 39,760 units in the beginning of 2020 to 1,116,621 ICS by project end.
- In Senegal: from 5% in the beginning of 2020 up to 48%, leading to an increase in annual ICS sales from a projected 39,760 units in the beginning of 2020 to 586,325 ICS by project end.

### (b) Business penetration
- In Kenya: from 1% in the beginning of 2020 up to 4%, leading to an increase in annual ICS sales from a projected 39,760 units in the beginning of 2020 to 158,671 ICS by project end.
- In Senegal: from 1% in the beginning of 2020 up to 4%, leading to an increase in annual ICS sales from a projected 39,760 units in the beginning of 2020 to 58,671 ICS by project end.

### (c) The economic potential
- In Kenya: more than six million households (more than five million in rural areas and one million urban households).
- In Senegal: more than three million households.
- The replication potential is other countries is also significant. Globally, there are over three billion households with a high share of cooking emissions from woodfuel.

### (d) Knowledge
- Knowledge about the climate benefits of ICS remains low in both countries and is steadily growing due to population growth.
- Knowledge about the climate benefits of ICS remains low in both countries and is steadily growing due to population growth. Awareness that achieving NDC goals is not only widely perceived, but will contribute to improving knowledge about the ICS sector and its climate benefits.

### (e) Collaboration
- At least two countries with a high share of GHG emissions from the cooking sector.
- The creation of knowledge among the many ICS actors (sector leaders, producers, distributors, retailers, consumers, etc.) is essential for success. This will be achieved through workshops and training sessions as well as through authorities.

### (f) Regulatory framework
- The project will contribute to regulatory frameworks and policies in both countries.

3.3 Sustainable development potential

24. The project will deliver numerous sustainable development benefits and avoid tangible costs to achieve the United Nations’ Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) for over 17.3 million people directly during the project lifetime and over 30.16 million people indirectly by 2030 via:
- Reduction of energy poverty (SDG 1);
- Lessening exposure to health risks (SDG 3);
- Reducing reliance on biomass fuel and biofuel (SDG 5);
- Improving access to modern energy (SDG 7);
- Reducing GHG emissions and strengthening communities’ resilience to climate change (SDG 13); and
- Diminishing pressure on forest resources (SDG 15)

25. The project will also result in the following additional climate change adaptation benefits:
- Reductions in fuel use directly contribute to the sparing of resources (money or labour) required by the households to acquire the fuel. These avoided expenditures can allow households to allocate the resources into investments for adaptation measures;
- Climate-resilient livelihoods are created and strengthened through diversification, using the availability of biomass fuel. Reductions in specific use allows households to adapt to shortages in the supply of biomass fuel; and
- Introducing new technologies such as improved stoves, adapting the ICS technology can lead to an improved level of resources for adaptation. The household-level emissions will need to be reduced significantly, as well as laboratory testing of the stoves to ensure that the health benefits are realised.

26. The project will also benefit vulnerable groups. The project has been designed to maintain and improve access to biomass fuel and biofuel in rural areas outside national market transformation. Yet at the same time, this transformation will ensure substantial adaptation benefits for women who are disproportionately affected by climate change. Women have been underserved targeted by the project but have also been identified as among the most vulnerable to climate impacts in view of their climate risk profile and dependence depleting forest resources.

27. Scaling up the use of improved stoves, which reduces drudgery for women in collecting wood fuel, and saves money if fuel needs to be purchased. Benefits are further enhanced by reducing the time spent on collecting firewood; their costs severed if and when the health, social and environmental benefits are accounted for.

28. Further measures to improve the supply of biomass fuel and forestry management outside of the project area will also need to be implemented in order to adequately address the problems resulting from the growing demand for biomass fuel.

34 Other sustainable development benefits of the project include the reduction of energy poverty, improved access to modern energy services, increased income opportunities and income-generating activities, in particular for women engaged in the IGS value chain.
