## Establishment of timber plantations for sustainable biomass supply through various programs such as agroforestry.

### Senegal

In Senegal, because of the large share of CO2 emissions stemming from the use of firewood and charcoal for cooking, there are explicit interventions for reducing the use of non-renewable energy sources. The government has defined a number of specific national targets for interventions addressing the emissions from domestic cooking with wood fuels have been defined in the National Action Plan (NAP) for 2015-2030. These include the installation of 45,000 ICS for firewood and charcoal. As far as Senegal's key development priorities are concerned, the project will contribute to the achievement of the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) formulated in the Plan Sénégalo-Energétique (PSE): (i) foster economic growth and (ii) reduce poverty.

### Kenya

The GCF project will be embedded into on-going GIZ programmes in Kenya and Senegal where the core project implementation structure is already in place. The programme has a very good track record of working with government agencies, private sector companies, NGOs, institutions and MoGs. Project teams also exert a very close monitoring of beneficiaries and of supported activities. In addition, the project will be closely linked to the work done by the Agricultural Services in Kenya who meet every month with LMOs to monitor their sales. This provider of regular stakeholder engagement has been used during preparation of the GCF proposal.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

The proposed GCF project has been designed to ensure minimum concessionality of GCF funding such that GCF grant funding is deployed only to support viable climate initiatives that cannot currently be financed by the non-grant instruments.

Without intervention, the financial return on the four ICS producer classes is poor. Cash-based, informal activity will lead to underinvestment in equipment. The support the producers receive from the project will enable them to make more informed investment decisions as opposed to production. It does not allow for hiring additional staff, creating sufficient stock or increasing productivity. It is essentially a stagnant situation, with an increase in sales of 5 per cent over the baseline.

For Kenya, the average financial internal rate of return (FIRR) is between 3 and 2 per cent and the project will be able to achieve a FIRR of +10 per cent. The GCF scenario for this category is positive in the business-as-usual (BAU) case. For the professional class, there is a destruction of value since the BAU anticipates general underinvestment and postponing of significant capital expenditures. The project will enable the producers to invest in equipment and manual machines are continued (at lower amounts). Investments in safety equipment, work wear and other items are also supported by the project. The average FIRR for this category is raised by only 1.5 per cent year over the baseline, the GCF scenario for this category is +12 per cent. Outside support for investment is clearly needed on its own to reach the next highest category.

With GCF support and the feasibility viability of the four ICS producer classes increases with the introduction of the project. The average FIRR for the business-as-usual case is 39 per cent if the designated capital expenditures are implemented and additional staff hired, then production increases as does the extent of the marketing and distribution network. It is important to note


---
**Page 138**
---

that these FBR results are positive, they are modest and do not allow the ICS producers sufficient returns to access the respective traditional corporate banking markets from a purely financial point of view. This sets the stage for the technical assistance and professionalization activities that are described in this document. The objective is to increase the number of producers that are able to formulate business plans and successfully conclude financing agreements with existing entities in the respective markets.

A sensitivity analysis was conducted on the key investment parameters at the ICS producer level in Kenya and Senegal was carried out. The following observations were made:

Generally, the FBR estimates are more volatile to changes in different inputs. This is because there are fewer variables driving these calculations. The FBR calculation relies most for its results on the scale of sales and volumes of sales while taxes are minor and derived from these inputs.

(FBR) calculations are most sensitive to changes in sales volume and production volumes. Variations in the price of electricity have such a strong impact on the FBR calculation. The most significant sensitivity impact is the combination of a decrease in sales volume and an increase in electricity prices.

Internal economic rate of return (ERR) estimates are not as volatile to changes in different inputs. This is partly due to more factors (the addition of the economic value of GHG emissions reductions) that influence ERR calculations. The additional economic benefits from GHG emissions reductions are very large and are either captured by ERR or FBR.

Given the result of the sensitivity analysis and the impact on FBR and ERR for the sector, the increasing scale and sales volumes has a huge impact on the sustainability of the market.


### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

a. **Environmental and social risk category:** The AE did not identify any significant adverse environmental and social risks associated with the project. The direct impacts are expected to be minor and would come mainly from the project's support for small-scale manufacturing operations in rural areas.

b. **Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP):** The ESMP provided an Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA). The ESA which included an environmental and social impact assessment, was prepared by the AE. The ESMP also identifies potential negative environmental impacts and risks of ICS production. The ESMP presents the organization and staffing for safeguards, tasks and responsibilities, grievance redress mechanism, occupational health and safety, and training and capacity building. The ESMP also includes a description of the contents of individual producer's ESMP. The ESA also provides a brief description of the national environmental policy and legislation that applies to the project area in Senegal. It is expected that environmental permits will be required for the business class production.

c. **Environmental and social risks and impacts:** The project proposal emphasized that aside from existing regulations, the ICS technology has two additional benefits: (1) higher efficient use of firewoods; and (2) lower emission of particulate matter which likely also improves the indoor air quality of homes. The AE was able to identify and describe other wider


---
**Page 139**
---

### Direct environmental and social risks would include occupational health and safety, material handling (i.e., logging and extraction), industrial pollution and nuisance as well as noise of energy and operation of mechanized equipment and labor conditions.

Land and land rights acquisition are also a risk that could lead to displacement or involuntary resettlement (gallery) transaction hence no displacement or involuntary resettlement impacts are expected. There are indirect risks associated with the project activities, which will be addressed in the project design. In addition, the AEF triggered in conflict and context sensitivity safeguard will be used to address any potential and context sensitivity.

### Indigenous peoples

The project is not expected to adversely affect the indigenous people living within the project area. The project will provide employment opportunities as it will contribute to resource conservation and reduce potential conflicts over resources. The ESMP provides that the project and its engagement will be implemented in a culturally-appropriate manner, with respect for conduct of conduct with all affected for all affected parties.

### Community health and safety

The project is seen to contribute to the improvements in the dietary diversity and respiratory health due to the reduction of indoor air pollution from using ICS compared to traditional cooking methods. The quality of the biomass fuel, which will need to be monitored through field testing to ensure that exposure at the household level will not exceed the WHO guidelines for safe levels. This will include the quality of the cooking energy systems (ICS) using the CES methodology developed by the national laboratories. The project will also continue to work with the national certification bodies and product standard bodies to improve product health and safety of the ICS.

### Indirect risks and impacts

There are potential indirect risks and impacts of the project across the wider population, including those who do not use ICS and therefore contribute to increasing pressure on firewood. This will need to be monitored through field testing to ensure that exposure at the household level will not exceed the WHO guidelines for safe levels.

### Improvements in standards

Enelh will continue to support and contribute to the improvement of its parallel and as part of the project, it will work with other stakeholders on: (1) improvements of manufacturing processes and standard of operations and assets; (2) use of ICS design and construction guidelines; (3) development of a monitoring and evaluation system to periodically assess indoor exposure to emissions from cooking using ICSs. The project will also continue to support the implementation of the provisions of 'training on the use of equipment, work safety, and social standard of work'; and 'supporting the establishment of a quality assurance system as part of "production" support'. The project will work with other stakeholders to promote further efforts for improving supply chain as well as addressing barriers related to sustainable fuelwood production and access.

### Project ESMP

The Project ESMP describes a process for due diligence of ICS producers receiving support from the project. The process includes the environmental and social screening of business and/or production plans by the project environmental and social officer and the

### Gender policy

The proposed country has a comprehensive and thorough gender assessment, and therefore complies with international guidelines of the GCF Gender Policy and Action Plan. It also includes a gender action plan to support the implementation of issues identified in the gender assessment.

The gender assessment indicates that both Senegal and Gambia are countries with gender equity issues, including low scores in economic participation, educational attainment and political empowerment (only 15% women in parliament). There are laws and legislations that support the equality of women and men in access to land, resources and assets, but customary laws and social norms still need to be addressed to achieve gender equality in the countries. Both countries have policies that try

### Risks

The gender assessment of the sector for both countries provides in-depth information on the cooking sector. The assessment was done based on reviews of studies and consultation with women and men in the sector. It also includes a review of existing interventions to reduce exposure to smoke-related health hazards, ease the burden on women and children to collect firewood, and improve access to income-generating opportunities through the use of ICS.

The assessment recognizes that women face the brunt of the problem but are also the potential solution. The analysis shows that women have a high level of experience in the production of ICS. Findings indicate that women are found to be efficient in using cookstoves and much more efficient than men, which is an important factor for the adoption of ICS.

The context analysis indicates that women's household work is a barrier to spending much more time engaging in the production of ICS unless some female relatives perform these household tasks on their behalf. The context analysis also shows that women are less likely to be involved in decision-making processes and that women's access to land, credit, and other resources is limited.

The assessment recognizes that women have a high level of experience in the production of ICS but that they face barriers such as lack of access to land, credit, and other resources. The context analysis also shows that women are less likely to be involved in decision-making processes and that women's access to land, credit, and other resources is limited.

The project understands that there will be gender-related issues that need to be addressed but it tread very carefully, meaning it will use a gender-sensitive approach, to address these issues. The project will also ensure that the project team has experience in working with women and men indicated that the roles played by women and men do not pose any challenge so far. The project will also ensure that the project team has experience in working with women and men aimed at convincing them of the benefits of cookstove use, such as the potential reduction in time spent on cooking.

The project is expected to have major positive impacts on addressing challenges faced by women by ensuring access to and use of ICS to reduce the burden in collecting fuel wood and provide women with income-earning opportunities through engagement in the RSC value chain activities. The project will also ensure that the project team has experience in working with people with disabilities as well.

# Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

**(a)** GCF is expected to provide a grant of EUR 83 million for the provision of technical assistance to strengthen the ICS supply chain. The total project cost is EUR 57.5 million with co-financing by way of grants from a range of institutions including BMZ, the Government of Germany, and the World Bank. The project is expected to leverage parallel financing of EUR 31.7 million from ICS producers, banks, financing partners, and other sources.

**(b)** The project is based on the pilot approach of and experience gained through EnDev. Over 12 years, EnDev supported the ICS sector in moving from the pre-commercial towards commercialization phase by providing technical assistance to ICS producers and implementing partners to convince unserved households to invest in ICS as well as the capacity building for the implementation partners. The project has a 50% co-financing rate.

### AE/EE capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

**(a)** GIZ (the AE) has an extensive track record in implementing ICS over 12 years in the region, including the EnDev project. GIZ is currently implementing the ICS component in Africa, Latin America and Asia under the global EnDev energy access partnership, including 19 countries with active ICS support projects. In all components, GIZ will take the responsibility for the overall implementation of the project.

**(b)** The proposal states that GIZ will ensure a clear and strict separation between the two different components of the project: the AE and the EE component.

### Implementation in Kenya (medium risk)

**(a)** In Kenya, the project is implemented by a government AE (the Ministry of Energy) and two international EEs (GIZ Kenya and SNS Netherlands Development Organisation). In Swaziland, the project will be implemented through three large NGOs (ENDA Nigeria, eedo ECOPUP and ONG CONCEPT Senegal) will be implementing the project. The implementation partners are expected to reach 150,000 villages in the 14 regions of the country. The three NGOs have been selected via a competitive tendering process based on their technical expertise, financial management and administrative capacities and physical presence in the regions.

**(b)** As identified in the funding proposal, the project involves multiple EEs and other implementation partners with different mandates and responsibilities which makes the implementation structure complicated. The AE is relied upon to provide necessary capacity building for the EEs as well as to manage the administrative and transaction costs during the implementation of the project.

### Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

**(a)** Complaints from dropped artisanal producers: the funding proposal expects that approximately 10% of the artisanal producers will drop out due to the limitation in growth potential during the commercialization process. There is a risk of providing backlash from these producers and their supporters, which could lead to increased complaints during the implementation of the project. The AE states that the project team has developed a plan for addressing such complaints, and a regular grievance mechanism will be established.

### Delay in execution: three potential causes (medium risk)

**(a)** Delays are expected due to: 1) lack of sufficient capacity among producers; 2) producers not being able to scale up at the anticipated rate; and 3) producers not being able to make 20 per cent of their production available for sale. The AE states that these factors will have a limited effect on the level of impact of the project. The disbursement is not linked to a fixed calendar schedule, but rather to the achievement of milestones. If the first tranche is not completed by the end of year 2 quarter eight. In case a significant number of producers do not qualify for the first tranche within the first two years, the AE states that


---
**Page 143**
---

project will assess sales growth and future performance and consider whether it will allow for a grace period to reach the targeted sales volume or reduce the support. In addition, if the 20 per cent producer contribution proves to be a large hurdle in the first year of implementation, the project may need to adjust its assumptions on sales growth and consider either delaying the disbursement until the 20 per cent contribution is made or reducing the grant amount accordingly. The specific criteria for these adjustments will be set out in the selection criteria for options to consider will be elaborated in the grant operation manual. Components 1.1.1 and 2.1.1 include the provision for performance-based incentives. Given this nature of the project, it is important that the GCF Secretariat works closely with the co-financing amount for the performance-based grants and undisbursed funds for compliance purposes.

(b) Low willingness to pay in rural areas: the funding proposal states that there is great market potential for EHR, but actual demand is geographically limited. The demand is mostly concentrated in urban areas where the population has a higher income level than it does more on rural areas. The funding proposal estimates the latent demand potential by calculating the number of households with an annual income above USD 10 per household (70–90 per cent) who are able to pay. The funding proposal indicates that globally, there is a market for approximately 25 million units at a price point of around USD 5. However, there is a gap between ability to pay and willingness to pay, and the project aims to address this by conducting a survey in rural areas to overcome the barrier of low willingness to pay and mobilize rural sales at a significant rate. The project aims to deploy various methods to sensitise the population in rural areas based on their income levels and willingness to pay. The project will also conduct regular surveys to monitor the sales volume in the rural areas; and

Economic analysis conducted by the GCF Secretariat demonstrates that the cost of EUR 3.86 per household for the awareness-raising campaign for sensitizing (activities 1.2.1 and 2.2.1) is a cost-efficient compared to the range of global estimates (USD 1–9 per household). The project aims to conduct this campaign in rural areas, which will result in an IRR ranging from 6 per cent to 19 per cent. However, the sensitivity analysis shows that the project would not be financially viable and the NPV could be negative even with GCF grant financing. The project aims to create a self-sustained market for EHR in rural areas by addressing the awareness-raising campaign impacts.

(c) GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk):

(i) If approved, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration risk remains low as the project remains within the GCF limit in terms of concentration limits.

(d) Compliance (medium risk)

(a) This project deals with the conversion of cookstoves to more efficient versions, thereby reducing emissions. The project is expected to involve some significant procurements and distribution of the improved technology among beneficiaries. Typically, such projects are subject to a higher level of compliance and corruption risk. Therefore, it is important that the AEC and delivery partners exercise a great amount of due diligence in selecting suppliers and ensuring adherence to best procurement and quality practices. The GIZ is a well-known and respected development partner and has a number of measures and safeguards in place to oversee and address these risks. Furthermore, the project will be subject to regular monitoring by the GCF Secretariat for appropriate oversight and accountability over the use of GCF funds in this project. As GCF relies on grant funding from the World Bank, it is expected that any issues related to corruption and related financial integrity issues should be mitigated. Accordingly, the project would be assigned a "medium" risk rating for compliance.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP103

## Proposal name: Promotion of Climate-Friendly cooking: Kenya and Senegal

### Accredited entity: Deutsche Gesellschaft für internationale Zusammenarbeit GmbH (GIZ)

### Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: High

#### 1. Over 60 per cent of the world's population still use solid fuels as the primary source of cooking energy. The stoves generally used are biomass (fuel such as biomass, fuelwood and charcoal) or traditional wood-burning stoves. These stoves are often poor and as a result, cooking with them is not only cause emissions of greenhouse gases (GHGs) but also health hazards. Generally, women and children spend hours every day collecting firewood and other solid fuels for cooking. The use of improved cookstoves (ICS) can potentially increase burning efficiency and thereby reduce emissions of GHGs. It is estimated that the use of ICS could reduce household energy-related carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) emissions within the range of 0.6 to 2.4 gigatons of carbon-dioxide equivalent (GtCO2e) per year.

#### 2. A large majority of African households still use solid fuels in traditional cooking stoves. People living in Kenya and Senegal are no exception. So, for the penetration of ICS in these countries to be successful, it is necessary that the technology is affordable and accessible to urban populations, adequate enough to pay for the market price of ICS. The root cause of the problem lies in micro-scale production and sales operation by artisanal producers who lack sufficient capacity to produce and supply ICS at scale. This is a major challenge to the production and marketing of ICS. Overseas development assistance (ODA) does not generate adequate investment to address the financing gap. Moreover, there are no existing policies or frameworks in place that can support the scale-up and subsequent awareness-building and persuasion on the demand side to transition from traditional cooking stoves to ICS.

#### 3. The proposed project aims to demonstrate an innovative strategy to transform the RCS market for ICS production and sales by doing two things: (i) increasing the private sector contribution to nationally determined contributions (NDCs). The project intends to accelerate the scale-up of ICS production and sales in Kenya and Senegal, which is a prerequisite with an irreversible market transformation. It will transform the sector from one which is dominated by a number of small artisanal producers into a sector led by a few large-scale producers into a much stronger economic engine. This includes adequate technological know-how for sustainable business management, gaining access to commercial capital and the ability to deliver better-quality products at competitive prices.

#### 4. The intended outcome of the project is to triple annual ICS production and sales volume by the project end (after five years) and achieve a roughly six-fold increase by 2030. Analysis suggests that this will be sufficient for Kenya and Senegal to make significant progress toward their NDC targets and to achieve ODA-independent growth. The project will significantly increase the


---
**Page 148**
---

# 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

## Scale: High

### a. The project in not a novel project, since the promotion of ICS has already been established on a limited scale in the target countries. In that respect, it is not innovative as such. However, the delivery modalities have been developed based on thorough analyses and subsequent consultations with key stakeholders. The results from these detailed feasibility studies and analyses in both countries have helped identify current gaps, while the project has addressed each gap and developed a comprehensive business model to address them. This will enable Sengal to reach its stated NDC target for GHG emissions in the energy cooking sector and Kenya to meet its 2030 targets (50% reduction of 2010 levels) for the NDC GHG emission reduction target in the cooking stove vector of Kenya.

### b. One of the elements of paradigm shift may be realized when the current dependency on DCO for the promotion of ICS is replaced by a market-oriented demand and supply of ICS that involves both private sector actors. This will help to overcome the currently painstakingly slow penetration rates in an unstable DCO-dependent financing environment. The development of a business model that relies on market-based financing and mutual benefits, supported by awareness raising campaigns and policy advocacy, is expected to lead to a completely new paradigm for the ICS market in the two countries.

### c. The project possesses significant replication potential, especially within the two participating countries where the project will be implemented. It has already incorporated knowledge acquired from a previous project (i.e. Energizing Development [EnDev]), and intends to replicate its success by building on this experience. The project is expected to heavily depend on strengthening the producers of ICS as intended, ensuring the ability of last-mile entrepreneurs, promoting design improvements to address context specificity and longevity of the devices, and


---
**Page 149**
---

13 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The project contributes directly to the achievement of the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs):

(a) SDG 7: ensure access to affordable, reliable and sustainable energy for all by removing supply-side barriers for ICS;

(b) SDG 8: end poverty in all its forms and promote well-being by promoting household cooking stoves with greater burning (i.e. calorific) efficiency and reduced emissions of black carbon and fluorine;

(c) SDG 5: empower women and girls by giving them greater access to a less health-hazardous and more cost-efficient cooking stove;

(d) SDG 12: ensure sustainable consumption and production patterns by creating reduced demand for firewood and promoting the use of alternative fuels for cooking;

(e) SDG 13: take urgent action to combat climate change by reducing GHG emissions from fuelwood burning for cooking; and

(f) SDG 15: protect, restore and promote sustainable use of terrestrial ecosystems and sustainably manage forests by saving woodlots from being consumed as fuelwood in inefficient cooking stoves in Kenya and Uganda.

Since the overall effort will be to stimulate the production and marketing of ICS, the project will strengthen production and marketing capacities of ICS producing enterprises. It is expected that the project will result in the introduction of 20,548 units of ICS (supporting over 27,456 green jobs) in the two target countries. Moreover, the conservative estimates based on previous ICS promotions suggest that beyond the third month of the introduction of an


---
**Page 150**
---

ICS is a low-cost household, it will save fuel-related costs in households where people are haltered to purchase firewood from the market. In an average year, as high as EUR 70 could be saved per unit of ICS, which would be significant potential savings for a low-income household.

16. One of the major social co-benefits of the project is most likely to be realized through the enhancement of (primarily) women and children's health as they are generally exposed to indoor combustion emissions from firewood and charcoal burning in their homes. The use of ICS offers an improved burning condition where the calorific value is increased through the near-complete combustion of wood pellets, which are more efficient than traditional firewood or biomass. This will improve the quality of air that the cook and children breathe. Not only it will reduce the number of people suffering from bronchial diseases but also will save costs on the treatment of patients.

17. The other major social benefit involves saving time and effort devoted to the collection, preparation, and carrying of firewood for cooking and heating purposes in cutting woodlots. Although a significantly high proportion of households rely on market-based sources of firewood, the time saved could produce many social benefits, particularly for women and children.

18. Any efforts towards the promotion of ICS have built-in co-benefit regarding gender equity. In particular, it will empower household women to participate in income-generating activities, which will be a significant benefit to their families. The project will also provide time savings and even economic benefits. In the extension process, as many as 8000 women will be involved in the promotion of ICS through the establishment of women's groups. Women will also be involved as extension service workers in the promotion of such effective technologies. All of these aspects combined contribute to ensuring this is a truly gender-friendly project.

20. As stated above, under SIG 15, the positive externality of this project is to reduce the demand for firewood and charcoal from forest areas. This will provide an additional opportunity to survive and thrive. However, without conducting a parallel forest management programme with sustainable woodlot management, following institutional certification and monitoring of the project's impact on the environment, it may not be possible to pursue neither involves the relevant official institutions nor intends to engage in the larger challenge of long-term forest management - the latter clearly being beyond the scope of the project.

20. Given the mandate of the project, it appears that is offers significantly high sustainable development potential.


### 1.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

21. Both recipient countries belong to the African group, where the need for sustainable development is paramount and urgent. The Human Development Index ranks Kenya and Senegal among the lowest in Africa. While both countries have made progress in addressing the socio-economic challenges of these countries. Since the ultimate gain will be in terms of reduction of poverty and improvement of living standards, which are internationally applicable well recognized under the UNFCCC, the project fits well within the financing framework of GCF.

22. The need for financial support for the recipient countries is well justified. Both countries exhibit the highest levels of energy poverty in Africa. Kenya has a significant untapped potential in energy efficiency measures. However, the countries are not in position to finance such activities on their own. In 2017, Kenya's total public debt was EUR 38 billion, of which 5.19 percent is due for repayment within one year. Senegal has also been unable to implement effective measures that would simultaneously address lack of basic and reliable energy services for their citizens and safeguard their remaining forest resources. There exists a critical financing

**Page 151**

## Country ownership

The project aims and specific objectives are fully aligned with climate change and development priorities of both countries. Both countries share a common vision regarding low carbon development – a fundamental aspiration under the Paris Agreement. As indicated earlier, Kenya has already committed to this goal in its National Climate Change Action Plan (NCAP). For Kenya, CS market development is considered in the draft National Climate Change Action Plan 2014-2030. The Kenyan government has also included low carbon development as part of the National Climate Change Action Plan 2013 meant low carbon development, despite the fact that the primary objective of the NCAP is to promote renewable energy and energy efficiency. Renewable energy and renewable fuelwood was considered as one of the objectives in the NCAP. It sets a target of the distribution of 8.4 million CS units. The objective is fully aligned with a vision document titled Plan Seno.

As the primary driver of the project, Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) has been implementing and managing the project since its inception in Kenya and also the major executing entity. GIZ is globally known for its relentless efforts towards promoting low-carbon energy technologies, which so far have been successful in providing more than 10 million people with access to clean cooking solutions.

The project benefits from the experience of an earlier project called Energie, which has been implemented and managed by GIZ. The project was designed to target larger countries to achieve SGGs in gender equality and energy access. The Energie project has provided good environmental and social benefits. GIZ has previous experience in working with relevant ministries, civil society organizations (CSOs), the Swiss National Fund for Sustainable Development (SNN) and other non-governmental organizations. Although the project does not involve any government agencies or institutions directly, it is expected that the project will be implemented through GIZ, SNN and non-governmental organization partners; it may be inferred that the proposed project still finds a considerable institutional footing for its implementation.

1 In terms of monthly volume of production and subsequent sales.

28. The project has been developed by involving the respective national designated authorities (NDA). The no-object ion letters have been duly signed by the NDA of both countries. Since human health is an important agenda of the project, the project collaborates with the World Health Organization in both countries. A wide range of stakeholders involved in the process of development of the proposal and particular attention has been given to engaging women’s groups. The project team has also ensured that a significant number is reflected in the inclusion of over 8,000 women's groups in the delivery and awareness-raising around the project.

29. The proposal package contains a plan for further involving women in the implementation. It also provides a mechanism for grievance redress so that the implementation process occurs in harmony with social and cultural sensitivities.

30. By examining various subcriteria of the investment framework of GCF, the ITAP infers that the country ownership of the project is high.


### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Moderate to High

The project is designed for two African countries: Senegal, one of the least developed countries; and Kenya, a low-income country. Incidentally, both the countries have high public debt, which makes it difficult for them to finance projects related to energy efficiency and debt servicing. Despite the fact that multilateral benefit potentials are available in the promotion of energy efficient and health saving ICS services, such a project cannot be envisaged to be financed by either of these two countries on their own. Hence, GCF is expected to provide financing for this project.

31. Out of an estimated budget of EUR 57.9 million, EUR 38.6 million is sought from a GCF grant. Since both African countries and their citizens, the majority of whom are poor, will be the target audience for a hand-held biomass technology that is also a money-saver, the grant support by GCF is expected to have a significant impact on poverty alleviation.

32. The project involves a number of stakeholders in both countries, including government agencies, project executing agencies, and private sector entities. The total cost of the project implementation shows throughout the project duration at a cost of EUR 78.24 million. However, the latter is not considered as the final cost for financing. In spite of such high costs, the project has a good chance of co-financing ratio in GCF.

33. The majority of users will go into the market to finance ICS projects in the manufacturing sector, after receiving incentives and performance-based additional support, is expected to drive ICS production within five years of the project duration. This will also help in creating new jobs for the poor people in both countries' markets. Given the budgetary spread as presented, the financing strategy appears to be innovative and cost-effective.

34. The cost effectiveness of the project is slightly higher than other energy efficiency projects being approved and financed by GCF. However, a significant proportion of the cost is for training and awareness raising activities which are not directly related to ICSs but are committed to purchase ICS units, which are generally costly. However, the implementation of the project promises to have a positive impact on both countries' energy sectors. As the biomass ICS landscape in the two countries and their capacities are improved and incentivized, they cannot operate at a scale that justifies the cost by attracting financing from market-based operators (i.e., banks and other financial institutions). Hence, GCF is expected to provide co-financing which will perpetually serve the cause, even beyond the project lifetime, the financing is


1 With respect to the baseline flow of ICS units that generally penetrate the market in a business-as-usual scenario.


---
**Page 153**
---

therefore efficient in the longer run to ensure a transformative change in the cooling energy sector.

The project does not promise large-scale leverage of private investment in the project financial scheme. However, the project intends to upgrade ICS producers and help them become creditworthy for access to financing from commercial banks and other sources of finance and growth. Assuming the experiences of EnDev, the predecessor project, will be replicated and scaled up in Senegal, it is expected that the project will have a positive financial return on its own during or after the project period. Such an exit strategy looks viable and promising.

The financial and economic returns (FIRR and EERR respectively) of the project appear to be robust for all three countries. For example, in terms of FIRR, in most high-income countries, the professional as well as artisanal ICS producers will have higher levels of FIRR and EERR which are above 10 per cent. However, there is a wide variation across countries; however, there are inter-country differences in financing returns efficiency: The Kenyan average FIRR jumps to 25 per cent for the professional sector compared with 8 per cent for artisanal producers, respectively. For Senegal, professional producers are likely to incur better FIRR gains, from a negative 17 to 6 per cent, considering that GCF support is made available for the project.

Despite an encouraging financial outlook, the project goals will be sensitive to timing of rolling out the new ICS technology and its adoption by consumers. The willingness-to-pay for different life-purpose designs of ICS are within surveyed estimates of consumers’ willingness-to-pay for similar products in the market. However, there is a risk that the new ICS may not be adopted if there is no strong marketing programme to promote the new ICS. There is an element of risk in terms of negative FIRR, although the EERR still gives positive results with a 10 per cent increase in capital costs and a simultaneous 10 per cent decline in sales for artisanal producers.

The project’s implementation timeline is ambitious. It is anticipated that emission reductions will be achieved early on as a result of the increased capacity and professionalism of the artisanal producers with professionalization kits and subsequent training can be achieved so early that the new ICS units will start reducing emissions as envisaged. Much of the impacts and their effectiveness in terms of emission reduction, however, depend on whether commercial partners in both countries can cut out and engage the current small-scale ICS producers. The effectiveness of the project also depends on the extent to which the new ICS technology needs to be brought through a strong marketing programme. The project, however, promises strong monitoring for both ICS production, sales and periodic monitoring of actual usage of the ICS units.

Given the above analyses, the IEAP is of the opinion that, despite the risks mentioned above, the efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed project is moderate to high.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The IEAP recommends the project to the Board for approval.
The IEAP recommends the following to enhance the efficiency of the project towards achieving its goals: (i) strengthening the monitoring system in both countries; (ii) Link ongoing projects that oversee forest conservation and restoration for the sustainable supply of forest products, including woody biomass;

*With a EUR 0.24 million grant, the project expects to leverage EUR 678 million in loan financing, which is only a small fraction of the total financing.

# Response of the accredited entity to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP103)

## Proposal name: Promotion of Climate-Friendly Cooking: Kenya and Senegal

### Accredited entity: Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit GmbH (GIZ)

#### Impact potential

GIZ welcomes the positive review on the impact potential and ITAP's acknowledgment of the relevance of traditional and inefficient biomass-based cooking and its global impacts on GHG emissions. GIZ supports ITAP’s view that there is a critical gap in addressing basic energy needs and the protection of forest resources, and this project can make an important contribution to this issue.

#### Paradigm shift potential

GIZ takes note of the positive review of the paradigm shift potential. GIZ concurs fully with ITAP's recognition of the co-benefits for health, gender, and the environment in addition to climate change-related benefits.

#### Sustainable development potential

GIZ welcomes the acknowledgment of the high sustainable development potential, and ITAP’s recognition of the impact co-benefits of the project for health, gender, and the environment, in addition to climate change-related benefits.

#### Needs of the recipients

GIZ is glad to see the positive rating in this area and concurs with the assessment that the needs of the recipients in the two countries, Kenya and Senegal, are high. GIZ supports ITAP’s view that there is a critical gap in addressing basic energy needs and the protection of forest resources, and this project can make an important contribution to this issue.

#### Country ownership

GIZ takes note of the review's assessment of country ownership and ITAP's recognition that the project not only supports the countries' NDC targets but also national agendas for health, the environment and gender.

#### Efficiency and effectiveness

GIZ welcomes the positive assessment with regard to efficiency and effectiveness. One clarification could be made in answer to a question posed in the review: Emission reductions from existing biomass-based cooking are not part of the baseline of this project due to the conditions specific to this project. First, the GCF project will scale-up existing baseline projects in the ICS.


---
**Page 156**
---

sector that are running now, and build on existing relationships with producers and stakeholders. Secondly, the project will start preparing producers and stakeholders for the coming years by providing them with information about the new market. The resulting production will begin very quickly in 2020 and lead to notable sales increases within the first year. Otherwise, GIZ would consider that increased sales in the first year of an ICS promotion project are unlikely.

Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

GIZ thanks ITAP for its recommendation to the Board for approval.

The project will take up the two recommendations regarding forestry and awareness raising for sustainable biomass supply chains, including to also link these topics to complementary activities of other ongoing as well as planned interventions by various implementers.

### Gender Assessment

GIZ funding proposal "Promotion of Climate-Friendly Cooking in Kenya and Senegal" Gender Assessment – 22 January 2019

This assessment was prepared for GIZ by Marlies Schuttelbar, independent Environmental and Social Safeguard Specialist, with the help of GIZ EnDev staff, in particular Beatrice Hombi, Anna Ingev, Walter Kipruto, Maxwell Muoka, Oliver Sifuna in Kenya; Mireille Aboudj Ehemba, Omar Badiano, Christoph Messinger, Nana Ofori in Senegal.

# Summary

In conformity with GCF’s environmental and social policy dated March 2018, a Gender Assessment has been conducted for the project “Promotion of Climate-Friendly Cooking in Kenya and Senegal”, submitted by GIZ to the Green Climate Fund (GCF). The project consists in technical assistance and limited investments to scale up the current promotion activities on climate-friendly cooking methods in Kenya and Senegal. It includes awareness raising, marketing activities, and developing awareness to augment the adoption of ICS by households with the aim to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from domestic cooking.

The gender assessment shows that the project has major positive impacts on gender equality, by reducing household time spent on cooking and increasing women’s income through engagement in paid work in CS production and distribution. The project has no potential negative gender impacts. Surveys of gender impacts of similar projects were available and extensive consultations have been held with women organizations in both countries. Women are the primary users of ICS and the majority of households that are engaged in cooking are headed by women, as well as other household members. Regarding economic inclusion of women in the CS value chain, the project will provide training to women on business skills, financial literacy, and how to engage in entrepreneurship and to address administrative or other formal organizations, and be capable of writing and keeping records. This lack of skills and confidence hamper their access to finance, to available assistance programs, and to market opportunities.

A Gender Action Plan has been developed for the project, where specific training of women is an important component. Gender-sensitive monitoring is included in the project and should verify that women benefit from equal work opportunities, and equitable pay for equal work. The GAP will be presented as a separate document.

The gender assessment highlights the importance of the project to address gender equality issues, including significant experience in working with gender issues at grassroots level and in all areas of Senegal and Kenya. G2R gender focal points will be established for each country and area of intervention, to coordinate implementation of the Gender Action Plan and ensure that gender equality is mainstreamed into the project.

The project benefits all age groups, especially children and the elderly, who are more likely to spend time in the kitchen than adults. The project will also benefit women and men on both sides of the supply chain. On the supply side, youth and women are mainly targeted in the project because (i) they are considered important agents of change; (ii) youth unemployment and lack of women participation in the economy are two priority dimensions of gender equality that need to be addressed by the project; and (iii) youth and women have a wide range of capabilities and ages, and the project value chain is inclusive of all groups in society, including the elderly because this will also help dissemination of the stove among all user groups. The project also includes training for men on how to cook with ICS.

Both willingness and ability to pay for the cookstoves is good, except for households with the lowest revenues. The GCF project will seek further opportunities to benefit this group, by collaborating with the relevant stakeholders in both countries to provide them with access to affordable financing options that can also benefit from the existence of a sustainable ICS sector with high quality and competitively priced stoves.

Domestic violence is a significant issue in both Senegal and Kenya. SNV and several implementing partners have experience working on this topic, which will be useful for the project. Gender-based violence has been essential for project implementation. Some stakeholders have mentioned the importance of ensuring that approaches to changing cooking methods in the household are always culturally appropriate when targeting new regions with different cultures. Activities to this end are included in the Gender Action Plan.

# Introduction

The gender assessment was performed during proposal development, which allowed the incorporation of recommendations for gender mainstreaming into project activities. At the time of writing the present document, the funding proposal was still under development. Some details and figures regarding the project description may have changed in the final Funding Proposal.

## GCF Project "Promotion of Climate-Friendly Cooking in Kenya and Senegal"

### General Objectives

The project aims at accelerating the growth of the market for improved CookStoves (ICS) and increase the share of ICS users among the rural and most vulnerable populations in Kenya and Senegal. It will enable these countries to significantly reduce consumption of non-renewable biomass in the energy cooking sector and reduce GHG emissions.

### GCF Contribution

Burning solid fuels for cooking in open fires and traditional stoves releases emissions of carbon dioxide and short-lived climate forces (SLCFs) like black carbon (BC) and organic carbon (OC) aerosols and methane (CH4). The integration of ICS into the energy sector is a key element to reduce these emissions. According to the IEA, energy efficient improved cookstoves has a global mitigation potential between 0.6 and 2.4 Gt CO2eq/yr, while at the same time delivering a wide range of sustainable development benefits such as reducing the pressure on forests and woodlands, improving indoor air quality, and providing health benefits to women and children for collecting fuelwood, and saving money for the poor (when the fuels need to be purchased). Global patterns in population growth, urbanization, and historical fuel use suggest that the number of people using ICS will increase by 1.5 billion between now and 2030. This increase is primarily due growing food consumption and rising charcoal use in Sub-Saharan Africa (SSA), which will counterbalance the declines in solid fuel use in Asia and Latin America. For this reason, two Sub-Saharan African countries: Kenya and Senegal, have been selected as the target countries for this GCF project.

### Senegal

In Senegal, the use of wood-based fuels (firewood and charcoal) is the largest single contributor to the energy sector GHG emissions (66%). This is the result of continued population growth and the overwhelming reliance on fuelwood and charcoal. The country has experienced a 2% increase in population annually over the past decade and annual fuel consumption by 3.15% per year. As a result, every year, about 40,000 ha of forest are lost due to deforestation and overexploitation of the forest resources. The UNFCCC estimates the fraction of non-renewable biomass in total biomass consumption in Senegal at 86%, being used unsustainably and leading to net GHG emissions.

The Government of Senegal defined its Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) significant emission reduction targets for the energy sector: Because of the large share of emissions stemming from the use of


---
**Page 162**
---

Agriculture for cooking, the NDC defines explicit target, i.e. 8.4 million improved cookstoves (ICS) sold cumulatively between 2010 and by 2035, and interventions for reducing the use of non-renewable wood as fuel for domestic cooking, such as (1) Dissemination of improved stoves for firewood and charcoal, (2) Increased efficiency in charcoal production, (3) Increased sustainable wood-fuel production and (4) Diversification of household fuels.

### 1.1.4 Kenya

Over 80% of Kenyans rely on the traditional use of biomass as their primary source of energy for cooking and heating. The most common forms of biomass are firewood (95%), charcoal (3%) and to a much smaller extent, kerosene (2%). Kerosene is used in rural areas with about 95% of the households relying on it. Consequently, fuelwood consumption remains one of the major factors for deforestation and environmental degradation. According to UNFCCC, 90% of biomass consumption in the country is categorized as non-renewable.

In its NDC, the Government of Kenya has committed to the mitigation contribution of 30 reduction (24.8 Mt) CO2eq of GHG emission by 2030 relative to the baseline scenario of 143 Mt CO2eq. The Kenyan NDC has identified the energy sector as a key area for reducing emissions and has set an ambitious target in this sector, with energy demand sector. The specific NDC target for cooking energy is an annual abatement of 2.8 Mt CO2eq in 2030.

### 1.2 Baseline Enduse Projects

The project builds upon several ICS promotion projects which are being implemented by the EnDev partnership in Kenya and Senegal. In these so-called "baseline" projects, EnDev is working with a large network of local partners, institutions and NGOs, and has developed a good knowledge of the sector and an understanding of the barriers to the adoption of improved stoves. The aim of this proposed GEF project is to scale up, reinforce and geographically expand EnDev's current actions in the ICS sector in Kenya.

### 1.3 Household demand for ICS: ability to pay, and barriers to further ICS dissemination

There is empirical evidence that rural households are able to own and use ICS. G. Berchtold J. and Peters (2014: Rural Energy Paper) p. 496 show that, among households who were newly equipped with wood-burning ICS, 95% had been using the stove for one year, and 90% after two years. This drop was the result of the limited availability of ICS, as the selected villages had no access to ICS markets for replacements. These figures, as well as the results from the user consultations held during project development, show that households are able to pay for ICS.

The ICS promoted by EnDev are well affordable for the majority of households, including female-headed households. This issue is discussed in the Funding Proposal and in the Environmental and Social Assessment. The payback period for the stove is short, especially when taking into account extra liquidity constraints can mostly be overcome by women entering a income-generating job in Senegal or "merry-go-round" farming.

1. **Enabling Development (EnDev)** is an energy access partnership currently financed by six donor countries: the Netherlands, Germany, Denmark, Norway, Sweden and Switzerland. The project aims to provide affordable modern energy services that meet the needs of the poor - long lasting, affordable, and appreciated by users. EnDev works in Kenya, Senegal, and Mozambique. As of 2015, EnDev has taken a leading role at promoting access to Sustainable Energy for All (SE4All). The Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) cooperates closely with the Netherlands Enterprise Agency (RVO) on the global programme level.

### The ICS supply chain

An improved cooking stove (ICS) is defined by FCRs as a stove with an efficiency of at least 40% compared to the fuel it replaces. There are two types of stoves, which are either three-stone fire for firewood stoves, and fireless Ceramic Jars for charcoal stoves. In Kenya, the annual value of ICS sales estimated at the beginning of 2020 will be 240,000 stoves comprising of stoves for new consumers, but also stove replacements and stoves stacking in similar households.

Stove production generally requires the making of a ceramic insert, made by potters, with a metal casing, made by smiths. Some stores (such as "rocket" stoves in Kenya and "fancy box" in Benegal) are built directly in the client's kitchen. Some producers are specialized in ceramic liners, others in metalware and pasting of clay on the inside of the liner.

The ICS supply chain is made up by the following types of enterprises exist in the ICS production chain:

- individuals artisans producing only ceramic liners (potter)
- individual artisans producing final ICSs (smiths and/or potters)
- individual builders/masters of stoves in the client's kitchen
- cooperative-like groups (mostly of women) producing only liners, but sometimes sub-contracting the metal casing to other smiths
- larger "workshops" producing final ICS, who may produce ceramic liners and/or buy them from other producers, depending on demand

Individual artisans are mostly active in rural areas (only in Kenya).

Production is mostly artisanal. Even the larger producers are mainly large workshops, often without stone buildings. Mechanization is currently very limited. All categories of producers may employ workers part-time or full-time, on a casual basis or more permanently. In the current situation, ICS production takes place in the informal sector and is not regulated by any law. The main activities involved in the production process are clay and sand unloading and working with feed, and skilled workers have more permanent work in pottery or metalworking.

ICS stoves are either directly to the end client, or to a range of intermediaries such as wholesalers, retailers on the weekly markets, projects or NGOs, women groups in villages. The term "Last Mile Entrepreneur" (LME) generally covers sellers, retailers, as well as installers of stoves and offerers of after-sales services.

1.5 Project outputs and sub-components

The project consists of two national level outputs and 1 global output (excluding project management). The cost estimates below are as of September 2018.

- Output 1: Accelerated market development for climate-friendly cook stoves in Kenya [GCF financier: ca. EUR 20 million; co-finance: ca. EUR 10 million]
- Output 2: Accelerated market development for climate-friendly cook stoves in Senegal [GCF financier: ca. EUR 35 million; co-finance: ca. EUR 7.5 million]
- Output 3: Improved knowledge on climate-friendly cooking solutions and their contribution to NDCs (GCF finance: ca. EUR 1 million; co-finance: ca. EUR 1.5 million)

### 1.5.1 Output 1: Accelerated market development for climate-friendly cookstoves in Kenya

a) Geographic coverage

The project will support activities within the current focal areas [22 counties] of Embu, Kenya, but also venture into seven growth countries, where initial outreach took place, and eight so-called underserved counties* in the parts of Rift Valley, Central region, Coastal region and Northern Kenya.

---

*Current Embu focal areas: Adumu, Bungoma, Busia, Vihiga, North, Uasin Gishu, Homabay, Aluoch Migori, Bomet, Kericho, Siaya, Kisii, Nyamira, Kirinyaga, Mbita, Tharaka Nithi, Kamatio, Murang'a, Embu, Matungu, Machakos.

*Growth countries: Kajiado, Narok, Makueni, Migori, Homa Bay, Siaya, Bomet, Kericho, Nakuru, Kiambu, Kirinyaga, Mombasa, Kilifi, Taita Taveta, Isiolo, Kitui, Meru.

New (underserved) counties are: Marakwet, KISI, Keale, Baringo, Tata Taveta, Isiolo, Kitui, Narok.

### 2.2 Creation of enabling market conditions

The GIZ project implementers will work with the Ministry of Energy to improve the policy and regulatory framework for improved cook stoves, by supporting the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoEF&F) in strengthening its capacity on MoEF&F targets on the climate friendly cooking sector, and the Ministry of Health (MoH) on indoor air pollution. The project will finalise development of a national framework of cooking stove regulations and standards with working closely with the Energy Regulatory Commission (ERC) and Kenya Bureau of Standards (KEBS). This will be done by supporting MoEF&F in revising existing stove testing center, including the Kenya Agricultural Research Institute (KARI). In addition, the project will support establishment of new stove testing centers preferably in academic and research institutions to complement KARI. Finally, the project will collaborate with existing initiatives to develop a sustainable fuelwood industry.

Activity 2.1.1 Professionalization of ICS production

The project will provide so-called “Professionalization kits” (see description under activity 1.1.1) to ca. 57 artisanal producers (based on current performance data), and up to 22 professional producers. They will receive very intensive technical and business training and the technological support will be based on individualized needs assessment by business coaches.

In addition, the project will foster innovation and upgrade of ICS products, including revision of ICS design for faster production, developing and testing technical solutions to reduce exposure risks in commonly found cooking systems of Senegal, as well as conducting lab- and field-testing of new national and international ICSs.


Activity 2.1.2 Expansion of distribution and retail channels

The project will address deficiencies in distribution and retail network with a particular focus on six under-served regions at the sales level. The project will promote collaboration with existing distribution infrastructure of other sectors and facilitate linkage between potential retailers in the regions and the producers and distributors of ICS. Various distribution models including direct sales, third party dealer-distributors, and wholesale markets will be explored to ensure that the target market is reached.

The project will select 650 young people in rural settlements to start retailing ICS in their communities as LMIs. The project will interweave the collaboration with women associations to better reach communities.


Activity 2.1.3 Facilitating access to market-based finance and other sources of capital

EcoEnergy will work with Micro-Finance Institutes (MFIs) and banks to remove “access-to-finance” barriers through individualized training and coaching of the producers. Micro-Finance Institutes and banks will be familiarised with the business and investment potential of the ICS sector based on documents, meetings with ICS producers and distributors, and visits to the field. Producers who have been identified as having a good chance of qualifying loan application documents will be supported to acquire a loan from the MFIs/banks through an intermediate financial institution (IFI). The terms and conditions for loans will be determined by the interested parties in the sector; exact terms and conditions will be determined further during the implementation phase.

### 1.6 Direct project impact

The project will result in an approximate cumulative total of 1,915,000 additional ICS sales in Kenya and 950,000 in Senegal during the 5-year project duration. ICS sales will continue to increase after the end of the project period (2024-2030) with an estimated cumulative total of 618,750 additional ICS units sold (MIOO2eq) during the project period 2020-2024, and an additional 25.6 MIOO2eq in the project influence period until 2030. The project will directly benefit 11.2 million people, including 1.9 million mainly rural households with access to improved cooking stoves and 9.3 million children. It will also limit consumption of non-renewable biomass in the cooking sector.

# Project employment impact

The project is expected to create an estimated 2,741 work opportunities by the project end, including about 705 jobs in KS production sector alone and the rest along the KS distribution/supply chain.

In Kenya, the number of jobs in the supported production sector is expected to increase by 360 at the end of the project. Besides, 2,278 installers and retailers (last-mile entrepreneurs) will be trained and it is estimated that they will create about 15,490 work opportunities in their respective communities. Therefore, the project is expected to create about 1,880 work opportunities total in Kenya.

In Senegal, the number of jobs in the supported production sector is expected to increase by 345 at the end of the project. Besides, 700 installers and retailers (last-mile entrepreneurs) will be trained. Additionally, women groups will be supported for KS. The project is expected to create about 785 work opportunities in the country.

## Project gender dimension and impact

The project has a very strong gender dimension. The cooking energy sector is a sector where women are traditionally the main agents of change, and the support we provide will be designed for a great part on how these women can benefit from it. Therefore, the project particularly benefits women and children by improving indoor air quality, and reducing time and efforts spent on collecting fuel. The project also provides work, income generation and capacity building opportunities for women, and the existing ENERG Initiative projects have been shown to have a positive impact on empowerment of women in both Kenya and Senegal.

1.9 Purpose of the gender assessment and Gender Action Plan

1.9.1 GCF approach for gender mainstreaming

Gender equality is an essential condition for human rights-based, economically and environmentally sustainable and socially just development. It is an objective in itself and a mandatory guiding principle of international and national [development] policy and cooperation.

GCF's Safeguards and Gender Management System and Gender Strategy require a gender assessment in the preparation phase of projects to identify gender equality issues and risks, as well as the specific gender-related risks that need to be prevented or mitigated through specific measures. The results and recommendations of this assessment are taken into account for the objectives, indicators and the results monitoring system of the project.

Despite existing legal principles and binding political commitments, gender equality has not yet been achieved in any country in the world. Women and girls, who generally make up half of the population of partner countries, face a higher risk of poverty than men and boys. When gender-based discrimination exists in the country, region or sector in which the project is implemented, women and girls will be more difficult or impossible to achieve and sustain. In the worst case a project can exacerbate the situation. Therefore, prevention through the application of specific gender-sensitive measures (safeguarding) is necessary.

To achieve positive and sustainable results, it is particularly important to actively promote the achievement of gender equality in all projects. The GCF Gender Action Plan is an integral part of the Safeguards and Gender Management System therefore goes beyond the risk assessment in the sense of a do-no-harm approach and requires additionally, as a binding standard, the assessment of potentials for promoting gender equality.

1.9.2 Gender Equality

The Green Climate Fund has decided to mainstream gender perspectives from the outset of its operations as an essential decision-making element for the deployment of its resources. Its commitment to gender equality centers on gender-responsive climate action programs and projects that benefit women and men. Gender mainstreaming is a key component in all GCF projects, including those funded by the GCF, which include both women and men of all ages as stakeholders in the design, development and implementation of strategies and activities (see financed GCF website: 2018).

1.9.3 Gender assessment

In conformity with the GCF's Gender Policy, the project has undergone a gender assessment to:

- Determine how the project can respond to the needs of women and men in view of the specific climate change issue to be addressed;
- Identify the patterns of change and the gender dynamics in order to achieve the project adaptation or mitigation goals;
- Identify and design the specific gender elements to be included in the project activities;
- Estimate potential impacts on women and men;
- Select output, outcome and impact indicators; and
- Design project implementation and monitoring institutional arrangements.

Following this assessment, a Gender Action Plan is developed which is presented as a separate document. The gender assessment has been developed by means of information from ECHO gender impacts studies and surveys in Kenya and Senegal, complemented by consultations of women beneficiaries and

2 Gender equality situations in Kenya and Senegal

2.1 Macro level socio-economic indicators

UNDP’s Human Development Report (HDR) 2016[1] ranks Kenya 146 out of 188 countries in the medium human development category, and Senegal 162 out of 184 in the low human development category.

The Gender Development Index (GDI) is the ratio of female to male Human Development Index (HDI). The 2016 HDR states that: “Women are more likely than men to be poor, less healthy and less educated. More generally speaking, but not always, women are more vulnerable to relative deprivation due to their disadvantaged position in society because women constitute half the world’s population. The deprivations facing women are the most extreme because they are both a cause of poverty and an effect of it.”[2] Women have shorter life expectancy than men and the fact that in most regions the expected number of years of schooling for girls is similar to that for boys, women consistently have a lower HDI value than do men. Much of the variation in HDIs between countries can be explained by differences in female educational attainment compared with male educational attainment among women relative to men. Part of the variation in the HDI between men and women is generated by barriers to women working outside the home, to accessing education, to voicing their concerns about health care, and to having access to adequate information on reproductive health and “sexual and reproductive health care”. In 2015, Kenya had a GDI of 0.891 and Senegal of 0.886, which is relatively low[3], see details in the table below.

As an indicator for political empowerment, the Human Development Report states that Senegal has 47% of women in parliament (in 2015), Kenya only 20%. The World Economic Forum’s Global Gender Gap report ranks Senegal on world average for political empowerment, while Kenya scores low on this criterion.

Regarding reproductive health and sexual and reproductive health indicators, the HDR 2016[4] states that the HDR concentrates on reproductive health. Gender-differentiated health indicators in both countries are acceptable, and even good for Kenya. However, according to the HDRI Kenya scores low on reproductive health indicators while Senegal scores slightly higher.

As a summary:

* Both countries score clearly below world average on gender equality.
* Kenya scores low on political empowerment, but Senegal reaches world average.


[1] The 2016 report was used since it provides a convenient comparison across outcomes
[2] The largest difference captured by the GDI are in South Asia, where the HDI for women is 17.8 percent lower than the HDI value for men, followed by the Arab States with a 34.4 percent difference and Sub-Saharan Africa with a 25.9 percent difference.
[3] This number has increased slightly after the 2017 elections where, when at least 23 women have been elected to the National Assembly of Kenya (the maximum number allowed by law), the total number of women in parliament will be 48. The 12 nominees by political parties, will bring the women in the National Assembly up to 76. These women have been elected governors.[5] Three other women were also elected as members of women elected members of county assemblies and also as representatives from 84 to 96 of the total 3450 (http://www.theeastafrican.co.ke/news/Women-elected-in-Kenya/2708-405488-dflkzg/index.html, retrieved July 2018).
