## Gender equality situation in Kenya

Unless stated otherwise, most information on women’s position is from the OECD Development Centre's Social Institutions and Policies (SIP) country profiles, which provide comprehensive qualitative information on legal, cultural, and traditional laws and practices that discriminate against women and girls (<www.genderinfo.org>). Other reports on country gender situations which were available online often built upon the SIP indicators already quoted above, or else were dating back to before 2010, and were rarely used. The SIP often uses data from the USAID Demographic and Health Survey program (DHS).

Data for 2016 have been used as a comprehensive dataset is available for comparison across countries.


---
**Page 174**
---

2.2.1 Background

Kenya’s population is estimated at 48 million (about 9.5m households, 2016) and is growing at annual average of 2.7%. 78% of the population is in the rural regions and 22% in the urban area. 33% of urban dwellers and 51% in the rural areas (45% of the population) live under the poverty line on less than US $2 a day. Poverty is higher in rural areas where 70% of Kenyans live. The economy is based largely on agriculture, which includes household-based enterprises, subsistence agriculture and a small wage sector. A large proportion of the country’s population is engaged in agriculture (32%) on family owned land while 7.7% percent have no work. Most rural households (area 60kha) work on family agricultural holdings with low or no pay employment. (GCF Concept Note.)

Kenya is a multi-ethnic state, composed of 47 counties. The largest ethnic group living in Central Province are the Luo in Western; Kalenjin, Turkana, Maasai and Kipsigis in Rift Valley; Kamba and Kikuyu in Nairobi; Kamba and Meru in Eastern; Somali in North Eastern; and Mijikenda and Arab at the Coast. Most Kenyans are Christians (over 80%), Muslims are a minority of 15% (KNBS 2009 Census). Christians are distributed across the country, with the largest concentration residing in Coast, North Eastern (37%) and the uplands of Rift Valley (50%) regions. Muslims at the Coast make about 50% of the entire population in the area (ICA, Pocha, 2013).

2.2.2 Institutions and norms

a) Family law

Characteristic for Kenya’s institutional situation is the progressive 2010 Constitution which guarantees, among others, equal rights for men and women. The National Gender Equality Commission (NGEC) was established in August 2014 to ensure that all laws are gender-sensitive. However, there remains a gap between the law on paper and its implementation. Women, men, persons with disabilities, the youth, children, the elderly, minorities and marginalized communities. However, it remains difficult in Kenya to mainstream gender in practice. Customary law as well as the Muslim personal law (Sharia) are still widely practiced in Kenya. These laws are patriarchal in nature or inheritance, with usually less equal provisions than in the law. Controversial issues in Kenya which may impact on women’s rights include polygamy, child marriage, early and forced marriages, sometimes ethnically driven, the use of customary law, the poor reliability of the police force, land disputes and forced displacement of indigenous populations related to the colonial past, and gender-based violence.

b) Family code

The 2010 Kenyan Constitution gives equal rights to parties to a marriage between a woman and a man. The 2013 Marriage Bill gives equal legal recognition to all types of marriages, provides for a simplified procedure for matrimonial matter, and allows for the registration of marriages performed under customary law. The bill makes it much easier for married women to understand their rights. Since April 2014, polygamy is legal in Kenya.

Parental authority is equally shared by men and women and there are no legal grounds to prevent unmarried or separated women from becoming heads of households. However, some customary marriage practices are discriminatory in this matter as the custody of children is sometimes awarded to the father where a customary marriage ends in divorce.

The Kenyan law provides equal inheritance rights to women and men. However, in practice, these rights are not adequately fulfilled. (Divorced and separated women are vulnerable; both formal and informal court systems discriminate against women (adapted from DIEC, 2014).


---
**Page 175**
---

### c) Physical integrity

There is no specific law against domestic violence. According to the country’s previous two reports toCEDAW, police are frequently reluctant to intervene when they consider to be a “Family matter.” According to a study conducted by the Kenya National Bureau of Statistics (KNBS), 40% of Kenyan women have experienced physical violence at least once in their lifetime. The study also found that 35% of Kenyan men had been involved in intimate partner violence acceptance. Given five reasons why a man might be justified in beating his wife, 63.7% agreed with at least one reason. Men were also less likely to support wife beating for each of the five reasons.

Under the Sexual Offences Act (2001), all forms of sexual assault are criminalized. Under the Act, consent cannot be given by a person under the age of 18 years and any form of sexual intercourse with a minor is prohibited. However, no specific law addresses rape in marriage, nor is it clear whether the perpetrator can escape prosecution for rape by marrying the victim.

A 2008/2009 household survey showed that 60% of women who have been married, divorced or separated have experienced physical violence at least once in their lifetime. The same survey also found that 45% of women reported sexual violence against their will. 41% of women reported experience physical and/or sexual violence in their lifetime.

The police and the justice system rarely prosecute cases of rape, and local women’s rights organizations claim that police are reluctant to investigate rape cases, and procedures in handling rape cases act as a significant deterrent to reporting.

The culture of silence around gender-based violence makes both reporting acts of violence and collection of data on gender-based violence difficult. The 2013/2014 KNBS report on election violence in Kenya in 2008 and 2013, the 2010 report of the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) states that much of the 2008 sexual violence was ethnically driven.

Sexual harassment is addressed under the Sexual Offences Act and in the Employment Law. But according to the US Department of State, women are often reluctant to report sexual harassment because they fear retaliation or retribution from their employers.

The law does not allow abortion; however doctors have been permitted to carry out abortions in cases where the woman’s life was in danger. A Reproductive Health and Rights Bill, which would allow abortion in cases of rape, as well as safeguard the reproductive health of women and girls in Kenya, has been under consideration since 2010.

### d) Sex bias

There is evidence to suggest that Kenya is a country of low concern in relation to missing women. The gender gap in primary and primary education is very small, in detriment of girls. The trend is more significant in secondary education. Data on the gender division of household chores between girls and boys were not available. Based on 2002-2013 data from UNICEF, child labour slightly affected males more than female children. Maternal data from 2009 indicates that male children were more vulnerable in this regard than Kenyan women.

### e) Access to resources and assets

The Constitution guarantees equal enjoyment of ownership rights for all Kenyan citizens. Women are free to buy, own and sell assets as they choose. However, in practice women’s access to land and access to property other than land are severely restricted by customary law, which prohibits women from owning or inheriting land and often requires that women obtain permission from their husbands before selling or mortgaging land. This custom prevents them from using it as collateral for bank loans or from disposing of it as they see fit. In the event of her husband’s death, this “interest” disappears upon remarriage. According to the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO), women in Kenya are less likely than men to own livestock or land; when they do acquire assets in their own name, their husbands often act as intermediaries in the transaction.


---
**Page 176**
---

There have been several recent advances in legislation addressing women’s land rights. The National Land Policy adopted in December 2009 calls for the government to repeal existing laws and outlaw regulations, customs and practices constituting discrimination against women and children in relation to land ownership. In 2012, the National Land Commission Act was enacted (Act No.6 of 2012; Land Act; and Act No.3 of 2012: Land Registration Act). The 2012 National Land Commission Act established a National Land Commission for the management of land in Kenya, in part to remove the current discriminatory practices against women in the formal and informal court systems.

Since they rarely have assets of their own, Kenyan women historically have had trouble providing the collateral required by lending institutions, making it difficult for women to access financial services. According to the latest data from the World Bank (2011), 39% of women had accounts at formal financial institutions, compared to 45.6% of men; while the percentage of men who had loans was 78%, compared with only 55.5% for women. In terms of access to credit, 41.2% of women – 8.8% (1) However, women do make up over half (53.81%) of borrowers at microfinance institutions.

### c) Civil Liberties

There do not appear to be any legal constraints on women’s freedom of movement and access to public space in Kenya beyond existing requirements that women secure their husband’s or father’s consent before obtaining a passport have been removed. However, of the women interviewed for the 2008-2009 DHS, 26.3% reported that they had difficulty traveling alone by train or bus because they were allowed to travel only with family and relatives. The freedom of movement is further restricted on account of periodic internal displacement. In 2011 shadow report to CEDAW, forced evictions in Kenya’s slums and informal settlements are common, and women are particularly vulnerable to forced removals. Women have the same right as men to pass Kenyan citizenship onto their children.

Women and men enjoy the same rights to vote and stand for election in Kenya, although standing for public office often faces hostility. Kenyans indicate that they believe that men and women are equally capable as politicians, but women’s participation is limited compared to the national average of 15% in terms of political participation.

Under the Employment Act (2007), female employees in Kenya are entitled to three months of paid maternity leave if their employers can pay up to 100% of their wages during their leave. Fathers are entitled to two weeks paternity leave.


Source: https://www.genderinfo.org/wp-content/uploads/files/DataSheets/VI.pdf

g) Other information

The latest report from the National Quality Council (NGQC) has produced a 2018 report which gives a very good overview of the equality situation in Kenya by means of extensive data collection and data surveys, as well as a well-written analysis, and which provided good background information. This report is interesting in that it provides a lot more detail than the previous reports. However, the data are not presented in maps and a geographic analysis would require more time than was available.

The role of the NGQC is to mainstream the needs of Special Interest Groups (SIGs), a definition which encompasses people with disabilities (the latter including people with sickness), children, youth (people aged 18-35 years old) and women. The SIGs are defined as groups that have been historically marginalized or excluded from participation in society by virtue of their age, gender, race, ethnicity, religion, sexual orientation, disability, or other status. The SIGs include people as defined in international policy.

GCF funding proposal: "Promotion of Climate-Friendly Cooking in Kenya and Senegal" Gender Assessment – 22 January 2019

Homosexuality is considered illegal in the Kenyan law, but some observers state that it is not punished in practice anymore, and that the definition of “marginalised groups” for the NIGEC could be understood as including homosexuals.

HIV has a high rate of prevalence in Kenya. EnDev Kenya has continuously carried out awareness raising on HIV in its program activities, during trainings, and stove building meetings.

## 2.3 Gender equality situation in Senegal

### 2.3.1 Background

Senegal is one of the world’s least developed countries. The economy relies heavily on cash crops and fishing, both of which are vulnerable to climate change. Those living in urban areas have far better access to resources than those in rural areas; according to WFP11, a quarter of people living in the capital, Dakar, are poor, while 50% of those in rural areas are poor. The country has one of the highest rates of child mortality and poverty rates, sitting at 46%. Overall, 17 percent of people face food insecurity, and so too – mostly rural – parts of the country, the prevalence of global acute malnutrition is critical. Rapid population growth (2.5% per year) coupled with a high dependency ratio (80%) puts pressure on natural resources, as well as vulnerability to climate change (source: GIZ; GCF Concept Note).

Senegal is also a multi-ethnic society. The majority groups (Peuls, Toucouleurs and Sarakolais) are mostly Muslim and live in the north of Senegal. Smaller ethnic groups live in the rainforest region in southern Senegal [Forest people], near the borders with Mali or Guinea-Bissau. Foreigners or “Nairs” mainly from Mauritania, Algeria, Morocco, Gambia, Guinea- Bissau, and Côte d’Ivoire are mostly living in urban centers (Source: Laval University, http://www.adf.univ-laval.ca/sgp/pdf/senegal.pdf).

### 2.3.2 Institutions and norms

#### a) Overview

Senegal’s 1960 constitution guarantees equality between women and men. A National Strategy for Gender Equality and Health has been developed, to run from 2005-2015. The country has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All forms of Discrimination against Women (in 1983), and the Optional Protocol on violence against women (2007). However, despite these laws, gender equality is not fully respected in Senegal. The Senegalese Constitution, but customary practices still exist. The Family Code grants most rights solely to men.

The GIZ reports that in families touched by poverty, women and girls are disproportionately affected by gender inequality, with girls leaving school earlier so that their brothers can continue their education. Discriminatory practices, especially in the domains of the family and inheritance, persist, although Senegal has successfully passed a law on the equal division of property between men and women.

While in urban areas laws protecting women are generally respected, rural areas are still dominated by customary and religious practices, and few women are aware of the legal rights that are in place to protect them.

The constitution and law provide for both freedom of assembly and association, although the government has at times restricted the former. Protests staged in the run-up to the presidential election in 2012 were broken up using violent methods, resulting in the injury and/or arrest of many protesters, and the deaths of nine


---
**Page 178**
---

people. Public permits for civil society and political demonstrations were regularly denied or given delayed responses.

Freedom of expression is protected by the Constitution and is generally respected. However, journalists have been targeted for criticizing of the government and in months preceding the 2012 presidential election, a variety of media outlets (both male and female) were harassed, threatened, or physically harmed were documented.


**b)** Family code

The Family Code of 1977 provides a minimum age at marriage of 18 for women and 21 for men. The payment of dowry is prohibited. Although forced and early marriages are banned in the family code, it is having sexual relations with girls under the age of 13, such marriages are reportedly quite common. The 2010-2015 Demographic and Health Survey (DHS) reports that among women ages 15–49, 28.6% were married, divorced or widowed.

Many marriages are customary or religious and thus are not registered. Registered marriages can be either monogamous or polygamous, in which case the husband may marry up to four women. According to the 2003-2004 DHS, 15.8% of married women were living with a husband who had more than one wife.

The Senegalese Family Code grants parental authority solely to the father, and women are unable to take legal responsibility for their children. The father is legally considered the head of the household, is responsible for all domestic decisions, and has the right to sell family property without his wife's consent or knowledge in place of residence. When it comes to decision-making, husbands also primarily make choices regarding important household purchases. When it comes to decisions regarding the use of women’s earnings, however, women have more say than they do when it comes to other decisions. Women are not considered recognized head of household if her husband formally renounces his authority in court. The proportion of households headed by women stood at 23%, as of 2010-2011. In the event of divorce, the issue of child custody is decided according to Islamic law. If a woman has children from a previous marriage and they are not recognized as her husband’s children (this type of diverse rarely occur outside urban areas), the court often grants custody to the mother while requiring the father to participate in their upkeep.

There are three legal systems that coexist under the Senegalese regime: civil law and Islamic Sharia law. While living, individuals must choose which system they prefer; the default regime is the civil code. Civil law inheritance is more favorable to widows and daughters, granting them the same rights as sons. It is this form of inheritance that is most common in rural areas, where it is also the dominant legal system under Islamic Sharia law. In some cases, women are expected to marry the deceased husband’s brother, or expected to become part of a son household.

Reconciliation is the only way for a divorced wife to divorce her husband unilaterally and without warning, explicitly prohibited by law and women are able to request a civil divorce in the same way as a man. However, women may not remarry until a period of 300 days has passed since the dissolution of the previous marriage. In Islamic marriages, however, there is no such prohibition on remarriage; indeed, it is quite common. Divorce is more frequent under Islamic law than civil law, notwithstanding the secular nature of Senegalese civil law, the most frequent cause of divorce is repudiation. Women who request divorce, especially in customary marriages, may be required to reimburse their husband for expenses incurred during marriage, unless the reason for divorce is due to the husband abandoning the household or if he is sick or infertile.


**c)** Physical integrity

The revision of the 1999 Penal Code introduced a clause on domestic violence. Victims are, however, often reluctant to report cases – preferring to appeal to their families to help resolve the issue – and even when


---
**Page 179**
---

they do, the police rarely intervene and few cases reach court. There have been critiques of judges, who fail to adequately apply the domestic violence law. Accurate up-to-date data as to the prevalence of domestic violence is not available, although data on attitudes suggest the latter is widely accepted. The 2010-2011 Demographic Health Survey (DHS) found that 35% of women aged 15–49 had experienced physical or sexual violence by a current or former partner in their lifetime. Of these, only 6% reported the incident to police, and beating her for one of the proposed reasons. Rape is also criminalised under the 1999 revised penal code, but spousal rape is not recognised. Very few rape cases are taken to court; and of those that are few result in a conviction. The majority of women who report being raped do so to their families or friends rather than the police. Women who report sexual violence often face social stigma and discrimination. Many cases remain unpunished and were released without facing a trial. In part, this is because sexual violence and rape remain taboo topics and cases are often settled out of court.

Senegal's penal code was revised in January 1999 to include a clause specifically regarding sexual harassment. While some progress has been made on the issue, it remains very problematic, and the law not effectively enforced. Women’s rights groups have lamented the fact that providing sufficient proof to secure prosecution is difficult because of the lack of forensic evidence.

Female genital mutilation (FGM) has been illegal under the Senegalese Penal Code since 1999. It is still practiced, however, and is more common among Muslims and several ethnic groups than among Christians. According to the 2010-2011 DHS, 25% of women aged 15–49 had undergone the procedure, but these rates range from as low as 3% in some regions (e.g., Casamance) to as high as 68% in others (e.g., Fatick and Mbacké). On the one hand, this practice is often linked to acceptance, respectability and marriage for young girls. On the other hand, it is also associated with a lack of education and poverty (especially among Mandingue). The DHS reports that only 16.6% of women feel the practice should continue, compared to 78.7% who this should be abandoned. There also appears to be considerable political will to challenge and eradicate FGM. In recent years, Senegal has been working closely with national and international NGOs on campaigns to encourage abandonment of FGM.

The DHS reports that 30% of women in a union declare having an unmet need for family planning (among all women, this percentage is 19%). The average age at first birth among women aged 25–45 is 21. Abortion is only legal in cases where the pregnant woman’s life is in danger.

### d) Sex bias

There is evidence to suggest that Senegal is a country of no concern in relation to missing women, infant and early childhood mortality rates are higher for boys than for girls, so are infant and under-five mortality rates. The high cost of education means that, in poor families, girls’ education is often sacrificed to enable their brothers to continue schooling. UNICEF similarly reports that although gender parity has been achieved in primary school, there remains a significant gap at the secondary level: 50% of boys were enrolled as compared with 27% of girls who were enrolled in secondary education, and for every 100 boys, there were approximately 79 girls.

### e) Access to resources and assets

Until the Constitutional Act of 1981, women were not allowed to own land in Senegal, but since 2001 women and men have equal property rights with regard to land ownership, and there have been government campaigns to encourage leaders in rural communities to ensure that this law is respected. Women are legally entitled to acquire land through inheritance or purchase, and can also obtain it by marriage. However, they do not always own their own property after marriage. The default marital property regime for civil marriages in the separation of property, and it is the originally owner who legally administers property during the marriage. However, women have greater access to agricultural inputs and, more generally, land and other resources. In addition, where


---
**Page 180**
---

The dowry system of marriage applies, any property, assets deposited at the bank, alms, etc. given to the woman upon marriage are to be handed over to her husband.

Land is allocated—at least on paper—through local administrative processes in Senegal that do not officially recognize land sales or bequests. Land instead belongs to the national domain and local land committees arbitrate disputes. Women can inherit land through their parents, grandparents, and other members of the aforementioned land committees, and as such rarely received land through this allocation mechanism.

Customary practices relating to land ownership where discrimination against women are specifically banned under the constitution, and yet in practice continue to limit women’s access to land.

Women have better access to credit than they did 10 years ago. However, but in reality women often struggle to obtain loans: because women tend to lack use, but not ownership of land, they are unable to provide the security needed to secure loans. Many farming cooperatives do not recognize women as producers on their own right, meaning that they cannot access the credit and other services provided by these cooperatives.

As mentioned for Kenya under section 2.2.2.h., women have however good access to loans from micro-finance institutions in Senegal.

Relatively low levels of French literacy also complicate access to bank loans and credit: French is the official language of the country and yet only 10 or 25% of Senegalese women speak the latter (compared to 15-20% of men).

### f) Civil Liberties

Regarding access to public space, the constitution and law provide for freedom of movement. However, married women do not have the right to choose where to live, in this right falls exclusively to their husbands.

Survey data suggests that daily movements are constrained. According to the 2010-2011 Demographic and Health Survey (DHS), only 39.5% of women aged 15–49 years report being able to travel alone without a male chaperone, while 67.8% say they can visit family and relatives. In addition, 39.9% of women agreed her husband is justified in beating them if they go out too late at night.

There is no specific law on discrimination against women’s associations. Women's rights groups are, however, very active in Senegal, leading vocal campaigns against police failure to prosecute perpetrators in cases of violence against women, as well as encouraging communities to abandon FGM.

Women have been able to make significant progress since the adoption of the 2004 law on the equality between men and women. The law was adopted following a series of actions by Senegalese political parties, women’s organizations and NGOs who pushed for a gender quota in electoral lists. While a law on gender parity on candidate lists was passed in 2007, it was later ruled as unconstitutional by the Constitutional Council.

Women have made significant progress since the adoption of the 2004 law on equality between men and women in Electoral Lists, and in the 2012 elections that followed 64 women were elected as members of the 150-seat parliament (or 42.7% of seats), up from 13 after the previous election.

Concerning working rights, there are no laws in Senegal providing for equal pay based on gender in hiring, promotion or dismissal. Employers must, however, grant women an equivalent position upon their return from maternity leave. Women in Senegal are entitled to 14 weeks of paid maternity leave financed by the government, but not many women work in the informal sector and are not entitled to such leave.

### g) Other Information

Homosexuality is considered illegal in Senegal. The punishment for same-sex relations is 1 to 5 years of imprisonment, plus a fine 100000 to 1000000 francs CFA.

3 Gender in the ICS sector

### Problem statement

The lack of energy is experienced differently by men and women and entails different consequences. The use and access to modern energy which impacts women and men in varying ways. In developing countries, wood, charcoal or animal and plant residues serve as main energy sources for cooking and heating. Women play a central role in this prevailing energy system as perpetual traditional division of labor within the household often places them at the center of energy-related activities such as collecting firewood and other services. However, in many societies women and men also have differing access to decision-making and financial resources within the household: Women’s access to decision-making within the household and community is limited which can influence their ability to influence processes related to resource allocation on including energy (Idrèv – Kinyanjui, 2013).

The “conventional wisdom” in the energy sector is that women benefit from biomass use in the kitchen from wood or charcoal. However, this is not always true as there are many women who potential low income generating activities, from direct income generation activities as builders and sellers of cookstoves, increased safety when they do not need to go out to collect wood.

The objective of the present gender analysis was to collect data to confirm how these statements apply for beneficiaries in Senegal and Kenya. Identify possible gender-related barriers to successful project implementation, and opportunities for further improving gender equity in the sector.


### Overview of available gender surveys

Basic information was collected from 3 surveys that collected gender-disaggregated data from the existing EnDev cooperative projects, and included systematic indicators and data collection.

#### 2015 EnDev survey for Senegal based on a sample of 47 ICS users in the 2015 EnDev Senegal FACILITY project

The 2015 EnDev survey for Senegal gathered baseline information from 47 ICS cooperatives in the PERABGDO program by conducting interviews. For the ICS component, interviews were conducted with women: 23 users of ICS, one president of a women’s group; and 11 potters producing inserts for the ICS “tambour”. Further interviews were conducted with 5 men and 4 women in the gender report. The survey was conducted in the Bassin Arcachier area of Senegal (EnDev Senegal, 2015).

#### Gender survey prepared in 2017 for EnDev Kenya

A detailed survey of 108 households and 177 SMEs was conducted for EnDev Kenya in 2017. The table below shows the gender distribution among the 177 SME respondents were working in the stove business (the remaining were working in solar panels only), out of which 97 were female and 72 were male. It is not clear from the survey result if the interviewed SMEs were engaged in production, marketing, customer sales or other activities along the chain; but from the responses it is obvious that many were engaged in production also.


---
**Page 182**
---

# Gender aspects of stove usage

The 2013 Senegal gender survey confirmed the health, comfort and time-saving benefits of cookstove usage, adding that:

- cooking on an ICS saves clothes by avoiding them to get dirty.
- cooking in an ICS makes it easier to carry out other domestic activities
- the time saved daily by cooking on an ICS and not collecting fuel is not used for income generating activities, but rather for other domestic work in the house, finishing work earlier in the evening, and recreational activities (such as watching TV for instance).

The 2017 Kenya gender survey noted that:

- for 70% of respondents, women were the key decision makers for acquiring stoves; this was acknowledged both by male and female household respondents. Whoever was the key decision


---
**Page 183**
---

maker, the respondents also acknowledged that they consulted their spouses before making the decision to buy a stove.

- despite the women being seen as key decision makers, men more often make the payments for stoves, reportedly because they have access to credit (and) hence are responsible for family spending.

Cookstove users consulted for this assessment in Kenya in 2018 confirmed the many benefits of the stoves. Women expressed ideas and wishes about possible improvements of the stoves. The local EnDev teams regularly listen to such wishes and check if the proposed ideas are favourable in terms of energy saving.

No gender-related negative impacts or barriers to stove dissemination were identified in the available gender studies and the consultations. In more than 10 years of project work, there were no observations on gender-specific barriers that could be linked to stoves. Studies including external evaluations have produced no evidence for a specific problem of female households.

In Senegal, village based women groups are at the centre of the proposed dissemination approach. Female-headed households are known for their participation in women groups, as they require the solidarity and support from other group members. Women groups also provide training to each other group members and even better chances than non-members of the women-groups. Furthermore, both country programs will work with NGOs specialised on gender issues.

Some limited studies have done research opportunities to changing gender roles in the households. The 2013 Senegal survey highlighted that regarding the division of labor, it is essential to raise the awareness of men towards the hard work of women[1]. During the consultations held in 2018 in Kenya and Senegal, men did not mention any significant challenges during their participation in cooking. In Kenya, men did not have a problem with the kitchen as they had already cooked while being a bachelor. Consulted women stove users did not express a willingness for these roles to change (but consulted stove producers did, see below). In the Lwa area where the consultations were held, he expression for "being able to cook" was considered as a sign of femininity.

### 3.4 Gender aspects of stove production

#### 3.4.1 Gender organization in stove production

In both Kenya and Senegal, pottery is traditionally a women's skill. Women are mostly organized as women's groups of up to 40 persons (sometimes including a few men), who work as potters. All metal workers are men. In the larger private workshops, all business owners are men, which may explain why between 5-20 casual

1) According to the study, this can be achieved through meetings and sessions that address gender issues or through activities that allow women to participate in the production process. This could include training on how to use the stoves, as well as on the collection of firewood. To motivate people to get involved, competitions could be organized. The former GIZ project ProCooP organized a contest for couples in which the man who helped their wives the most in the rice production was rewarded with a stove. This competition was very successful and led to an increase in the number of stoves sold by local markets. Looking at the budget constraints for "soft intervention", it will be feasible to include dedicated gender interventions, but these should not be prioritized over other activities that are more cost-effective (e.g., training on gender roles). Considering that there are 13,000 villages in Senegal, for example, this would have a huge additional budget requirement. The GOF country projects will aim to leverage gender support through these cooperation partners outside the GOF financial resources.

27


---
**Page 184**
---

workers. Most of these are men, some of whom work on the pottery and others on the metal. A few women also work in these workshops. In Kenya, one woman worked together with her husband who is a business owner, often managing the workshop for him.

The consulted private producers said they learned the pottery skill from their mothers. They also employed a few older, older women to benefit from their experience. In Kenya, at least one woman is known to work as a blacksmith.


There are also many people who produce stoves independently, often next to another activity such as farming. These products can be men or as well as women.

According to the Kenyan survey, the time spent in production often differ, because they perform different types of activities and do not spend the same amount of time on the work. The Kenyan study showed (RwL, 2016), that 7.5 per cent of men earn more than EUR 270 per month, whereas only three percent of the women earn this much. In Kenya, women are more likely to be involved in production activities and the vast majority of men belong to this lowest income group. However, men usually work longer hours in their businesses while women instead are often occupied with household activities that do not create income. Especially in rural areas, where most women grow food provides women with their own source of income and a possibility of more independence (EdOv., 2016).


### Results from Senegal 2013 survey

In the city of Kaolack in Senegal, two women groups, respectively six and twelve women as well as one blacksmith group were interviewed. In total, twenty-two women were interviewed. Among these eleven women interviewed, ten were already active in the production of pottery before engaging in the production of ceramic inserts. The other five women started to produce ceramic inserts because they were producing of ceramic inserts due to its profitability and with the support of Erdev have engaged in the production of ceramic inserts. The decision of producing ceramic inserts was taken by the women themselves (in eight out of eleven cases). In three out of eleven cases, the women had already been involved in other activities before engaging to produce ceramic inserts but had not chosen it for their husbands' permissions, except for one woman being a widow. The women of the women's group Groupement d'Intérêts Economique de Femmes (GIEF) were interviewed by two interviewers. The first interviewer was a female relative who assumed by a female relative, allowing the interviewees to rest after coming from work. The other two interviewers bear the full burden of domestic chores themselves and perform their household tasks before or after the interviews. They also have to prepare the lunch for all participants in order to facilitate the preparation of meals, cleaning and other chores before going to work. One could therefore argue that the production of ceramic inserts increases the women's workload and in at least two cases extends their working day by one hour (from 6 a.m. until 7 p.m.). However, it is important to note that these women becoming involved in the production of ceramic inserts, thus the increased workload is not directly related to the production itself but rather to the fact that they have to spend more hours of labor within their households. Moreover, in eight out of eleven cases, it was the decision of the woman herself to produce ceramic inserts and all interviewees asserted the importance for a woman to earn money.

A woman who earns money is highly valued in Senegalese society and especially the art of pottery is very appreciated by the interviewee's family members. To perform an income generating activity is of high


---
**Page 185**
---

importance for the women interviewed. Nine out of eleven reporters noted that in a Sengalese household, both the husband and wife have to contribute to the household's income. This is the women's definition of gender equality. However, the idea of a man doing housework is completely rejected.

Compared to the income of a blacksmith, a typical man's job, or a potter, a typical woman's job earn more. Amongst the blacksmiths have higher material costs and more competition on the market.


### 3.4.3 Surveys from 2017

The survey surveyed 170 entrepreneurs, of which 97 women and 72 men, without clearly stating how many were working in production. Unfortunately, the data on the roles of men and women in production were not very clear. The majority (85%) of the information came from surveys. Not all the study mention if producers were organized in groups, or individually.

At production sites, women are a minority. In all visited sites (the name and location of sites is not mentioned in the methodology) women comprised less than 85% of membership, except in Murang'a. The roles of women at these sites are varied: some are involved as part-time workers; others are employed full time; they engage employ on a casual basis and few men who are members of the group to do strenuous work like digging or carrying out other heavy tasks.

84% of respondents use the occasional services of other persons in stove production. Some male LME's sought services of outsiders, while women were comfortable working with LME's in their group.


Women prefer to work with fellow women than with men and youths.

In production centers in Lake Victoria and Western clusters of counties, women are the majority because "most of them have children" compared to the Central cluster where the majority are men (unclear if these data come from the survey itself).

There were no gender differences in record keeping. The literate members of the group were assisted by other members.

Most groups spend more time per week working on their stove business, because women need to attend to their family obligations. However, 10% of women entrepreneurs spend more than 50 hours per week on the business, arguing that they are passionate about the job. One of the female respondents has become an "engineer."

All LME respondents are satisfied with their job, without significant gender differences in the reason behind their satisfaction, which is mainly income generation.

One woman acknowledged that she does not depend on her husband anymore for money. Two women acknowledge that stove construction is an achievement for them since the act of construction is often seen as a man's affair.


### 3.4.4 Interviews with women producers in Kenya in 2018

Consultations with a group of women producers in Kenya yielded mostly similar results as the other surveys, with the following exceptions or slight differences:

- the group of 15 women were organized as independent producers working on the same spot and helping each other at turn;
- this organization allowed the women to work more flexibly around household chores;
- the heaviest tasks were performed by hiring young men;


---
**Page 186**
---

- the women did not mention having to ask for their husband’s permission, which is consistent with the differences in the Kenyan and Senegalese family codes;
- some interviewees said that their husbands sometimes cooked for them when they came home late from the stove production workshop.
- consulted producers estimated that a blacksmith would earn more than a potter.

### 3.4 Gender aspects of distribution

In Senegal, the EASEN project has supported implementation of a distribution network for ICS with 179 men (mostly kitchenware retailers) and 186 women (mostly promoters).

In Kenya, the 2017 survey of LME’s showed that:

- Customers buy stoves equally from women and men
- Men can spend more time than women selling solar products out of the house (solar products require night-time sales), but this does not seem an issue for ICS
- Attendance at meetings is good and does not depend on gender.
- Women LME’s have done more demonstrations than men [Solar LME’s] have done more demonstrations than ICS LME’s]
- Entrepreneurs in the solar business are sometimes required to travel and stay away overnight, which is a particular challenge for women. Some of these entrepreneurs have allowed their spouses to spend time away from the house after they have improved their sales.
- Strategies for reaching out to customers differ between male and women entrepreneurs, women appreciate more visits, phone calls, chats, and home visits.

13% out of 270 LME’s targeted women as selling drivers because they are the primary users, are more available at home, and sometimes because they are considered as being able to convince their spouses; 44% or 270 target both genders.


### 3.5 Gender aspects of training

Training is an important component of the project. The 2017 Kenya survey showed that:

- Women think that men do not like to be trained by a woman
- Beneficiaries were more satisfied by the work of the stove training, which are generally conducted at ward or sub-county level, as opposed to the trainings (this is not the case for solar trainings which require more traveling and are more difficult to attend for women)
- Persons selected to train other LME’s were also satisfied even if location was further; people enjoyed the practical training.

A meeting held in 2018 with individual women stove builders in Kisumu showed that women still lack confidence in entrepreneurship and are eager for receiving more dedicated training.


### 3.6 Assessment and conclusions

The assessment of the collected information is reported in section 5, “Summary gender assessment”.

# Gender in the sectoral institutions and implementing organizations

## Project organization

### Component 1: Kenya

The project Executing Entities (EE) in Kenya are the Ministry of Energy (MoE), SNV, and GIZ/EnDev Kenya. GIZ/EnDev coordinates projects activities at overall sector level.

#### Sector coordination
- Coordination of the Project Coordination Committee on regular basis
- Consultation of the Project Advisory Board on regular basis
- Coordination of the implementation of national standards
- Contribution to the implementation of the stove regulation and the national stove standards
- Coordination of the implementation of the national awareness campaigns
- Installation and promotion of stove training and awareness creation

#### Professionalization of ICS sector
- (Co) Investment into production assets
- (Co) Investment into distribution assets
- Linkage and capacity development to enable access to finance
- Technical assistance for quality assurance, testing and branding
- Advice on work norms (social, health and environmental standards)
- Coordinating Quality assurance, quality control, testing and branding
- Extension services

The Ministry of Energy (MoE) has 16 energy centres throughout the country with 160 staff members. Since 2009, the MoE is the lead political partner of EnDev and is chairing the EnDev Project Steering Committee (PSC). The MoE has the following roles in the ICS sector:
- Provision of technical assistance to energy centres
- Development of the Kenyan ICS standard, coordinated by the Kenyan Bureau of Standards
- Development of Regulations for improved cookstoves
- MoE is the chair or the co-chairman on clean cookstoves committees

The MoE has the following roles in project coordination:
- Chairing the Project Advisory Board of the project
- Finalization and implementation of policy and regulation via the 47 county governments
- Development (jointly with EnDev, SNV, Ministry of Health) of awareness and behaviour change messaging materials and coordination of the implementation via the counties governments and via the local associations
- Implementation of awareness creation measures and stove demonstration/exhibition through their 16 energy centres
- Contribution to training and capacity development of stove producers and distributors
- Provision of the platform for coordination, collaboration and knowledge sharing between the two GIZ projects in Kenya and SNV

SNV Development Organisation (the Netherlands) is a non-profit international development organisation. SNV is working with GCF Enlarge programme in Kenya since 2012, SNV is implementing the Sector Support project, aiming at coordinating the cooking sector of Kenya and strengthening the Clean Cooking Alliance of Kenya (CCAK). SNV will work in collaboration with banks, MFIs and other financial institutions for access to finance. SNV will be responsible for:

### 4.1.2 Component 2: Senegal

In Senegal, this are GIZ/DevSe Senegal and several larger NGOs for regional interventions.

National level activities and interventions will be implemented by GIZ/DevSe Senegal as the leading EE of the project. This entails all activities concerning the professionalization of the ICS production and large scale distribution, as well as the measure to improve the coordination of the sector.

GIZ/DevSe Senegal has the following tasks (among others):

- Information of concerned ministries (including field visits)
- Meetings of stakeholders of the ICS sector (including annual "stove camp")
- Policy advice, development of policy documents and relevant studies
- Development and application of monitoring system for the ICS sector and its contribution to the NDC targets
- National Media campaigns (TV, radio)

**Professionalization of ICS producers:**

- (Co-)Investment into production and distribution assets
- Technical assistance on stove production, workshop organization
- Improvements of stove design and production processes, materials and tooling
- Advice on work norms (social and health standards)
- Extension services for the sector
- Linkage to finance sector (improve access to credit)

For reaching out to most of the 31,000 villages in the 58 regions of Senegal for awareness raising and last mile marketing support, the Senegal groups have been grouped into clusters of 2–4 regions. The project activities in these clusters are coordinated by a cluster manager (e.g., Cluster Manager Unit, CMU) and the GIZ. The EE have been selected together with the Ministry of Petroleum and Energy via a competitive Expression of Interest process, and have undertaken due diligence.

- ENDA ENERGIE works on behalf of 30 villages in their region. Their work is focusing on access to energy, climate change, desinterfication and gender in energy'.
- ENDA EDP25 has 15 years of experiences in good governance in rural areas of Senegal. They work intensively with local authorities to improve the quality of their services to the communities.

### 4.1.3 General project governing structure

**Project Coordination Board**

The project uses an already existing Evolve governing board structure for the overall governance of all activities. Members of the board are representatives of the Directorate-General for International Cooperation of the Dutch Ministry of Foreign Affairs (MFA NL), the German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ), the French Agency for Development (AFD), the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), the Swiss International Development (SIDF), the Swiss Agency for Development and Cooperation (SDC), and the Swedish International Development Cooperation Agency (SIDA). Meetings take place twice per year. GIZ is invited to these meetings for regular reporting on project impacts, outcomes, outputs and implementation progress.

# Summary gender assessment

The analysis of available documents, surveys, and interviews yields an array of conclusions on gender-related aspects which are relevant for the sector and project and will be used as a basis to develop the Gender Action Plan.

## a) Gender mainstreaming capacity in the sector

1. The countries and energy sectors have gender policies, targets and programs, but knowledge and experience vary.
2. The EnDev team projects have a very good track record of engaging with both genders at grassroots level.
3. Both EnDev country teams have staff who is trained and experienced in gender mainstreaming. GIZ headquarters also have learned and experienced staff in gender mainstreaming. However, among the non-specialist staff, knowledge on gender mainstreaming needs is variable. Experience with gender-sensitive budgeting could be reinforced.
4. NCA and other national counterparts (e.g., NGOs such as DOWIE) have significant capacity in implementing gender projects at grassroots level. They can complement EnDev by bringing in significant experience in geographic areas not covered up to now.
5. There is a lack of gender-disaggregated data on current project impacts.

## b) Successes and opportunities

6. Women's increased control over energy in the household and need to be involved to foster success of interventions in the sector.
7. Taking into account the needs of both males and females increases the rate of success and the long-term sustainability of projects in the energy sector.
8. The baseline projects have been successful in providing work opportunities for women, and in empowering women.
9. Women entrepreneurs were found to be more efficient in selling cookstoves than their male peers which is due to a higher level of training and support (Global Alliance for Clean Cook Stoves, quoted in EnDev Kenya 2017).
10. Women's engagement in distribution and marketing can provide other women with comfortable spaces within which to discuss their particular concerns (Gill et al., 2012, quoted in EnDev Kenya 2017).
11. Women have ideas regarding design of cookstoves and are eager for technological innovations in the sector.
12. Women show a high capacity at collaborating and are successful in organizing women groups such as meetings/rounds or "hutunies".
13. Cookstove production involves heavy work (such as pounding and kneading of clay) which can be mechanized.
14. There are opportunities for work in the cookstove sector for people with disabilities.
15. Women are satisfied with the contents and convenience of the trainings provided by EnDev. They appreciate that these trainings can be performed near home.

36


---
**Page 193**
---

18\. The available studies to date do not indicate that empowering women could generate conflicts in the household, most husbands on the contrary seem satisfied.

19\. There is evidence from studies that working conditions are worse for women than for men in the stove production sectors. The majority of women involved in stove production and distribution are mainly in the informal sector.


### Potential gender-related barriers to successful project implementation

20\. Women spend 50% or more of household work. Domestic activities either prevent women from spending as much time as men on working out of the house, or force them to have a double workload.

21\. Women who cannot perform their household tasks because they are engaged into stove production have these tasks performed by other women (mostly female relatives).

22\. Women who are not involved in stove production and distribution do not have access to capital.

23\. Despite the positive results from the available Endev studies and consultations, the country context analyses show that there is a risk that in some households, women will have a very poor decision-making power. This would be more pronounced in Senegal, in rural areas, and in “frontier” counties of Kenya.


### Gender equality in the household varies with class, ethnicity/rural/urban areas, and per household size.

25\. Women feel less confident than men to address administrators or other formal organizations, and are less capable of writing and keeping records. This hampers their access to finance, to available assistance programs, to training, and to information on how to write and keep records.


### References

[1] (Endev Kenya 2013) argues that the strategic gender needs of women are not met with this solution. In Senegal, by generating income and by assuming household chores, women assume the role of the woman designated and expected by society and themselves. From their point of view, net only productive but also strategic gender equality is achieved. However, the study conducted in Kenya shows that strategic gender empowerment would not be the case. In addition, the absence of the potter in the daily life of the household and the lack of involvement of women in the production process are factors which could have a negative impact regarding to households chores. While the production of ceramic inserts allows the interviewees to assume another role in their community, it does not allow them to assume the role of the woman designated by society. The household member assumes the role of the housewife and the inherent household chores which are defined as women’s work. This implies that strategic gender equality is not achieved.

[2] (Endev Kenya 2013) argues that allowing gender equality as a sole definition implies a society in which women and men enjoy the same opportunities, outcomes, rights and obligations in accordance with the UNFPA [4].

It is important to note that although the consultations and the gender studies performed for the project completely reflect the gender equality described in the overview sections of the present document.

26. Women literacy rates have been mentioned in Senegal.

27. Women tend to keep traditional roles in production (potters), especially in Senegal.

28. There are indications that men take on the most profitable positions in the production sector, for instance embracing pottery – a traditionally women role – when this appears profitable, while women do not take on metalwork (only one case observed), which is at the end of the supply chain and has often been considered as a women’s work.

29. There is a reluctance for husbands (and societal) to let women travel and go out at night, and women fear going out. However, if this is an issue for distribution of solar panels in EWB projects, it is not an issue for cooking stoves and distribution, which can generally be performed near home.

30. Some men are reluctant to be involved.

31. Women are politically under-represented especially in Kenya, and do not have the same participation and decision-making powers as men. Their access to assets and lands is low, and their participation in the economy is lower than that of men especially in Senegal.

32. Access for women to credit is difficult because women are more likely to be accepted from MFIs as clients because they are considered more reliable).

33. Gender-based violence is widespread in the countries and women do not always feel safe.


# Development of the Gender Action Plan

## Approach and methodology

The following approach has been followed for developing the Gender Action Plan:

- understand GCF and GIZ requirements for a Gender Action Plan
- based on the analysis of the project’s context and environment, identify needs for gender-responsive activities in the project
- discuss the potential activities with the coaching team and with local partners
- select the most appropriate activities to be included in the project
- define gender-sensitive targets and gender-sensitive monitoring and evaluation criteria
- integrate the selected activities into the project logic tree.

## Rationale for the Gender Action Plan

### GCF perspective

The GCF’s commitment to gender equality centers on gender responsive country programmes and initiatives that benefit everyone, women and men. This requires gender analysis to understand the social, economic and political factors underlying climate change-exacerbated gender inequalities, and the potential contributions of women and men in addressing these challenges. The Gender Action Plan (GAP) for EWB projects entails adopting methods and tools to promote gender equality and reduce gender disparities in climate funding by mainstreaming gender in the project cycle. Finally, it means measuring the outcomes and impacts of projects through a gender lens using gender-sensitive monitoring and evaluation (M&E). Gender mainstreaming should shape the entire project cycle, from project identification to M&E.


38


---
**Page 195**
---

### GIZ approach

According to GIZ, the potential for promoting gender equality and women’s rights must be leveraged wherever possible. This involves, among other things:

- Creating equal access to law and justice;
- Enhancing political participation, decision-making and representation of women;
- Promoting economic empowerment and participation;
- Enabling equal access to education, to resources and services, to vocational training, to the labour market and to life-long learning;
- Tackling violence against women and girls; and
- Supporting state institutions and/or non-state stakeholders that actively campaign for gender equality.

### Endev Gender Action Plan

Endev has developed an approach which is relevant to the energy sector, and distinguishes between three types of gender impacts:

1. **Energy beneficiaries** – Description and appraisal of gender-specific reduced health and safety risks, co-benefits from the measure (job creation) etc.) and inclusion of female-headed households.
2. **Energy access accelerators** – Harness specific roles of men and women as agents of change
3. **Impact of interventions changing gender roles** (if applicable and feasible)

Endev’s approach takes into account these three types of impacts in its implementation.

### Needs for gender-responsive activities in the project

The gender assessment has tried to summarise the GCF and GEZ commitments into a set of objectives. Discussions have been held with the country teams about how these objectives should translate into gender-sensitive activities to be included for the project. The results are summarized in Table 10.

#### Objective 1

First, the project should make sure it presents any negative impact of the interventions on gender equality and any negative impact on any group of people. The only potential negative gender-related impact of the project is a very exceptional risk for conflicts in the household: In general, prevention of unintended negative gender impacts can be achieved through regular monitoring and on the implementation of a project grievance mechanism. It is also recommended:

- to sufficiently tackle the experience of those project partners who have experience in supporting women’s empowerment initiatives;
- if necessary, to carry out household survey of gender issues before intervening in areas where project experience is not sufficient enough, in order to improve the understanding of cultural issues in these areas, especially when expanding into the border areas of Kenya.

#### Objective 2

Second, the project has to benefit men and women equally. Here the use of cookstoves in the household tends to benefit women more than men, but thereby only reducing existing inequalities. The further implication of this principle is that the project should seek to make sure that work opportunities as well as income generation opportunities are equally allocated to men and women. To this end, a series of


---
**Page 196**
---

# Gender Assessment — 22 January 2019

## Objective 3

Third, differentiated needs of men and women need to be taken into account to ensure the efficiency and sustainability of the interventions. To this end, the project will engage in continuous gender-specific consultations of project beneficiaries and translate the results of these consultations in project design.

## Objective 4

Fourth, the project should harness specific roles of men and women as agents of change: whereby women can exert a specific role in becoming “energy access accelerators.” The baseline projects already have a strong tradition of working with women groups; this has proven successful in the energy sector and will be continued.

## Objective 5

Fifth, the project will include activities for capacity building in gender mainstreaming targeted at GIZ, UNDP and the local implementing entities. Gender implementation capacity seems good in the network of implementing entities, but coordination and monitoring will be required since the number of partners and the geographical extension is large. Therefore a gender focal person will be responsible for coordinating activities, as well as for regular reporting on progress to the project management team. A gender mainstreaming plan for each entity’s mainstreaming will be developed each year for the entire organization. This program may include knowledge transfer from the more experienced project partners to less experienced.

## Objective 6

Sixth, the project has looked at possibilities to support improvement of the national institutional frameworks for gender mainstreaming. The needs assessment identified that institutions generally have gender policies and frameworks, but that concrete experience in gender mainstreaming and gender budgeting was sometimes lacking. The fact that the country is a member state of the CEDAW, which covers large geographic coverage, provides an excellent opportunity for collection of gender-sensitive data and dissemination of lessons learnt. Therefore knowledge sharing activities have been included in the Gender Action Plan, national as well as regional/country level.

## Other Objectives

The Gender Action Plan, in line with both GIZ and UNDP’s policies, should harness the opportunities given by the project to improve gender equality. This can also be understood as opportunities for better satisfying women's strategic needs. For this project, we mainly consider that the empowerment of women


---
**Page 197**
---

GCF funding proposal "Promotion of Climate-Friendly Cooking in Kenya and Senegal"  
Gender Assessment – 22 January 2019

Two more opportunities were identified for improving gender equality through the project and were discussed during this meeting:

- A discussion was held as to whether the project should tackle the general division of household labour and childcare. This subject is often controversial. There is no universally accepted opinion as to whether the issue at stake in this issue is (i) if women have equal share than the tasks, (ii) to provide public childcare or to encourage men to take part in childcare and other domestic chores, and (iii) to provide paid work. There is even no consensus on the gender literature as to whether women and men should be given equal share of household chores or whether they should be given different shares.

- Another opportunity was identified for improving gender equality by including a module explaining how much time is spent by women on cooking included in the project.

Finally, we also examined how if the project should improve gender equality situations in the countries by working along GIZ’s principles:

1. Creating an enabling legal and law environment
2. Enhancing political representation, decision-making and representation of women;
3. Promoting equal social and economic participation;
4. Ensuring access to education, resources and services; to vocational training, to the labour market and to life-long learning.
5. Tackling gender-based violence against women and girls;
6. Supporting state institutions and/or non-state stakeholders that actively campaign for gender equality.

It was considered that the project, by training and empowering women, will contribute to improving gender equality along principles 1, 2, 3 and 4 above. Regarding principle 5, it is not considered a core goal of the project as it is more related to the social and economic empowerment of women. However, considering the importance of this principle in the context of the project, a grievance mechanism can be used to help identifying and solving possible arising issues. Regarding principle 6, the project will support several gender organizations as partners, and will contribute to improving gender equality by supporting them (see also the regional action plan 6 of the action plan). Thereby, The Project and the Gender Action Plan are considered able to successfully contribute to GIZ’s gender mainstreaming principles.

41


---
**Page 198**
---

### Needs of other groups in society

Youth and women are mainly targeted in the project because (i) they are considered important agents of change, (ii) the project offers significant opportunities to improve two priority development issues in the targeted countries, which are youth unemployment and lack of women's participation in the economy - both GE and the GCF are committed to promoting equality between men and women.

The elderly people are considered as a Special Interest Group (SIG) within the gender analysis. We see some evidence that the elderly are more likely to belong to the poorest of the poor, on the one hand, but also more likely to be employed than youth. For Kenya for example, we draw on the 2016 report from the National Gender and Equality Commission (NGEC) which states that older persons in Kenya are considered by the NGEC as a "Special Interest Group" (SIG) as much as disabled persons, youth, marginalized groups, etc.

Older persons are disadvantaged in this group (as in other groups) as they are less often in formal employment. The main issue for them is to ensure that they have access to ICS and can use it effectively.


Older persons can be expected to benefit from the positive health impact and ease-of-use of ICS even more than other segments of population, as they may also spend more time in the kitchen and gathering fire. Older people, who are generally more often in other age segments, will have access to ICS for cooking fuel use - if they are economically active. They will see the same opportunity to participate in the ICS supply chain as other groups.

ICS production is already open to a wide range of persons even with disabilities, and the project will help optimize the way it adapts them to people with special needs.


Targets for vulnerable persons (with HIV, with disabilities, or women-headed households) have further been included in the Gender Action Plan.

To ensure that all groups benefit from the incomes, overcome the following barriers: | Specific targets for training women and men equally (through on-the-job training) |

# 6.6 Gender-sensitive targets

The project Gender Action Plan searches to increase women participation in the economy, and the project has therefore considered setting a target for the number of women participating in the cookstove production sector.

The gender balance in the ICS sector is currently good. In Kenya, gender-disaggregated data were available on LMEs, whereby 42% were women and 57% were men (EdDev Kenya, 2017), and on individual builders of rocket stoves, where 63% were women and 37% were men (EdDev Kenya, 2017). Table 5.1, on p. 26 shows that women are still most numerous in the sector, because they are mostly the sole end producers of ICS (metaworks). However, there are more women builders of banco stoves, and gender balance is lower than for rocket stoves.

The project will monitor employment of men and women in the sector, with the aim at obtaining a slightly higher participation for women. However, in the market approach, the project cannot directly control the number of women employed or the proportion of women in the sector. The project can only provide support establishment of LMEs in their activity, but only trains them. Once trained, it is their own decision to engage in market activities. Regarding access to finance, women with the same level of education are expected to have more difficulty than men, because financial institutions are thought to trust women more easily (source: country teams).

The project has therefore decided not to set gender targets for employment creation, but to provide more training to women in order to contribute to bringing the target group into participation.

# Gender Action Plan, budget and M&E

The final Gender Action Plan (GAP) is presented below, integrating the selected activities in the project activities, and integrating gender-sensitive targets when needed. The activities are budgeted as part of the project activities. The gender-sensitive targets are integrated into the overall project logframe. The summary of the Gender Action Plan is presented below. The final Gender Action Plan and the project logframe are presented in separate documents.

## Summary of Activities

### General Project Impact: The project increases gender equality by reducing household work for women and making them participate in the co-kinetic value chains

As women are the ones who collect firewood and are responsible for the fuel collection or purchase, they are the ones benefiting most from reduced fuel usage and local emissions.

1. **Potential unintended negative gender-related project impacts are prevented**
   - Enhance project partners' knowledge of gender issues in order to ensure culturally appropriate activities in all communities.
2. **Women and men benefit equally from the project**
   - Ensure project co-benefits of employment and income generation for women and men equally, by overcoming barriers to economic participation.
3. **Differentiated needs of men and women are taken into account in project activities**
   - Differentiated consultation of men and women, equal representation of both genders in all meetings in project activities, and gender-differentiated approaches to awareness raising and design of promotional materials.
4. **Specific roles of men and women are recognized as agents of change**
   - Work with women groups in awareness raising, design and promotion of the project.
5. **Gender mainstreaming capacity in the project team is increased**
   - Increasing capacity of project team to carry out gender mainstreaming activities, among others by sharing existing knowledge between partners, and training staff on gender-sensitive budgeting.
6. **Institutional frameworks for gender mainstreaming are strengthened**
   - Knowledge sharing between the project and national institutions, including regional administrations, ministries and NGOs.

## Implementation arrangements

Implementation arrangements for the GAP have been included as follows in the project organization:

1. At national level, GCF/ENDev will have persons who will be "local points" for gender mainstreaming.


---
**Page 202**
---

2. The project will also have regional focal points for gender mainstreaming. In Senegal, these staff can be with EiDiv or with the EE's in Kenya, with the EiDiv cluster management or with SNV.
3. All EE's and implementing partners will have designated contact persons for gender issues.
4. A project grievance mechanism is established:
   - possible complaints are regularly collected by the EE's during their consultations of beneficiaries, producers, workers in the supply chain, and project staff;
   - business units or factories that receive goods from EiDiv suppliers will be required to have their own local mechanism, for instance a contact phone number advertised at the factory entrance.
   - complaints reporting will be accepted and treated by the EiDiv country teams.

General E&S management will comprise the following tasks and responsibilities:
1. GIZ's Head Office oversees compliance with GIZ Safeguards and Gender management system
2. The Project Advisory Board is responsible for overall guidance on the project, including on aspects related to gender equality and social inclusion in line with the project's alignment with E&S safeguards.
3. The Project Coordination Committee is responsible for monitoring implementation of the ESMP and the Gender Action Plan.
4. The GIZ Country Office coordinates the Gender Action Plan across organizations
5. A training program in gender mainstreaming is implemented for the entire project organization, harnessing among others the capacity of EE's and implementing partners (suggestion: in year 1 and in year 3).
